A T ​ b Tb TYPE THEOREM FOR SUPPRESSED KERNELS
MARINA FERNÀNDEZ-VILASECA

Abstract
In this article, a non-homogeneous T ​ b Tb type theorem for arbitrary dimensional Calderón-Zygmund singular integral operators is proved. This is an extension of an analogous non-homogeneous T ​ b Tb theorem for the Cauchy transform, in the planar setting, due to Nazarov, Treil and Volberg. The novelties of the present work are the change of dimension and the fact that the operators to which the theorem applies are not necessarily antisymmetric. The techniques used in the proof include, among others, suppressed kernels, decompositions in L 2 ​ ( μ ) L^{2}(\mu) , where μ \mu is a Radon measure in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} , and a probabilistic argument resulting from taking averages of the operators involved.



Introduction

A classical problem that has led to the development of many techniques in Analysis is Painlevé’s problem on the metric or geometric characterization of removable sets for bounded analytic functions. More precisely, given a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{C}
, we say that it is removable for bounded analytic functions, or simply, removable, if for every open set
\Omega\supset E
, every bounded and analytic function on
\Omega\setminus E
admits an analytic extension to the whole of
\Omega
. For example, if
E
is a finite collection of points, it is removable, but if we take
E
to be a disk, it is not.

A key tool in the description of removable sets is that of analytic capacity, which was introduced by Ahlfors
[
1
]
and is defined as follows. For a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{C}
, we let its analytic capacity be the number

\gamma(E):=\sup|f^{\prime}(\infty)|=\sup\bigg|\lim_{|z|\to+\infty}z\left(f(z)-f(\infty)\right)\bigg|,

where the supremum is taken over all
f\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}\setminus E)
with
|f|\leq 1
in
\mathbb{C}\setminus E
. The connection between analytic capacity and removability is described by a result, due to Ahlfors, which states that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{C}
is removable if and only if
\gamma(E)=0
. Even though this is a positive result in the characterization of removable sets, since it is a purely analytic characterization, rather than a metric or geometric one, it fails to be the sought answer to Painlevé’s problem.

Another capacity, which turns out to have a better geometric interpretation, is the analytic capacity
\gamma_{+}
(or capacity
\gamma_{+}
), which is defined, for
E\subset\mathbb{C}
compact,

\gamma_{+}(E)=\sup\mu(E),

where the supremum is taken over all positive Radon measures
\mu
, supported on
E
, and such that their Cauchy transform,

\mathcal{C}\mu(x)=\int\frac{1}{y-x}\,d\mu(y),

is in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
, with modulus bounded above by
1
. One advantage of this new capacity is that it has a precise description, due to Tolsa
[
13
]
, using previous ideas from Melnikov and Verdera
[
5
]
, in terms of curvature of measures, which is, indeed, a geometric notion, which was first introduced by Melnikov
[
6
]
.

From this, we see that the missing piece to solve Painlevé’s problem is how
\gamma
and
\gamma_{+}
are related. This was dealt with by Tolsa in
[
14
]
, in which he proved that there exists an absolute constant
A
such that for any compact set
E\subset\mathbb{C}
,

\gamma_{+}(E)\leq\gamma(E)\leq A\gamma_{+}(E),

which we write
\gamma_{+}\approx\gamma
, and we say that the capacities
\gamma
and
\gamma_{+}
are comparable. A key consequence of this result is that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{C}
is non-removable if and only if it supports a positive Radon measure that satisfies a certain growth condition and has finite curvature (more precisely, see
[
14
, Theorem 1.2]
). This description closes Painlevé’s problem.

A key ingredient in Tolsa’s proof of the comparability between the capacities
\gamma
and
\gamma_{+}
is a
Tb
type theorem, which is originally due to Nazarov, Treil and Volberg
[
11
]
. See Tolsa
[
15
, Section 5]
for a careful explanation of this result in the case of the Cauchy transform. The aim of the present work is to extend this theorem to a broader class of operators. Specifically, in this work it is shown that the reasoning can be adapted for higher dimension and that one can drop the assumption that the kernel that we deal with is antisymmetric. We will comment on the organization of the proof after a brief exposition on the topic of
Tb
theorems.

Broadly speaking, the notion of “a
Tb
type theorem” refers to a criterion for the boundedness of a special type of integral operators, which is obtained via their action on a suitable bounded test function, which is usually denoted by
b
. The operators that appear in this context are called singular integral operators (SIOs). More precisely, we consider a function
k
, which we call kernel, defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
except, perhaps, on the diagonal
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x=y\}
, and we let

T\mu(x)=\int k(x,y)\,d\mu(y),

where
\mu
is a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and for
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
, we set
T_{\mu}f(x)=T(f\mu)(x)
. Of course, the convergence of these integrals deserves some thought, which will be done in the first section of this work, in which we will also introduce Calderón-Zygmund kernels, a particular case of which is the kernel
\frac{1}{x-y}
that appears in the definition of the Cauchy transform above. These are the kernels that determine the operators appearing in
Tb
type theorems.

The family of theorems which we refer to as
Tb
type was developed as a tool to study the
L^{2}(\mu)
boundedness of singular integral operators. For instance, the ones in David, Journé and Semmes
[
4
]
and in Christ
[
2
]
. These theorems fall into the category known as classical Calderón-Zygmund theory. The adjective is due to the fact that most of these theorems assume that the underlying measure
\mu
is doubling, which means that there is some constant
c>0
such that for all
x\in\text{supp}(\mu)
and
r>0
,

\mu(B(x,2r))\leq c\,\mu(B(x,r)).

Originally, it was believed that reasonable results for Calderón-Zygmund singular integral operators could only be obtained in spaces for which the underlying measure satisfies the condition above. These kinds of spaces are called homogeneous. However, when working with analytic capacity, it is not uncommon to find a situation where the doubling assumption above fails. For instance, if the measure
\mu
is the Hausdorff
1
-dimensional measure restricted to a compact set. Nevertheless, in this type of context, an alternative and natural assumption is that
\mu
has polynomial growth of degree
n\leq d
, meaning that for
r>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}\,r^{n}.

(1)

For example, in Tolsa
[
13
]
, a non-doubling version for the
T1
theorem, that is, for the simpler case that
b=1
, for the Cauchy transform is proved.

The
Tb
theorem in the present work falls into the non-homogeneous setting. It will concern a finite Radon measure
\mu
without any doubling assumption. Moreover, the polynomial growth condition (
1
) will not necessarily be true for all the balls
B(x,r)
. Instead, we will allow a controlled family of balls to have bigger measure. In this sense, we will not obtain that our singular integral operator
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, but rather in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
, where the
\mu
-measure of
G
is not too small compared to that of
\text{supp}(\mu)
, and
\mu\lfloor G
will have the desired polynomial growth. Moreover, since the antisymmetric assumption is removed, the theorem deals with two bounded functions
b_{1},b_{2}
, satisfying that

\int T_{*}(b_{1}\mu)\,d\mu\leq c_{*}\,\|\mu\|,\quad\int T_{*}^{*}(b_{2}\mu)\,d\mu\leq c_{*}\,\|\mu\|,

where the integrals are taken in the complementary of a distinguished set. The complete details about the hypothesis and conclusions can be found in the precise statement, which is Theorem
2.1
from Section
2
.

Let us remark that the proof of the
Tb
theorem is going to be highly technical, as is the case in
[
11
]
and even in the simpler case
[
15
]
. This is the reason why we have decided to split the proof into several sections, which we outline now.

As we mentioned above, the first section is an introduction to the theory of Calderón-Zygmund kernels and singular integral operators. We give the basic definitions of the concepts and statements of results that will be key in the rest of the thesis. Some are very well-known and are the same in both the classical and the non-homogeneous setting, such as the dyadic Hardy-Littlewood differentiation theorem, but not all are valid in both cases. For instance, we will need a Calderón-Zygmund decomposition tailored to the non-homogeneous case. The proof of most of the results from this preliminary section can be found in
[
15
, Section 2]
.

The second section contains the complete statement of the
Tb
theorem that we prove, which is Theorem
2.1
. After stating it, the following sections are devoted to establishing the necessary tools for the rather long proof. We define what we call an exceptional set, which, morally, controls the portion of
\text{supp}(\mu)
where the maximal operator associated to
T
is big. Afterwards, we consider, for a Lipschitz function
\Theta\,\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
, an appropriate modification of the Calderón-Zygmund kernel that defines
T
, which we call
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
, the “suppressed” version of
k
, with associated operator
K_{\Theta}
. This notion was introduced by Nazarov, Treil and Volberg
[
9
]
and is helpful for overcoming the difficulties associated with the non-homogeneous setting.

Still in the second section, we introduce the essential concepts related to dyadic lattices. Instead of fixing one lattice, we will work with its translates, and for each of these we are going to classify its cubes according to their position with respect to some of the sets that are singled out in the statement of our
Tb
theorem. Later, we will see that this classification, along with a family of operators indexed by the cubes from the lattice, yields a decomposition in
L^{2}(\mu)
, which is Lemma
2.21
, and will be central in the rest of the proof.

In the last section of the second section, we introduce the notion of good and bad cubes, from one dyadic lattice with respect to another. The third section is devoted to proving a lemma concerning the action of
K_{\Theta}
on good functions, which are essentially the ones in which the
L^{2}(\mu)
decomposition only sees the good cubes. The proof of this lemma is long and technical, and consists of using different strategies depending the type of cubes appearing in the
L^{2}(\mu)
decomposition.

The fourth section is quite brief and deals with a Cotlar type theorem for a certain type of Calderón-Zygmund operators. Its proof will be independent of the classifications of dyadic cubes mentioned above and it will use the non-doubling Calderón-Zygmund decomposition stated in the first section.

The last section is devoted to concluding the proof of the
Tb
theorem through a probabilistic argument. It consists of showing that the probability that of being in a bad situation, in the sense of bad cubes and functions, can be made arbitrarily close to zero. Moreover, here we define the set
G
on which our original operator
T
is going to be bounded in
L^{2}
.

One of the motivations for introducing the non-homogeneous
Tb
theorem was the proof of the comparability between the capacities
\gamma
and
\gamma_{+}
. Of course, this was a result for the planar case, so it did not need the generalization that we prove in this work. Thus, it is reasonable to ask whether the additional work that we have done to prove the theorem in arbitrary dimension has a similar justification. The answer is affirmative and it concerns a reformulation of the problem of removability.

This reformulation refers to changing the type of functions that we study, switching from analytic functions to Lipschitz harmonic functions. So far, we have considered functions defined on
\Omega\setminus E
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
is open and
E
is compact. Then, we look for conditions on the set
E
that ensure that for
f
bounded and analytic in
\Omega\setminus E
, we actually have that
\overline{\partial}f(z)=0
for all
z\in\Omega
. We can write this problem in terms of only real functions, as follows. Instead of holomorphic functions, we consider harmonic functions:
u\,\colon\Omega\setminus E\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Delta u=0
. Equivalently, such that
\overline{\partial}\partial\,u=0
. We can call
f=\partial u
, and we see that
u
is harmonic if and only if
\partial u
is analytic. Using this, one sees that if
\gamma(E)=0
, then
E
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions. The converse implication is also true, but it requires using the fact that
\gamma\approx\gamma_{+}
.

In this context, it is natural to look for a Lipschitz harmonic capacity of the set
E
, a quantity that, similarly to analytic capacity, will vanish if and only if
E
is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions. By our previous comment, for
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, this capacity vanishes if and only if
\gamma(E)=0
. Now, note that this new capacity can be defined in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for
d\geq 2
, that is, it extends the notion of analytic capacity to a higher dimensional setting. Since the planar
Tb
theorem was useful for studying analytic capacity, it seems reasonable to think that a
d
-dimensional analogue of this theorem would be a useful tool to study Lipschitz harmonic capacity. This is indeed the case, for more details, see Section
2
of Volberg’s book
[
16
]
or
[
8
]
.

As a final remark, after completing the present work, I was informed by my advisor that Andrea Merlo, Mihalis Mourgoglou, Carmelo Puliatti
[
7
]
notified him that, simultaneously and independently of my work, they have proved a very general local
Tb
theorem, from which one could derive the
Tb
theorem explained in this thesis.

Acknowledgement.
I would like to thank Dr. Xavier Tolsa for supervising my Master’s thesis, for suggesting the topic, and for his guidance and support throughout the development of this work.

1
Preliminaries

In this section we introduce the basic notions related to the theory of singular integral operators in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We will discuss Calderón-Zygmund kernels, their adjoints and their maximal versions. We will also introduce some basic aspects of non-doubling Calderón-Zygmund theory and dyadic lattices, which are a key element in the main theorem of this thesis.

1.1
Calderón-Zygmund kernels and singular integral operators

Definition 1.1
.

We say that
k(\cdot,\cdot)\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x=y\}\to\mathbb{C}
is an
n
-dimensional
Calderón-Zygmund kernel
if there exist constants
c>0
and
0<\eta\leq 1
, such that the following inequalities hold for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},x\neq y
,

\displaystyle|k(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}},\quad\text{and,}

(2)

\displaystyle\text{if}\,|x-x^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|x-y|}{2},\,\,|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|+|k(y,x)-k(y,x^{\prime})|\leq\frac{c|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}.

Note that, without the condition that
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
, the second inequality in the second line above also holds if
|x-y|\approx|x^{\prime}-y|
, possibly with a different constant
c
.

A Calderón-Zygmund kernel allows us to define a linear operator on the space of Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the following way.

Definition 1.2
.

Given an
n
-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel
k
, for
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define

T\nu(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}k(x,y)\,d\nu(y),\quad\text{for }\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\text{supp}(\nu).

(3)

We also define
T\nu(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for which the integral above is well-defined. We say that
T
is an
n
-dimensional
singular integral operator
(SIO) with kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Note that the integral in (
3
) may fail to be absolutely convergent if
x\in\text{supp}(\nu)
. This is why we will also consider the following
\varepsilon
-truncated operators,
T_{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon>0
,

T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x):=\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}k(x,y)\,d\nu(y),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Now, the integral is absolutely convergent if, for example,
|\nu|(\mathbb{R}^{d})<\infty
. Moreover, we define the maximal operator as

T_{*}\nu(x):=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x)|.

Also, the
\delta
-truncated maximal operator is

T_{*,\delta}\nu(x)=\sup_{\varepsilon>\delta}|T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x)|,

and we set, for
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
,
T_{\mu,*}f=T_{*}(f\mu)
, and
T_{\mu,*,\delta}f=T_{*,\delta}(f\mu)
.

Given a fixed positive Radon measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can define, for
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
,

\displaystyle T_{\mu}f(x)\,

\displaystyle:=T(f\mu)(x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\text{supp}(f\mu),

\displaystyle T_{\mu,\varepsilon}f(x)\,

\displaystyle:=T_{\varepsilon}(f\mu)(x),\qquad

\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

The last integral is absolutely convergent for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
if, for instance,
f\in L^{p}(\mu)
and
\mu
has growth of degree
n
, meaning that there is
c>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r>0
,
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c\,r^{n}
.

We say that the operator
T_{\mu}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\mu)
if the operators
T_{\mu,\varepsilon}
are bounded in
L^{p}(\mu)
uniformly on
\varepsilon>0
and analogously with respect to the boundedness from
L^{1}(\mu)
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
. Moreover, we say that
T
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
if there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and all
\lambda>0
,

\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x)|>\lambda\})\leq C\frac{\|\nu\|}{\lambda},

uniformly on
\varepsilon>0
. Lastly, we say that a singular integral operator is a
Calderón-Zygmund operator
(CZO) if it is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Another notion that will be useful for our theorem is the following. Given a SIO
T
, originated from a kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
, we denote by
T^{*}
the SIO that arises from the kernel
\widetilde{k}(x,y)=k(y,x)
. That is, for
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we put

T^{*}\nu(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}k(y,x)\,d\nu(y),\quad\text{for }\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\text{supp}(\nu).

We will call
T^{*}
the
adjoint
of
T
. The reason for this notation is the following. Recall that if
H
is a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
S\colon H\to H
is a continuous linear operator, the adjoint of
S
, which we denote by
S^{*}
, is the continuous linear operator
S^{*}\colon H\to H
determined by the relation

\langle Sf,g\rangle=\langle f,S^{*}g\rangle,\quad\text{for all }\,f,g\in H.

It is straightforward to check, using Fubini’s theorem, that for
f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\langle T_{\mu,\varepsilon}f,g\rangle=\int T_{\mu,\varepsilon}f(x)g(x)\,d\mu(x)=\int f(x)T^{*}_{\mu,\varepsilon}g(x)\,d\mu(x)=\langle f,T^{*}_{\mu,\varepsilon}g\rangle,

which is precisely the relation that characterizes the adjoint operator.

In the special case that we have
k(y,x)=-k(x,y)
, we say that the kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
is
antisymmetric
.

Lastly, we give two examples of singular integral operators.

Example 1.3
.

The Cauchy transform is the SIO in
\mathbb{C}
obtained from the
1
-dimensional antisymmetric Calderón-Zygmund kernel

k(x,y):=\frac{1}{y-x},\quad x,y\in\mathbb{C}.

If instead we consider

k(x,y)=\frac{1}{|x-y|},

we obtain a
1
-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel which is not antisymmetric.

Example 1.4
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for an integer
0<n\leq d
, we consider the Riesz kernels,

k(x,y)=\frac{x_{j}-y_{j}}{|x-y|^{n+1}},\quad j=1,\dots,d,

where we write
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
and analogously for
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The
n
-dimensional Riesz kernels are the SIOs originated by these kernels. Note that these kernels are also antisymmetric.

1.2
The Calderón-Zygmund decomposition

As mentioned in the Introduction, the
Tb
theorem in the present work belongs to the ambit of non-homogeneous Calderón-Zygmund theory, that is, we work with measures
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the doubling condition

\mu(B(x,2r))\leq c\,\mu(B(x,r)),\quad\text{for any }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,r>0,

with a constant
c
, independent of the balls, is not necessarily true for all balls
B(x,r)
or cubes. This is why we introduce, the following notion. For
\alpha,\beta>1
, we say that a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-doubling
if

\mu(\alpha Q)\leq\beta\mu(Q),

where
\alpha Q
denotes the cube concentric with
Q
with diameter
\alpha\,\text{diam}(Q)
. We claim that, although
\mu
may not satisfy any growth condition like the one above, there are a lot of small doubling cubes. For a proof, see
[
15
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
\beta>\alpha^{d}
. If
\mu
is a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exists a sequence of
(\alpha,\beta)
-doubling cubes
\{Q_{k}\}
centered at the point
x
with
\ell(Q_{k})\to 0
as
k\to+\infty
.

A key tool in many results concerning SIOs is the following lemma, called the
Calderón-Zygmund decomposition
. Morally, it says that if we consider a Radon measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then for any finite measure
\nu
with compact support, we can split it into a “good” part, where it is controlled by the ambient measure
\mu
and a “bad” part, in which
|\nu|
is bigger than
\mu
. Moreover, the latter is a union of cubes with finite overlap. For a proof, see
[
15
, Lemma 2.14]
.

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For every

\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with compact support and every

\lambda>2^{d+1}\frac{\|\nu\|}{\|\mu\|}
,
we have:

(a)

There exists a family of almost disjoint cubes, that is, having bounded overlap,
\{Q_{i}\}_{i}
,
and a function
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
such that

|\nu|(Q_{i})>\frac{\lambda}{2^{d+1}}\,\mu(2Q_{i}),

(4)

|\nu|(\eta Q_{i})\leq\frac{\lambda}{2^{d+1}}\,\mu(2\eta Q_{i}),\qquad\text{for }\eta>2,

(5)

\nu=f\,\mu\qquad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}Q_{i},\qquad\text{with }|f|\leq\lambda\quad\mu\text{-a.e.}

(6)

(b)

For each
i
, let
R_{i}
be a
(6,6^{d+1})
-doubling cube
concentric with
Q_{i}
, with
\ell(R_{i})>4\,\ell(Q_{i})
,
and let
w_{i}=\frac{\chi_{Q_{i}}}{\sum_{k}\chi_{Q_{k}}}
. Then, there exists a family of functions
\varphi_{i}
with

\operatorname{supp}(\varphi_{i})\subset R_{i}
, each
\varphi_{i}

with constant sign, satisfying

\int\varphi_{i}\,d\mu=\int_{Q_{i}}w_{i}\,d\nu,

(7)

\sum_{i}|\varphi_{i}|\leq B\,\lambda,

(8)

(where
B
is some fixed constant depending only on
d
and
n
),
and

\|\varphi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\,\mu(R_{i})\leq c\,|\nu|(Q_{i}).

(9)

Lastly, we need a standard integral estimate concerning doubling cubes, which is easily proved by splitting the domain of integration adequately.

Lemma 1.7
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
Q\subset R
are concentric cubes such that there are no
(\alpha,\beta)
-doubling cubes (with
\beta>\alpha^{d}
) of the form
\alpha^{k}Q
,
k\geq 0
, with
Q\subset\alpha^{k}Q\subset R
, and
x_{Q}
denotes the center of
Q
, then

\int_{R\setminus Q}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q}|^{n}}d\mu(x)\leq c_{1}\frac{\mu(R)}{\ell(R)^{n}},

where
c_{1}
depends only on
\alpha,\beta,n
, and
d
.

1.3
The Hardy-Littlewood maximal operator

Let us introduce briefly an operator which is very well known in Harmonic Analysis: for a Radon measure
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the
centered maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator

M_{\mu}
applied to a complex Radon measure
\nu
is defined as

M_{\mu}\nu(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac{|\nu|(B(x,r))}{\mu(B(x,r))},

so for
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
, we have
M_{\mu}f=M_{\mu}(f\mu)
. A classical result, which can be proved using the covering theorem for bounded subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, due to Besicovitch, is the following.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The centered maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator
M_{\mu}
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
and in
L^{p}(\mu)
, for
1<p\leq\infty
.

For our purposes, this theorem will be useful because it will enable us to relate boundedness of two integral operators whenever their difference is controlled by the maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator.

Another result concerning this maximal operator, which we will use in Section
4
, is Cotlar’s inequality, which we state next.

Theorem 1.9
.

Let
\mu
be a positive Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
T
be an
n
-dimensional SIO. Let
0<s\leq 1
, and
0<\delta\leq\varepsilon
. Suppose that for some fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}\qquad\text{for }r\geq\varepsilon,

(10)

and that
T_{\delta}
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to

L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
. Then we have

|T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x)|\leq c_{s}\,{M}_{\mu}\!\left(|T_{\delta}\nu|^{s}\right)(x)^{1/s}+c_{s,T}\,M_{\mu}\nu(x),\qquad\text{for }\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(11)

Thus, if
\mu
has growth of degree
n
, then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

T_{*,\delta}\nu(x)\leq c_{s}\,{M}_{\mu}\!\left(|T_{\delta}\nu|^{s}\right)(x)^{1/s}+c_{s,T}\,M_{\mu}\nu(x),\qquad\text{for }\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(12)

The constant
c_{s}
depends only on the constant
c_{0}
in
10
,

s
,
n
, and
d
, and

c_{s,T}=c\left(1+\|T_{\delta}\|_{M(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}\right),

with
c
depending only on
c_{0}
,
s
,
n
, and
d
.

Note that in the theorem above we impose no doubling conditions on the measure
\mu
. Hence, this theorem is valid for the non-homogeneous setting, which will be the situation in the
Tb
theorem that we will prove. This result is due to Nazarov, Treil and Volberg
[
10
]
, although the form in which we have stated it here is from Tolsa
[
12
]
.

Lastly, we introduce the one-dimensional radial maximal operator,

M_{r}\nu(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac{|\nu|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}.

Note that if
\mu
has linear growth, i.e. there is some constant
c_{0}>0
such that
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r
for any
r>0
, then
M_{R}\nu(x)\leq c_{0}M_{\mu}\nu(x)
, and so
M_{R}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\mu)
for
1<p\leq\infty
and from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
.

1.4
Dyadic lattices

In the proof of our main theorem
2.1
, dyadic lattices, which we introduce now, will play a key role. For
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{D}_{j}
is the collection of all cubes of the form

Q^{j}_{k}:=\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\frac{k_{i}}{2^{j}}\leq x_{i}<\frac{k_{i}+1}{2^{j}},1\leq i\leq d\right\},\quad k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

The
standard dyadic lattice
(from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) is the union of all the
\mathcal{D}_{j}
,

\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}_{j}.

Note that for
k,s\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, if
k\neq s
, then
Q^{j}_{k}\cap Q^{j}_{s}=\varnothing
. For
Q^{j}_{k}
, we say that
j
is the
generation
of
Q^{j}_{k}
and
\ell(Q^{j}_{k})=2^{-j}
is its
side length
. Moreover, for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can translate the standard dyadic lattice and we obtain another dyadic lattice,

\mathcal{D}(w):=w+\mathcal{D}_{0}.

(13)

For any dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the following useful properties:

1.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a unique
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{j}
that contains
x
.

2.

Given
Q,R\in\mathcal{D}
, either
Q\cap R=\varnothing
or one is contained inside the other.

3.

Each
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{j}
is a disjoint union of
2^{d}
cubes from
\mathcal{D}_{j+1}
,

Q=\bigcup_{i=1}^{2^{d}}P_{i}.

We say that
\mathcal{CH}(Q):=\{P_{i}\}_{i=1}^{2^{d}}
are the
children
of
Q
and that
Q
is the
parent
of each
P_{i}
, which we denote
\widehat{P}_{i}=Q
.

A terminology that is often used when working with dyadic lattices and cubes, related to the second property, is the following. Suppose that we have some family
I\subset\mathcal{D}
of not necessarily disjoint dyadic cubes. We say that a cube in this family is
maximal
if it is not contained in any other cube from the family. We denote by
I^{\text{max}}\subseteq I
the subfamily of the maximal cubes. The advantage of considering the maximal family is that the cubes that form it are pairwise disjoint.

Given a Radon measure
\sigma
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the associated
dyadic maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator
(with respect to
\sigma
and
\mathcal{D}
) by

M_{\sigma,d}\nu(x)=\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}:Q\ni x}\frac{|\nu|(Q)}{\sigma(Q)},

for any complex measure
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. For
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\sigma)
, we put
M_{\sigma,d}f(x)=M_{\sigma,d}(f\sigma)(x)
. We show the boundedness properties of this operator in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.10
.

The operator
M_{\sigma,d}
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\sigma)
and also in
L^{p}(\sigma)
, for
1<p\leq\infty
.

The boundedness of this maximal operator is the key ingredient in proving the dyadic version of the well-known Lebesgue differentiation theorem. The proof is the same as for centered balls in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
. Then, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{\mu(Q_{n}(x))}\int_{Q_{n}(x)}|f(y)-f(x)|\,d\mu(y)=0,

where for each
n\geq 1
,
Q_{n}(x)\in\mathcal{D}
is the only cube in the dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
with side length
2^{-n}
that contains
x
.

In particular, using the
L^{2}(\sigma)
-boundedness of
M_{\sigma,d}
, one can prove the following Dyadic Carleson embedding theorem, which we state below. For the proof, see
[
15
, Theorem 5.8]
. Before stating it, we introduce the following notation. For
\sigma
a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\sigma)
and
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
a cube, we let

\langle f\rangle_{\sigma,Q}=\frac{1}{\sigma(Q)}\int_{Q}fd\sigma.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
\sigma
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be some dyadic lattice from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\{a_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}
be a family of non-negative numbers. Suppose that for every cube
R\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}:Q\subset R}a_{Q}\leq c_{2}\sigma(R).

(14)

Then, every family of non-negative numbers
\{w_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}
satisfies

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}w_{Q}a_{Q}\leq c_{2}\int\sup_{Q\ni x}w_{Q}d\sigma(x).

Also, if
f\in L^{2}(\sigma)
,

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}|\langle f\rangle_{\sigma,Q}|^{2}a_{Q}\leq cc_{2}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\sigma)},

where
c
is an absolute constant.

2
A
Tb
type theorem

Below we state the main theorem of the present work. As stated in the Introduction, this is an extension of an analogous result by Nazarov, Treil and Volberg
[
11
]
. The theorem concerns a SIO associated to, in the sense of Definition
1.2
, an
n
-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
, defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x=y\}
, with associated constants
C_{CZ}>0
, which, for commodity, will be denoted also by
c
, which will absorb other constants, and
0<\eta\leq 1
(in the sense of Definition
2
). Let us fix these constants throughout the rest of the article.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\mu
be a finite measure supported on a compact set
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that there exist two complex measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
and, for each
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, three subsets
H_{\mathcal{D}(w)},T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)},T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
made of dyadic cubes from
\mathcal{D}(w)
such that

(a)

Every ball
B_{r}
of radius
r
such that
\mu(B_{r})>c_{0}r^{n}
is contained in
\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
.

(b)

\nu_{1}=b_{1}\mu
and
\nu_{2}=b_{2}\mu
, where
b_{1},b_{2}
are functions in
L^{\infty}(\mu)
, that is, such that
\|b_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)},\|b_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq c_{b}
.

(c)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}}T_{*}\nu_{1}\,d\mu\leq c_{*}\mu(F)
, and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}}T^{*}_{*}\nu_{2}\,d\mu\leq c_{*}\mu(F)
for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(d)

If
Q\in\mathcal{D}(w)
is such that
Q\not\subset T^{i}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
, then
\mu(Q)\leq c_{\text{acc}}|\nu_{i}(Q)|
, for
i=1,2
(we say that
Q
is an accretive cube).

(e)

\mu(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)})\leq\delta_{0}\mu(F)
, for all
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and some
\delta_{0}<1
.

Then, there exists a subset

\displaystyle G\subset F\setminus\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)

(15)

such that

(i)

\mu(G)\geq c_{1}^{-1}\mu(F)
.

(ii)

\mu\lfloor G
has
c_{0}
-linear growth.

(iii)

The SIO
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
.

The constant
c_{1}
and the bound for the
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
boundedness depend only on
C_{CZ},c_{0},c_{b},c_{*}
,
c_{\text{acc}}
and
\delta_{0}
.

Note that from condition (a) above, from condition (
15
) we infer that
\mu\lfloor G
has
c_{0}
-linear growth. So, to check (ii), it suffices to prove (
15
).

Before moving on to the proof, let us remark that it will be highly technical and quite long. This is why it has been divided in three sections. In the present one, we introduce some objects, which will be the right tools to prove Theorem
2.1
, and we describe and prove their useful properties. In Section
3
, we prove a key lemma concerning one of these objects. Section
4
is brief and consists in proving a theorem that is analogous to one of the results in the section containing the preliminaries. Lastly, Section
5
combines all the work from the previous ones to conclude the proof of Theorem
2.1
via a probabilistic argument.

2.1
The exceptional set
S

The long proof of Theorem
2.1
starts by defining a set
S
, which we call
exceptional
, through the identification of the points from the support of
\mu
in which the maximal functions
T_{*}\nu_{1}
and
T^{*}_{*}\nu_{2}
are uniformly bounded. In this section, we are going to show that
\mu(S\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)})
can be controlled by the measure of the set
F
by choosing a big enough bound for the maximal functions.

For now, let
\alpha\gg c_{0}c_{b}>0
be a fixed constant, to be chosen below. We set

\displaystyle S^{1}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in F:T_{*}\nu_{1}(x)>\alpha\}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in F:\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|T_{\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|>\alpha\right\},

\displaystyle S^{2}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in F:T^{*}_{*}\nu_{2}(x)>\alpha\}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in F:\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|>\alpha\right\}.

In addition, we let

\displaystyle e_{1}(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\sup\{\varepsilon>0:|T_{\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|>\alpha\},\quad&x\in S_{0}^{1},\\
0,&x\in F\setminus S_{0}^{1},\end{cases}

(16)

\displaystyle e_{2}(x)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\sup\{\varepsilon>0:|T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|>\alpha\},\quad&x\in S_{0}^{2},\\
0,&x\in F\setminus S_{0}^{2},\end{cases}

(17)

We define the
exceptional sets

S_{1}
and
S_{2}
as

S_{1}:=\bigcup_{x\in S^{1}_{0}}B(x,e_{1}(x)),\quad S_{2}:=\bigcup_{x\in S^{2}_{0}}B(x,e_{2}(x)).

Moreover, we put
S:=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. In the next lemma, we are going to see that we can make
\mu\left(S_{i}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)
small by taking
\alpha
big enough, for
i=1,2
. For simplicity, we denote

A=2^{n}C_{CZ}\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{\eta}-1}\right).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
y\in S_{1}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
, then
T_{*}\nu_{1}(y)>\alpha-Ac_{0}c_{b}
. Thus, if
\alpha>2Ac_{0}c_{b}
, then

\mu\left(S_{1}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)\leq\frac{2c_{*}}{\alpha}\mu(F).

Proof.

First, note that if
y\in S_{1}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
, then
y\in B(x,e_{1}(x))
for some
x\in S^{1}_{0}
, and so there is
0<r<e_{1}(x)
such that
y\in B(x,r)
. By definition of
e_{1}(x)
, there is some
r<\varepsilon_{0}(x)\leq e_{1}(x)
such that

\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(x)\right|>\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad y\in B(x,\varepsilon_{0}(x)).

To prove the first statement in the lemma, it suffices to show that

\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(x)-T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(y)\right|\leq Ac_{0}c_{b}.

(18)

Indeed, if the previous inequality holds, we deduce that

\displaystyle T_{*}\nu_{1}(y)\geq\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(y)\right|\geq\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(x)\right|-Ac_{0}c_{b}>\alpha-Ac_{0}c_{b}.

Let us prove (
18
). We have that
\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(x)-T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(y)\right|
is bounded above by

\displaystyle\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\left(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))\right)(x)\right|+\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\left(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))\right)(y)\right|

(19)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\left(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))^{c}\right)(x)-T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\left(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))^{c}\right)(y)\right|

(20)

To bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
19
), we write

\displaystyle|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x)))(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{|x-z|\geq\varepsilon_{0}(x)}k(x,z)d\left(\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))\right)(z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x-z|\geq\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\frac{C_{CZ}}{|x-z|^{n}}d\left|\nu_{1}\lfloor B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))\right|(z)

\displaystyle\leq C_{CZ}\frac{|\nu|(B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x)))}{\varepsilon_{0}(x)^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0},

where in the last inequality we have used that
y\not\in H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
, so
\mu(B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(y)))\leq c_{0}(2\varepsilon_{0}(x))^{n}
. The second term on the right-hand side of (
19
) is estimated analogously and it can also be bounded by
2^{n}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0}
. The last term, in (
20
), can be bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))}\left|k(x,z)-k(y,z)\right|d|\nu_{1}|(z).

(21)

For
z\not\in B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))
, since
y\in B(x,\varepsilon_{0}(x))
, we have that

|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}(x)\leq\frac{|z-y|}{2},

so (
21
) does not exceed

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(y,2\varepsilon_{0}(x))}C_{CZ}\frac{|x-y|^{\eta}}{|z-y|^{n+\eta}}d|\nu_{1}|(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq C_{CZ}c_{b}\varepsilon_{0}(x)^{\eta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{2^{k}\varepsilon_{0}(x)\leq|z-y|<2^{k+1}\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\frac{1}{|z-y|^{n+\eta}}d\mu(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq C_{CZ}c_{b}\varepsilon_{0}(x)^{\eta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(B(y,2^{k+1}\varepsilon_{0}(x)))}{2^{k(n+\eta)}\varepsilon_{0}(x)^{n+\eta}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=2^{n}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k\eta}}=\frac{2^{n}}{2^{\eta}-1}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0},

using again that
y\not\in H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
. So we get

\displaystyle|T_{\varepsilon_{0}(x)}\nu_{1}(x)-T_{\varepsilon_{0}}\nu_{1}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot 2^{n}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0}+\frac{2^{n}}{2^{\eta}-1}C_{CZ}c_{b}c_{0}

\displaystyle=2^{n}C_{CZ}\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{\eta}-1}\right)c_{b}c_{0}=Ac_{0}c_{b},

which is (
18
). Now, if
\alpha\geq 2Ac_{0}c_{b}
,

T_{*}\nu_{1}(y)>\alpha-Ac_{0}c_{b}\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}.

Using Chebyshev’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mu(S_{1}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)})

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left(\left\{y\in F\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}:T_{*}\nu_{1}(y)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{F\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}}T_{*}\nu_{1}(y)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}c_{*}\mu(F),

where in the last inequality we have used the first inequality from assumption (c) in Theorem
2.1
.
∎

Arguing analogously, we have the following estimate.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
y\in S_{2}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
, then
T^{*}_{*}\nu_{2}(y)>\alpha-Ac_{0}c_{b}
. Thus, if
\alpha>2Ac_{0}c_{b}
, then

\mu\left(S_{2}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)\leq\frac{2c_{*}}{\alpha}\mu(F).

As a consequence, for
\alpha>2Ac_{0}c_{b}
, we have that

\mu\left(S\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)\leq\mu\left(S_{1}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)+\mu\left(S_{2}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)\leq\frac{4c_{*}}{\alpha}\mu(F).

Now, set
\delta_{1}=(\delta_{0}+1)/2
(so that
\delta_{0}<\delta_{1}<1
). Choose
\alpha=\max(2Ac_{0}c_{b},8c_{*}/(1-\delta_{0}))
, so that

\mu\left(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)+\mu\left(S\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)\leq\left(\delta_{0}+\frac{4c_{*}}{\alpha}\right)\mu(F)\leq\delta_{1}\mu(F),

(22)

for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.2
The suppressed operators

As in
[
9
]
and
[
15
]
, we are going to consider a
suppressed
version of our kernel
k(\cdot,\cdot)
. That is, we are going to modify it in such a way that we obtain a new kernel with properties that make it easier to work with, while keeping it similar enough to the original one so that it enables us to deduce properties which, without the modification, would be much harder to prove.

For this, let
\Theta\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be such that

|\Theta(x)-\Theta(y)|\leq|x-y|,\quad\text{for all}\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(i.e.
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz). Moreover, let
\widetilde{\chi}\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a smooth cut-off function which vanishes identically in
[0,1/2]
and equals
1
in
[1,\infty)
. We consider a new kernel

\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)k(x,y),

(23)

and we put
\widetilde{\chi}(\infty)=1
. Similarly to what happened in
[
9
]
and
[
15
]
, we have that
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=k(x,y)
if
\Theta(x)
or
\Theta(y)
vanish. We now give three useful properties of the new kernel.

Lemma 2.4
.

We have that
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=0
if
|x-y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\text{max}(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))
.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\chi}
vanishes in
[0,1/2]
, it is enough to prove that if
|x-y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\text{max}(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))
, then
\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. This is an easy consequence of the fact that
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz. Indeed, assume first that
\Theta(x)\geq\Theta(y)
. Then,
\text{max}(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))=\Theta(x)
and so

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{4}\frac{\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\Theta(x)}{\Theta(y)}.

Moreover, using the Lipschitz property,

\Theta(y)\geq\Theta(x)-\frac{1}{2}\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))=\Theta(x)-\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)=\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x),

and so we deduce that

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{4}\frac{\Theta(x)}{\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{2}{4}=\frac{1}{2},

which is precisely what we wanted. Now, if
\Theta(x)<\Theta(y)
, the situation is symmetric and the same arguments yield that
\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the bound

|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))^{n}}.

Proof.

Using the previous lemma, this is an easy computation. Indeed, if
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are such that
|x-y|\leq\frac{1}{2}\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))
, then
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=0
and the inequality in the statement is trivially true. Assume that
|x-y|>\frac{1}{2}\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))
. Then, since
\|\widetilde{\chi}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\leq|k(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}}\leq\frac{c\,2^{n}}{\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))^{n}}.

∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\Theta\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be a
1
-Lipschitz function. Then, the kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
, defined in (
23
), is also a Calderón-Zygmund kernel.

Proof.

First, recall that
k
being an
n
-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel means that there exist constants
c>0
and
0<\eta\leq 1
such that the following inequalities hold for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\neq y
,

\displaystyle|k(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}},\quad\text{and,}

(24)

\displaystyle\text{if}\,|x-x^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|x-y|}{2},\,\,|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|+|k(y,x)-k(y,x^{\prime})|\leq\frac{c|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}.

(25)

We need to show that the analogous estimates hold for
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
. For the size estimate (
24
), we use that
\|\widetilde{\chi}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|=\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)k(x,y)\right|\leq|k(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}}.

To check the second condition (
25
), first we show that there exists a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that if
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|x-y|}{2}
,

|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|\leq\frac{c^{\prime}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}.

(26)

For this, we write,

\displaystyle|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle=\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)k(x,y)-\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)k(x^{\prime},y)\right|.

Now, note that if both

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{1}{2},

then
|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|=0
and (
26
) holds trivially. Moreover, if both

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\geq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\geq 1,

then
|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|=|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|
and we can take
c^{\prime}=c
in (
26
). Thus, we can assume that
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)\neq\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)
. With this in mind, we write,

\displaystyle|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)(k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y))\right|

\displaystyle\qquad+\left|\left[\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)\right]k(x^{\prime},y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|+\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}}.

(27)

The first term is already in the form that we want and we can bound it by

|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|\leq\frac{c|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}.

Now we look at the second term in (
27
). Since
\widetilde{\chi}
is smooth and has compact support, the norm
\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}
is a constant. We are now going to show that there is a constant
\widetilde{c}>0
such that

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{\widetilde{c}|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}\leq\frac{\widetilde{c}|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{\eta}},

(28)

where in the second inequality we have used that
\eta<1
and that
|x-x^{\prime}|/|x-y|\leq 1/2
. This, taking
c^{\prime}=c\left(1+\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\widetilde{c}\right)
, gives us (
26
).

To show that (
28
) holds, we denote

A(x,y)=\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)},\quad A(x^{\prime},y)=\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}.

Recall that our function
\widetilde{\chi}
vanishes in
[0,1/2]
, is constant equal to
1
in
[1,\infty)
and is smooth with
0\leq\widetilde{\chi}(t)\leq 1
for
t\in(1/2,1)
. We will prove (
28
) distinguishing whether
A(x,y)
and
A(x^{\prime},y)
are in
[0,1/2],(1/2,1)
or
[1,\infty)
.

•

Case 1:

A(x,y)\in(1/2,1)
or, equivalently,
|x-y|^{2}<\Theta(x)\Theta(y)<2|x-y|^{2}
.

•

Case 2:

A(x,y)\in[0,1/2]
or, equivalently,
|x-y|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\Theta(y)
.

•

Case 3:

A(x^{\prime},y)\in[0,1/2]
or, equivalently,
|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)
.

•

Case 4:

A(x^{\prime},y)\in(1/2,1)
, or, equivalently,
|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}<\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)<2|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}
and
\Theta(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})
.

•

Case 5:

\Theta(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})
.

Proving the inequality (
28
) in each case is a matter of computations, writing the same things in different ways according to which case we are in and using that
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz appropriately. For commodity of the reader, we gather all these computations in the five following lemmas.

Lemmas
29
through
37
show that in cases
1
through 4, we have

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq 40\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|},

which means that we can take
\widetilde{c}=50
in (
28
). Using Lemma
2.11
, there is no need to use the mean value theorem in the last line of (
27
), because we can simply bound

\displaystyle|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x^{\prime},y)|

\displaystyle\leq|k(x,y)-k(x^{\prime},y)|+|k(x^{\prime},y)|\frac{20}{3}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}+\frac{20c}{3}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{\eta}}

\displaystyle=c\left(1+\frac{20}{3}\right)\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{\eta}},

which is exactly what we wanted. Lastly, the estimate

|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(y,x)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(y,x^{\prime})|\leq c^{\prime}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|^{\eta}}{|x-y|^{n+\eta}}

is a consequence of the computations that we have already done, since the roles of
x
and
y
are interchangeable in
A(x,y)
and moreover
|x^{\prime}-y|\approx|x-y|
.

∎

The next five lemmas are purely technical and their sole purpose is to deal with the different cases that we encountered when trying that the kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
is Calderón-Zygmund, in the lemma above. After these details, we define the SIOs associated to this kernel in page
40
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Assume that
|x-y|^{2}<\Theta(x)\Theta(y)<2|x-y|^{2}
. Then,

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\left(\frac{5}{2}+\frac{45}{2}\right)\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

(29)

Proof.

We can write

\displaystyle\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}

\displaystyle=\frac{|x-y|^{2}-|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}+|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{1}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{|x-y|^{2}-|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}+|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)\Theta(x^{\prime})}.

(30)

We can control the numerator in the first term by

\displaystyle\left||x-y|^{2}-|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left||x-y|+|x^{\prime}-y|\right|\,||x-y|-|x^{\prime}-y||

\displaystyle\leq||x-y|+|x^{\prime}-x|+|x-y||\,|x-x^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{2}|x-y||x-x^{\prime}|.

(31)

Thus, using that
\Theta(x)\Theta(y)>|x-y|^{2}
,

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}-|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{5}{2}\frac{|x-y||x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|^{2}}=\frac{5}{2}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|},

which is in the form that we wanted. For the second term in (
30
), we use, in addition, that
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz, to get

|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\left|\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)\Theta(x^{\prime})}\right|\leq\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}|x^{\prime}-x|}{|x-y|^{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})}\leq\frac{9}{4}\frac{|x^{\prime}-x|}{\Theta(x^{\prime})},

(32)

where in the last inequality we have used that
|x^{\prime}-y|\leq\frac{3}{2}|x-y|
. We see that we need a lower bound for
\Theta(x^{\prime})
in terms of
|x-y|
. To obtain such a bound, we first show that

\min(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))\geq\frac{3}{5}|x-y|.

(33)

Indeed, if we assume that the reverse inequality holds, we obtain that

\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))=\frac{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}{\min(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))}>\frac{5}{3}\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{|x-y|}=\frac{5}{3}|x-y|,

and so

|\Theta(x)-\Theta(y)|=\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))-\min(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))\geq\frac{5}{3}|x-y|-\frac{3}{5}|x-y|>|x-y|,

which is not possible since
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz, so (
33
) holds. Lastly,

\displaystyle\Theta(x^{\prime})\geq\Theta(x)-|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\Theta(x)-\frac{1}{2}|x-y|

\displaystyle\geq\min(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))-\frac{1}{2}|x-y|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{5}|x-y|-\frac{1}{2}|x-y|=\frac{1}{10}|x-y|.

We plug this in (
32
) and we get

|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\left|\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)\Theta(x^{\prime})}\right|\leq\frac{10\cdot 9}{4}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}=\frac{45}{2}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

This proves that (
29
) holds.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Assume that
|x-y|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\Theta(y)
. Then,

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\left(\frac{5}{4}+\frac{9}{8}\right)\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

(34)

Proof.

We write the left-hand side of (
34
) as in (
30
). Using also (
31
) and our hypothesis, we can bound the first term in (
30
) by

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}-|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{5}{2}\frac{|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{5}{4}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

Moreover, the second term is controlled by

\left||x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}|x-x^{\prime}|}{2|x-y|^{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})}\leq\frac{9}{8}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x^{\prime})},

because
|x^{\prime}-y|\leq\frac{3}{2}|x-y|
. Now, we claim that our hypothesis implies that
\Theta(x)\geq|x-y|
. This, combined with the fact that
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz and that
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
, implies that
\Theta(x^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
, and so (
34
) holds. To prove that our claim is true, we assume that the opposite inequality holds, i.e.,
\Theta(x)<|x-y|
. Then,

\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\Theta(y)<\frac{1}{2}|x-y|\left(\Theta(x)+|x-y|\right)<|x-y|^{2},

which contradicts our hypothesis. Thus, the claim holds and we conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Assume that
|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)
. Then,

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{|\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(x)|}\right|\leq\left(5+12\right)\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

(35)

Proof.

The proof in this case is similar to the previous case, but now we need to be slightly more careful. Now it will be more convenient to write the left-hand side of (
35
) as

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}=|x-y|^{2}\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}-|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}.

(36)

Using (
31
) and our hypothesis, we can bound the second term in (
36
) by

\left|\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}-|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{5}{2}\frac{|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{5}{4}\frac{|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}\leq 5\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|},

where in the last inequality we have used that
|x^{\prime}-y|\geq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
. For the first term, we use the hypothesis to get that

\left||x-y|^{2}\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x)}.

We see that we are in a situation similar to what we had in the previous lemma; we should be able to bound
\Theta(x)\geq a|x-y|
for some constant
a>0
. For this, we will assume that instead of
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
, we have that
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{3}|x-y|
. This extra assumption causes no problems in showing that the suppressed kernel satisfies the inequalities required to be Calderón-Zygmund. First, since
\Theta
is
1
-Lipshitz, we clearly have that

\Theta(x)\geq\Theta(x^{\prime})-|x-x^{\prime}|\geq\Theta(x^{\prime})-\frac{1}{3}|x-y|\geq\Theta(x^{\prime})-\frac{2}{3}|x^{\prime}-y|.

We now claim that
\Theta(x^{\prime})\geq\frac{3}{4}|x^{\prime}-y|
. If this holds, we have that

\Theta(x)\geq\frac{1}{12}|x^{\prime}-y|\geq\frac{1}{24}|x-y|,

and so (
35
) holds. To see that our claim is true, assume that the opposite inequality holds, that is,
\Theta(x^{\prime})<\frac{3}{4}|x^{\prime}-y|
. In this case,

\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)<\frac{3}{8}|x^{\prime}-y|\left(\Theta(x^{\prime})+|x^{\prime}-y|\right)<\frac{21}{32}|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}<|x^{\prime}-y|^{2},

which contradicts our hypothesis that
|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)
. This means that
\Theta(x^{\prime})\geq\frac{3}{4}|x^{\prime}-y|
, thus finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Assume that
|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}<\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)<2|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}
and
\Theta(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})
. Then,

\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\left(10+\frac{80}{3}\right)\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

(37)

Proof.

We write the left-hand side of (
37
) as in (
36
).
Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
29
, we have that

||x^{\prime}-y|^{2}-|x-y|^{2}|\leq\frac{5}{2}|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|.

Moreover, since from
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
we have that

\frac{1}{2}|x-y|\leq|x^{\prime}-y|\leq\frac{3}{2}|x-y|,

we can rewrite our hypothesis as

\frac{1}{4}|x-y|^{2}<\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)<\frac{9}{2}|x-y|^{2}.

Using this, we can bound the second term in (
36
) by

\left|\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}-|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{5}{2}\frac{|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\leq 10\frac{|x-y|\,|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|^{2}}=10\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|},

which is in the form that we want. For the first term in (
36
), we have

|x-y|^{2}\left|\frac{\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\right|\leq\frac{|x-y|^{2}|x^{\prime}-x|}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}.

(38)

Analogously as we saw in the proof of Lemma
29
, we have that

\min(\Theta(x^{\prime}),\Theta(y))\geq\frac{3}{5}|x^{\prime}-y|\geq\frac{3}{10}|x-y|.

(39)

Recall that we assume that
\Theta(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})
. Combining this with the minimum condition (
39
), we have that
\Theta(x)\geq\frac{3}{20}|x-y|
. Hence, returning to (
38
),

\frac{|x-y|^{2}|x^{\prime}-x|}{\Theta(x)\Theta(x^{\prime})\Theta(y)}\leq 4\frac{|x-y|^{2}|x^{\prime}-x|}{\Theta(x)|x-y|^{2}}=4\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{\Theta(x)}\leq\frac{80}{3}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|},

and we conclude.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that
\Theta(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})
. Then,

\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)\right|\leq\frac{20}{3}\frac{|x-x^{\prime}|}{|x-y|}.

Proof.

First, since
0\leq\widetilde{\chi}(t)\leq 1
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x^{\prime}-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)\right|\leq 1.

Once again, we have that
\min(\Theta(x^{\prime}),\Theta(y))\geq\frac{3}{10}|x-y|
. Moreover, using that
\Theta
is
1
-Lipschitz,

|x-x^{\prime}|\geq|\Theta(x)-\Theta(x^{\prime})|\geq\Theta(x^{\prime})-\Theta(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x^{\prime})\geq\frac{3}{20}|x-y|.

Hence,
{|x-x^{\prime}|}/{|x-y|}\geq 3/20
, which concludes the proof.
∎

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
, and a complex Radon measure
\nu
, we set

\displaystyle K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(x,\varepsilon)}\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)\,d\nu(y),\quad K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}^{*}\nu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(x,\varepsilon)}\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(y,x)\,d\nu(y).

(40)

The operator
K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}
is the (
\varepsilon
-truncated)
\Theta
-suppressed
version of the operator
T
and analogously for
K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon}
and
T^{*}
. We also set

K_{\Theta,*}\nu(x)=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu(x)|,\quad K_{\Theta}\nu(x)=K_{\Theta,0}\nu(x),

assuming that the integral that defines
K_{\Theta,0}\nu(x)
exists. Given
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, to simplify notation, we will write

K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f:=K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}(f\mu),\quad K_{\Theta,*}f:=K_{\Theta,*}(f\mu),\quad K_{\Theta}f:=K_{\Theta}(f\mu),

and, as usual, we will use the analogous notation for
K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon},K^{*}_{\Theta,*}
and
K_{\Theta}^{*}
.

In the following lemma, we are going to relate the truncated operators
T_{\varepsilon}
with the truncated suppressed operators
K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}
. We give estimates for both
T
and
T^{*}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and suppose that
\Theta(x)\leq\varepsilon
. Let
\sigma
be a complex Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, we have the following estimates,

|T_{\varepsilon}\sigma(x)-K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\sigma(x)|\leq c\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}},\quad|T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\sigma(x)-K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\sigma(x)|\leq c\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-k(x,y)|

\displaystyle=\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)k(x,y)-k(x,y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-1\right||k(x,y)|\leq\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-1\right|\frac{C_{CZ}}{|x-y|^{n}}.

We need to estimate the difference inside the absolute value. First, note that if
|x-y|^{2}\geq\Theta(x)\Theta(y)
, then it is zero. Now, assume that
|x-y|^{2}<\Theta(x)\Theta(y)
. We apply the mean value theorem,

\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-1\right|=\left|\widetilde{\chi}\left(\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right)-\widetilde{\chi}(1)\right|\leq\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-1\right|.

Using that
|x-y|^{2}<\Theta(x)\Theta(y)
, we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}-1\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{|x-y|^{2}-\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\right|=\frac{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)-|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}{|x-y|^{2}}\leq\frac{\Theta(x)\left(\Theta(x)+|x-y|\right)}{|x-y|^{2}}=\frac{\Theta(x)^{2}}{|x-y|^{2}}+\frac{\Theta(x)}{|x-y|}.

Hence,

|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\sigma(x)-T_{\varepsilon}\sigma(x)|\leq\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}C_{CZ}\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\Theta(x)^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2}}+\frac{\Theta(x)}{|x-y|^{n+1}}\right)d|\sigma|(y).

To estimate the last integrals, we write
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x-y|>\varepsilon\}
as a disjoint union of annuli,

\displaystyle\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+2}}d|\sigma|(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\int_{2^{k}\varepsilon<|x-y|\leq 2^{k+1}\varepsilon}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+2}}d|\sigma|(y)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,2^{k+1}\varepsilon))}{(2^{k}\varepsilon)^{n+2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{n+2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,2^{k+1}\varepsilon))}{(2^{k+1}\varepsilon)^{n}}\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{n+2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{4^{k+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{1}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}.

Analogously,

\int_{|x-y|>\varepsilon}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n+1}}d|\sigma|(y)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\varepsilon}\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}.

Using these two estimates, we can bound the difference

\displaystyle|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\sigma(x)-R^{n}_{\varepsilon}\sigma(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\left(\frac{C_{1}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Theta(x)^{2}+\frac{C_{2}}{\varepsilon}\Theta(x)\right)\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\sigma|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}},

where we have used our assumption that
\Theta(x)\leq\varepsilon
. The proof for the estimate involving
T^{*}_{\varepsilon}
and
K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon}
is the same, since the role of
x
and
y
inside
\widetilde{\chi}
can be interchanged in the definition of
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)
.
∎

Continuing in the line of the relation between the truncated operators and both truncated and suppressed operators, in the following lemma we are going to see how knowledge about the size of the first ones gives information about the size of the others.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
, possibly depending on
x
, be such that

•

\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}
for
r\geq\text{min}(r_{1},r_{2})
,

•

|T_{\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|\leq\alpha
for
\alpha\geq r_{1}
and
|T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|\leq\alpha
for
\alpha\geq r_{2}
.

Then,

(a)

If
\Theta(x)\geq r_{1}
, we have
|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|\leq\alpha+cc_{b}c_{0}
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

(b)

If
\Theta(x)\geq r_{2}
, we have
|K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|\leq\alpha+cc_{b}c_{0}
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

In particular, if
\Theta(x)\geq\max(r_{1},r_{2})
, both estimates hold simultaneously for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

For the commodity of the reader, we will only give the details of the proof of (a). A careful inspection of our argument quickly reveals that the proof of (b) is almost identical.

That being said, in order to prove (a) we will treat two cases separately. First, if
\varepsilon\geq\Theta(x)\geq r_{1}
, we use the previous lemma and we infer that in this situation we can bound

|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|\leq|T_{\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|+c\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{|\nu_{1}|(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}\leq\alpha+cc_{b}\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}\leq\alpha+cc_{b}c_{0}.

Now, if the opposite inequality holds, that is, if
\varepsilon<\Theta(x)
, we can bound

|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|\leq c_{b}\int_{B(x,\Theta(x))}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\,d\mu(y)+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(x,\Theta(x))}\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)\,d\nu_{1}(y)\right|.

(41)

For the first integral, we write

\int_{B(x,\Theta(x))}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|d\mu(y)=\int_{B(x,\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x))}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|d\mu(y)+\int_{\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\leq|x-y|<\Theta(x)}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|d\mu(y).

Since
\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}\max(\Theta(x),\Theta(y))
, by Lemma
2.4
, we have that
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=0
for all
y\in B(x,\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x))
. Thus, the right-hand side of the previous equation is bounded by

\int_{\frac{1}{2}\Theta(x)\leq|x-y|<\Theta(x)}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|d\mu(y)\leq 2^{n}\frac{\mu(B(x,\Theta(x)))}{\Theta(x)^{n}}\leq 2^{n}c_{0}=cc_{0},

since as
r_{1}\leq\Theta(x)
, we have that
\mu(B(x,\Theta(x)))\leq c_{0}\Theta(x)^{n}
.

Lastly, the second integral in (
41
) equals
K_{\Theta,\Theta(x)}\nu_{1}(x)
and, by our computation above, it is bounded by
\alpha+cc_{b}c_{0}
.
∎

Now, recall point (a) of Theorem
2.1
, which is the converse inequality to the one in the first hypothesis from the previous lemma,

(a) Every ball
B_{r}
of radius
r
such that
\mu(B_{r})>c_{0}r^{n}
is contained in
\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
.

The behavior of these balls motivates the following definition.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
x\in F
. We say that a ball
B(x,r)
is
non-
n
-Ahlfors
if

\mu(B(x,r))>c_{0}r^{n}.

In addition, we define the
n
-Ahlfors radius
of the point
x
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup\{r>0:\mu(B(x,r))>c_{0}r^{n}\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{r>0:B(x,r)\text{\, is a non-$n$-Ahlfors ball\,}\}.

If there does not exist any
r>0
such that
\mu(B(x,r))>c_{0}r^{n}
, we simply set
\mathcal{R}(x)=0
.

Note that for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, all non-
n
-Ahlfors balls and the balls
B(x,e_{1}(x)),B(x,e_{2}(x))
, defined in (
16
) and (
17
), are contained in
H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S
. This observation will be essential in proving the following lemma.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\Theta\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be a
1
-Lipschitz function such that

\Theta(x)\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S)),

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then,
K_{\Theta,*}\nu_{1}(x)\leq c_{4}
and
K_{\Theta,*}^{*}\nu_{2}(x)\leq c_{4}
for all
x\in F
, with
c_{4}
depending on
c_{0},c_{b},c_{*}
, and
\delta_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in F
and call
r_{0}=\max(\mathcal{R}(x),e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x))
. We claim that
\Theta(x)\geq r_{0}
.

Obviously, if
x\not\in H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S
,
r_{0}=\mathcal{R}(x)=e_{1}(x)=e_{2}(x)=0
, so the inequality holds trivially. If
x\in H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S
, we can see that
\Theta(x)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)
and
\Theta(x)\geq\max(e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x))
separately.

First, we show that
\Theta(x)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{R}(x)=0
, it is clear. If
\mathcal{R}(x)>0
, for any
0<r<\mathcal{R}(x)
, by definition of supremum there is some
r<r^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{R}(x)
such that
B(x,r^{\prime})
is non-
n
-Ahlfors, so
B(x,r^{\prime})\subset H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
. Hence,

\Theta(x)\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S))\geq r^{\prime}>r,\quad\text{for all}\quad r<\mathcal{R}(x),

from which we deduce that
\Theta(x)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)
.

Now, to prove that
\Theta(x)\geq\max(e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x))
, we will only show the details of the proof of
\Theta(x)\geq e_{1}(x)
. The other inequality is proved analogously.

If
x\not\in S^{1}_{0}
, we have that
e_{1}(x)=0
and so the inequality is clear. If
x\in S_{0}
, then
x\in B(x,e_{1}(x))\subset S
, so

\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S))\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S_{1}))\geq e_{1}(x),

and in this case the inequality is also satisfied. Analogously,
\Theta(x)\geq e_{2}(x)
.

We can apply the last part of Lemma
2.13
with this choice of
r_{0}
. Indeed, since
r_{0}\geq\mathcal{R}(x)
, for
r\geq r_{0}
we have that
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}
. Also, if
\varepsilon\geq r_{0}\geq\max(e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x))
, by definition of
e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x)
, we have that
|T_{\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|,|T^{*}_{\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|\leq\alpha
. This allows us to deduce that for all
\varepsilon>0
,
|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{1}(x)|,|K^{*}_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\nu_{2}(x)|\leq\alpha+cc_{b}c_{0}
. We conclude by taking supremum over
\varepsilon>0
.
∎

2.3
Dyadic lattices and the martingale decomposition

2.3.1
Random dyadic lattices

As we will see in the end of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, a probabilistic argument will play a key role in showing the
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
boundedness of
T
. The motivation for introducing randomness is that we are going to relate boundedness of our original operators with that of other operators arising from taking averages (or, in probabilistic terms, expectations) over the points of a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with which we translate the usual dyadic lattice. In this section, we define the set
\Omega
.

Let
N\geq 1
be some big integer, which we will choose later, and define
S^{0}=\left[0,2^{N}\right]^{d}
. In the rest of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, we assume that

\text{supp}(\mu)=F\subset\frac{1}{8}S^{0}.

Moreover, we set

\Omega=\left[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}\right]^{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d},

and we consider dyadic lattices
\mathcal{D}(w)
as in (
13
), with
w\in\Omega
. We write

Q^{0}(w)=w+S^{0},

(42)

so that
Q^{0}(w)\in\mathcal{D}(w)
. Moreover, by our choice of
\Omega
, we have the following.

Lemma 2.16
.

For all
w\in\Omega
, we have
F\subseteq\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}(w)
.

Proof.

Indeed, if we denote by
z_{Q^{0}(w)}
and
z_{S^{0}}
the centers of the cubes
Q^{0}(w)
and
S^{0}
, respectively, we have that

\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}(w)=w+z_{S^{0}}+\left[-2^{N-3},2^{N-3}\right]^{d}.

Let
x\in F
be arbitrary. In order to show that
x\in\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}(w)
, we have to prove that

\|x-(w-z_{S^{0}})\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\ell\left(\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}(w)\right)}{2}=2^{N-3}.

Using that
x\in\frac{1}{8}S^{0}
, we have that
\|x-z_{S^{0}}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\ell\left(\frac{1}{8}S^{0}\right)}{2}=2^{N-4}
. Hence,

\|x-(w-z_{S^{0}})\|_{\infty}\leq\|x-z_{S^{0}}\|_{\infty}+\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{N-4}+2^{N-4},

where we have used that
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{N-4}
, thus completing the proof.
∎

We will denote by
P^{\Omega}
the uniform probability on
\Omega
, that is, the normalized Lebesgue measure on the cube
\Omega
.

2.3.2
Transit and terminal cubes

Recall that in the first section we have singled out an exceptional set with the intention of controlling the maximal functions
T_{*}\nu_{1}
and
T^{*}_{*}\nu_{2}
. This will not be the only instance of the use of such a technique in the proof of Theorem
2.1
. In the present section, we are going to classify the dyadic cubes of any given lattice,
\mathcal{D}
, taking into account whether or not they are contained in the special sets
H_{\mathcal{D}},T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}
or
T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}
. We will see that, on the one hand, the cubes that are not contained inside these sets have some desirable properties regarding the growth of the
\mu
-measure of their dilates and their side lengths. On the other hand, by considering the maximal cubes contained inside the special sets, we will obtain a disjoint family of cubes with side lengths comparable to those of the better-behaved ones. We give this classification now.

Fix
w\in\Omega
and consider the dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}\equiv\mathcal{D}(w)
. Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}
be contained in
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}=Q^{0}(w)
with
\mu(Q)>0
. We say that the cube
Q
is

•

terminal of the first
(resp.
second
)
kind
if
2Q\subset H_{\mathcal{D}}
or if
Q\subset T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}
(resp.
2Q\subset H_{\mathcal{D}}
or
Q\subset T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}
),

•

transit of the first
(resp.
second
)
kind
if it is not terminal of the first (resp. second) kind.

We denote the set of cubes that are terminal of the first kind by
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},1}
, the set of those that are terminal of the second kind by
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term}}=\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},1}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}
and analogously with
\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}
for transit cubes.

Note that we assume all transit and terminal (of the first and second kind) cubes to be contained in
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
. This is because if a cube (or part of it) is outside
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
, then it cannot contribute any
\mu
-measure (or the only contribution comes from inside
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
).

From assumption (e) in Theorem
2.1
,
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
is always transit of both kinds. Indeed, if it were terminal of any kind, it would be contained in
H_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T_{\mathcal{D}}^{2}
, and so by (e),

\mu(Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}})\leq\mu(H_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}})\leq\delta_{0}\mu(F)<\mu(F).

However, by Lemma
2.16
,

\mu(F)\leq\mu\left(\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)\leq\mu(Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}),

which contradicts the previous inequality.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}\cup\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}
. Then,

\mu(\lambda Q)\leq c_{0}\ell(\lambda Q)^{n},\quad\text{for all }\,\lambda\geq 1.

Moreover, if
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}
, we have
\mu(Q)\leq c_{\text{acc}}|\nu_{1}(Q)|
, and for
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}
, the corresponding inequality is
\mu(Q)\leq c_{\text{acc}}|\nu_{2}(Q)|
.

Proof.

Since
Q
is transit,
Q\not\subset H_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}
. In particular,
Q\not\subset H_{\mathcal{D}}
and so by assumption (a) of Theorem
2.1
, any ball containing
Q
satisfies
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}
. Hence, if
z_{Q}
is the center of
Q
,

\mu(\lambda Q)\leq\mu(B(z_{Q},\lambda\ell(Q)))\leq c_{0}\ell(Q)^{n}.

Also, from
Q\not\subset T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}
, from assumption (d) in Theorem
2.1
, we have that
\mu(Q)\leq c_{acc}|\nu_{i}(Q)|
, for
i=1,2
.
∎

2.3.3
The martingale decomposition

So far, our classification of dyadic cubes of a given lattice has only given us information about the measure
\mu
on the dilates of the cubes and the relation between the
\mu
-measure and the
\nu_{i}
-measure of said cubes. However, the distinction between transit and terminal cubes has much more to offer. Indeed, in the following pages, we are going to define for each transit cube an operator on
L^{2}(\mu)
and with a little work we will be able to express any function from this space as a sum of these operators acting on our function. This is a standard technique in Harmonic Analysis and will involve the averages of our functions over the transit cubes, together with those of the functions
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
.

Let
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
,
Q
and any cube with
\mu(Q)\neq 0
, we put

\langle f\rangle_{Q}=\frac{1}{\mu(Q)}\int_{Q}f\,d\mu.

Remark 2.18
.

An observation which will be useful below is what follows. If
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, then
|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|\geq c_{\text{acc}}^{-1}
. Indeed, we can write

|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|=\frac{1}{|\mu(Q)|}\left|\int_{Q}b_{i}\,d\mu\right|=\frac{|\nu_{i}(Q)|}{|\mu(Q)|}\geq\frac{|\nu_{i}(Q)|}{c_{\text{acc}}|\nu_{i}(Q)|}=c_{\text{acc}}^{-1},

because if
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, in particular,
Q\not\subset T^{i}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

Moreover, we define the operators
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
as

\Xi_{1}f=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{1}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b_{1},\quad\Xi_{2}f=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b_{2},

where
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are the functions from Theorem
2.1
. Since
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
is always transit, by Lemma
2.17
, we have that neither
\langle b_{1}\rangle_{Q_{\mathcal{D}}^{0}}
nor
\langle b_{2}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}
vanish. Moreover, the definition of
\Xi_{i}
does not depend on the choice of the lattice
\mathcal{D}(w)
,
w\in\Omega
, since
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
contains the support of
\mu
. We display some more useful facts concerning these operators in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.19
.

For
i=1,2
, we have that

(a)

\Xi_{i}\,\colon L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)
are bounded, with norm depending on
\|b_{i}\|_{\infty}
and
\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}
.

(b)

\Xi_{i}^{2}=\Xi_{i}
, where
\Xi_{i}^{2}
means the composition of
\Xi_{i}
with itself.

(c)

The adjoint of
\Xi_{i}
in
L^{2}(\mu)
is

\Xi_{i}^{*}f=\frac{\langle fb_{i}\rangle_{Q_{\mathcal{D}}^{0}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}.

These properties are verified by means of simple computations, which we omit here.

Recall that, for a fixed dyadic cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, the set of, at most
2^{d}
, children of
Q
is denoted by
\mathcal{CH}(Q)
. From now on, we will use the same notation to refer to the set of children whose
\mu
-measure is not zero.

For any cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
and any
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mu)
, we define two new functions,
\Delta_{i,Q}f
,
i=1,2
, as follows,

\displaystyle\Delta_{i,Q}f=\begin{cases}0\qquad&\text{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}P,\\
\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b_{i}\qquad&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }\,P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i},\\
f-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}b_{i}&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}.\end{cases}

We collect some properties of the operators
\Delta_{i,Q}
in the next lemma.

Lemma 2.20
.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and all
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
,

(a)

\Delta_{i,Q}f\in L^{2}(\mu)
,

(b)

\int\Delta_{i,Q}fd\mu=0
,

(c)

\Delta_{i,Q}
is a projection, that is,
\Delta^{2}_{i,Q}=\Delta_{i,Q}
,

(d)

\Delta_{i,Q}\Xi_{i}=\Xi_{i}\Delta_{i,Q}=0
,

(e)

if
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, then
\Delta_{i,Q}\Delta_{i,R}=0
,

(f)

The adjoint of
\Delta_{i,Q}
is

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}f=\begin{cases}0\qquad&\text{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}P,\\
\frac{\langle fb_{i}\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle fb_{i}\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\qquad&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }\,P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i},\\
f-\frac{\langle fb_{i}\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof of assertions (a)-(f) is similar to the proof of Lemma
2.19
