, which we omitted, but the computations here are somewhat more involved. This is why we show the proofs of (b), (c), (d) and give an indication for (e).

Starting with (b), for
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, we can enumerate its children as
\{P_{k}\}_{k=1}^{2^{d}}
. Then,

\int\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu=\int_{Q}\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu=\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d}}\int_{P_{k}}\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu.

(43)

First, if
P_{k}\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
,

\displaystyle\int_{P_{k}}\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{P_{k}}\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P_{k}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P_{k}}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b_{i}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\langle f\rangle_{P_{k}}\mu(P_{k})-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\mu(P_{k})\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P_{k}}=\int_{P_{k}}f\,d\mu-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\int_{P_{k}}b_{i}\,d\mu.

Second, if
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, by definition of
\Delta_{i,Q}
it is obvious that

\int_{P_{k}}\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu=\int_{P_{k}}f\,d\mu-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\int_{P_{k}}b_{i}\,d\mu.

Lastly, if
P_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\cup_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}P
,

\int_{P_{k}}\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu=0=\int_{P_{k}}f\,d\mu-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\int_{P_{k}}b_{i}\,d\mu.

Hence, we can return to (
43
) and we find that

\displaystyle\int\Delta_{i,Q}f\,d\mu=\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d}}\int_{P_{k}}f\,d\mu-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\sum_{k=1}^{2^{d}}\int_{P_{k}}b_{i}\,d\mu=\int_{Q}f\,d\mu-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\int b\,d\mu=0.

To show (c), note that using (b) we can write

\displaystyle\Delta^{2}_{i,Q}f=\Delta_{i,Q}(\Delta_{i,Q}f)=\begin{cases}0\qquad&\text{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}P,\\
\frac{\langle\Delta_{i,Q}f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}},\qquad&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }\,P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i},\\
\Delta_{i,Q}f\qquad&\text{in }\,P\,\text{ if }\,P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}.\end{cases}

Hence, we only need to compute, for
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
,

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{i,Q}f\rangle_{P}=\frac{1}{\mu(P)}\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b\,d\mu=\langle f\rangle_{P}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}.

Multiplying by
\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}
, we obtain our claim. For (d), first we check that
\Delta_{i,Q}\Xi_{i}f=0
. We can write

\Delta_{i,Q}\Xi_{i}f=\Delta_{i,Q}\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b\right)=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}\Delta_{i,Q}b_{i}.

That is, we need to show that
\Delta_{i,Q}b_{i}=0
. This is verified by a very quick computation that we will omit. Now, using (b), we have that

\Xi_{i}\Delta_{i,Q}f=\frac{\langle\Delta_{i,Q}f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b_{i}=0,

which finishes the proof of (d). Lastly, for the proof of (e), one needs to distinguish whether
Q\cap R=\varnothing
or if they have intersection. Furthermore, one can fix which of
Q
and
R
has biggest side length and then use the fact that if two dyadic cubes have intersection and one has smaller side length than the other, the first is contained in only one of the children of the second.
∎

Now, let us show the main purpose of introducing such operators in
L^{2}(\mu)
. The following lemma, which will be key in the rest of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, shows that we can write any function
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
as the sum of the operators
\Delta_{i,Q}
and
\Xi_{i}
applied to
f
.

Lemma 2.21
.

For
i=1,2
, for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, we have the decomposition

f=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}f,

(44)

where the sum is unconditionally convergent in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Moreover, there exists some constant
c_{3}
depending on
c_{b}
and
c_{\text{acc}}
such that

c_{3}^{-1}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|\Xi_{i}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{3}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(45)

Proof.

We are going to follow four steps to prove the lemma. First, we will prove the second inequality in (
45
) and the first one for finite sums. Afterwards, we will prove (
44
) and lastly, the first inequality in (
45
) in full generality.

Step 1:
the second inequality in (
45
).
For
Q\in\mathcal{D}
, we define the operator
D_{Q}
as

D_{Q}f:=\begin{cases}0\qquad&\text{in }\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Q,\\
\langle f\rangle_{P}-\langle f\rangle_{Q}&\text{in }\,P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q),\end{cases}

and
Ef:=\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}
. Then, because of orthogonality,

\|Ef\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(46)

This sort of decomposition is widely used in the context of
Tb
type theorems. This technique was initiated, in the context of the Cauchy transform, by Coifman, Jones and Semmes, see
[
3
]
.

We can separate the middle term in (
45
) as

\|\Xi_{i}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

(47)

As noted in Lemma
2.19
, the first term is bounded by a constant times
\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. We now focus on the second term in (
47
). If
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, we have that

\Delta_{i,Q}f|_{P}=\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b_{i}=\left(\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)\langle f\rangle_{P}b_{i}+\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\underbrace{\left(\langle f\rangle_{P}-\langle f\rangle_{Q}\right)}_{D_{Q}f}b_{i}.

By Remark
2.18
, we have that
|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|\geq c_{\text{acc}}^{-2}
. Moreover, using that
b_{i}
is bounded, we get that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{i,Q}f|_{P})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)^{2}\langle f\rangle_{P}^{2}b_{i}^{2}+\frac{2}{\langle b_{i}\rangle^{2}_{Q}}|D_{Q}f|^{2}b_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{b}^{2}\left[\frac{(D_{Q}b_{i}|_{P})^{2}}{|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|^{2}}\langle f\rangle^{2}_{P}+\frac{|D_{Q}f|^{2}}{|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\left|D_{Q}b_{i}|_{P}\right|^{2}\langle f\rangle_{P}^{2}+|D_{Q}f|^{2}\right]

Therefore, we may bound

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}\|\chi_{P}D_{Q}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\langle f\rangle_{P}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=:(\text{I})+(\text{II}).

From (
46
), we have that
(\text{II})\leq\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Now, we can rewrite
(\text{I})
as

(\text{I})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\chi_{Q}D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\langle f\rangle^{2}_{Q},

where
\widehat{Q}
is the parent of
Q
. To estimate this quantity, we will apply the dyadic Carleson embedding Theorem
1.12
. We define
a_{Q}:=\|\chi_{Q}D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Let us check that these numbers satisfy the hypothesis (
14
) from Theorem
1.12
. For any cube
R\in\mathcal{D}
, we can write

\sum_{Q\subseteq R}\|\chi_{Q}D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\|D_{\widehat{R}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor R)}+\sum_{Q\subsetneq R}\|D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Since
b_{i}
is bounded, the first term can be bounded by
c\mu(R)
. For the second, we can apply the decomposition (
46
) to the function
b_{i}\chi_{R}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\subsetneq R}\|D_{\widehat{Q}b_{i}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\sum_{Q\subsetneq R}\|D_{\widehat{Q}}(b_{i}\chi_{R})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\sum_{Q\subseteq R}\|D_{Q}(b_{i}\chi_{R})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|b_{i}\chi_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{b}^{2}\mu(R).

This shows that the condition (
14
) holds and thus we have that
(\text{I})\leq\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
.

Now we will deal with the last term of (
47
). For
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
and
P\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, we can write

\Delta_{i,Q}f|_{P}=f-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}b_{i}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}=\left(f-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}b_{i}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)+\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b_{i}.

Using again Remark
2.18
and the fact that
b_{i}
is bounded, we get

\displaystyle|\Delta_{i,Q}f|_{P}|\leq c\left[\left(|f|+\langle|f|\rangle_{P}\right)+|\langle f\rangle_{P}-\langle f\rangle_{Q}|\right]=c\left[(|f|+\langle|f|\rangle_{P})+|D_{Q}f|_{P}|\right].

Hence,

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{P}\left((|f|+\langle|f|\rangle_{P})^{2}+|D_{Q}f|\right)^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq 2c\int_{P}(|f|+\langle|f|\rangle_{P})^{2}\,d\mu+2c\int_{P}|D_{Q}f|^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq 4c\int_{P}|f|^{2}\,d\mu+4c\int_{P}\langle|f|\rangle_{P}^{2}\,d\mu+2c\|D_{Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq c\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}+c\|D_{Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where in the last inequality we have used Jensen’s inequality. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}+c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(48)

The second term above can be bounded by
\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, using (
46
). Furthermore, note that the cubes
P\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
whose parent is transit are pairwise disjoint. Indeed, if
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
,
\widehat{P}_{1},\widehat{P}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
and
P_{1}\cup P_{2}\neq\varnothing
, we can assume that
P_{2}\subseteq P_{1}
. Since all the descendants of
P_{1}
must be in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, in particular
\widehat{P}_{2}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, which is a contradiction. This allows us to bound the first term in (
48
) by
c\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, finishing the proof of the second inequality in (
45
).

Step 2:
the first inequality in (
45
) for finite sums.
Consider an arbitrary finite subset
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
and, for the fixed function
f
in the statement of the lemma, define a new function

g=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\Delta_{i,Q}f.

From properties (d) and (e) of Lemma
2.20
, we have that
\Xi_{i}g=\Xi_{i}f
, and

\Delta_{i,Q}g=\begin{cases}\Delta_{Q}f,\qquad&\text{if }\,Q\in\mathcal{F},\\
0&\text{if }\,Q\not\in\mathcal{F}.\end{cases}

Hence, the decomposition (
44
) is trivially true for
g
. From property (b) of Lemma
2.19
and property (c) of Lemma
2.20
, we have

g=\Xi_{i}g+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}g=\Xi^{2}_{i}g+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta^{2}_{i,Q}g.

Using this decomposition, assuming that the functions that appear take real values,

\displaystyle\int|g|^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int\left(\Xi^{2}_{i}g+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta^{2}_{i,Q}g\right)g\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int\left((\Xi_{i}g)(\Xi_{i}^{*}g)+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}(\Delta_{i,Q}g)(\Delta_{i,Q}^{*}g)\right)\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(|\Xi_{i}g|^{2}+\left|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}g\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(|\Xi^{*}_{i}g|^{2}+\left|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}^{*}g\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\left[\int\left(|\Xi_{i}g|^{2}+\left|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}g\right|^{2}\right)d\mu\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\int\left(|\Xi^{*}_{i}g|^{2}+\left|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}^{*}g\right|^{2}\right)d\mu\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left[\|\Xi_{i}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\left\|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}g\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\|\Xi^{*}_{i}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\left\|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left[\|\Xi_{i}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\|\Xi^{*}_{i}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}},

where we have used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality two times, first in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the Euclidean norm and afterwards, in
L^{2}(\mu)
. From the last inequality, we see that we will complete the second step if we are able to show that

\|\Xi_{i}^{*}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}^{*}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

By the expression of
\Xi^{*}_{i}
from Lemma
2.19
, it is clear that

\|\Xi_{i}^{*}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

which means that the only term left to estimate is
\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. As in the first step, we separate this sum as

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

(49)

Our strategy will be once more to relate
\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g
to
D_{Q}g
. If
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
, using the expression for
\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}
from Lemma
2.20
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g|_{P}

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}-\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}+\langle gb_{i}\rangle\left(\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{1}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)=\frac{D_{Q}(gb_{i})|_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}-\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}D_{Q}b_{i}|_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}.

Again, using Remark
2.18
,
|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}|,|\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}|\geq c_{\text{acc}}^{-1}
, and so

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}(gb_{i})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}\|\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}D_{Q}b_{i}|_{P}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(50)

Again, from the decomposition (
46
), we deduce

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}(gb_{i})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|gb_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Note that the last term in (
50
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}|\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{Q}|^{2}\|\chi_{Q}D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and to estimate it we will apply again the dyadic Carleson embedding Theorem
1.12
. If we call
a_{Q}:=\|\chi_{Q}D_{\widehat{Q}}b_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, from the estimate in the previous step we have that the packing condition (
14
) is satisfied and thus we obtain

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)}\|\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}D_{Q}b_{i}|_{P}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}\leq c\|gb_{i}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Lastly, we need to deal with the terminal cubes in (
49
). For
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
and
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, we have

\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g|_{P}=g-\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}=\left(g-\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)+\left(\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}-\frac{\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right).

As usual, we bound

|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g|_{P}|\leq c\left[\left(|g|+\langle|g|\rangle_{P}\right)+\left|\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{P}-\langle gb_{i}\rangle_{Q}\right|\right]=c\left[\left(|g|+\langle|g|\rangle_{P}\right)+\left|D_{Q}(gb_{i})\right|\right]

This yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}+c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}\|D_{Q}(gb_{i})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Arguing by the same methods as in (
48
), we get that

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\cap\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor P)}\leq c\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

which finishes the proof of the second inequality in (
45
) for finite sums.

Step 3:
proof of (
44
).
We start by showing the unconditional convergence of the series that appears in (
44
). For this, let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
be a finite subset, and call
g=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\Delta_{i,Q}f
. Then, by the previous step,

\bigg\|\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\Delta_{i,Q}f\bigg\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

From this, recalling that by the first step,

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}<\infty,

we deduce the unconditional convergence in
L^{2}(\mu)
of the series
\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\Delta_{i,Q}f
.

Now, since convergence in
L^{2}(\mu)
implies
\mu
-a.e. convergence of a subsequence, to prove (
44
), it suffices to check that

\Xi_{i}f(x)+\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}:\ell(Q)\geq 2^{-n}}\Delta_{i,Q}f(x)=f(x),\quad\text{for }\,\mu\text{-a.e. }\,x\in F.

To do so, we distinguish two cases. Assume first that
x\in Q_{0}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, with
\ell(Q_{0})=2^{n_{0}},n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
Q_{0}
is the biggest terminal cube (of the
i
-th kind) that contains
x
. Such a cube
Q_{0}
always exists, because
x\in Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
, which is not terminal of any kind. If
x\in Q
and
\ell(Q)<\ell(Q_{0})
,
Q\subseteq Q_{0}
, so
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, so we have that

\Xi_{i}f(x)+\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}:\ell(Q)\geq 2^{-n}}\Delta_{i,Q}f(x)=\Xi_{i}f(x)+\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}:\ell(Q)>2^{n_{0}}}\Delta_{i,Q}f(x).

Call
Q_{1}
the cube in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
with minimal side length that contains
Q
. This means that the unique child of
Q_{1}
that contains
x
is in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
, so

\Delta_{i,Q_{1}}f(x)=f(x)-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q_{1}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q_{1}}}b_{i}(x).

If we denote by
Q_{2}
the parent of
Q_{1}
, since
Q_{2}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
,

\Delta_{i,Q_{2}}f(x)=\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q_{1}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q_{1}}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q_{2}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q_{2}}}\right)b_{i}(x).

Hence,

\Delta_{i,Q_{1}}f(x)+\Delta_{i,Q_{2}}f(x)=f(x)-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q_{2}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q_{2}}}b_{i}(x).

We iterate this process until we reach
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
,

\Delta_{i,Q_{1}}f(x)+\Delta_{i,Q_{2}}f(x)+\cdots+\Delta_{i,Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}f(x)=f(x)-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b_{i}(x)=f(x)-\Xi_{i}f(x).

Lastly, since we always assume transit cubes to be contained in
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
, the biggest cube that appears in the limit we have to compute is
Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}
, and so the limit is true for such
x
.

Assume now that
x
does not belong to any cube in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},i}
. Fix any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
n
is not too negative, there exists only one cube
Q\ni x
with
\ell(Q)=2^{-n}
and
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}
. If
P
be the only child of
Q
that contains
x
and
Q^{1}
is the parent of
Q
,

\Delta_{i,Q}f(x)=\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}\right)b_{i}(x),\quad\Delta_{i,Q^{1}}f(x)=\left(\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{1}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{1}}}\right)b_{i}(x).

Thus,

\Delta_{i,Q}f(x)+\Delta_{i,Q^{1}}f(x)=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}b_{i}(x)-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{1}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{1}}}b_{i}(x).

We can iterate this process, similarly to the previous case, and we get that

\Delta_{i,Q}f(x)+\cdots+\Delta_{i,Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}f(x)=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}b_{i}(x)-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}}}}b_{i}(x)=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}b_{i}(x)-\Xi_{i}f(x).

We now write

\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}b_{i}(x)=\frac{1}{\mu(P)}\int_{P}f(y)\,d\mu(y)\left(\frac{1}{\mu(P)}\int_{P}b_{i}(y)\,d\mu(y)\right)^{-1}b_{i}(x).

By the dyadic Lebesgue differentiation Theorem
1.10
, we find that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{i}\rangle_{P}}b_{i}(x)=f(x),\quad\text{for }\,\mu\text{-a.e. }\,x\in F,

which is what we needed to prove.

Step 4:
proof of the first inequality in (
45
).
In the second step, the advantage of considering only finite sums was that the decomposition (
44
) was true. Now we know that it holds for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, so following exactly the same steps as before, we obtain the first inequality in (
45
) for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
.
∎

Remark 2.22
.

The previous lemma also holds by replacing
\Xi_{i}
and
\Delta_{i,Q}
by
\Xi_{i}^{*}
and
\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}
, respectively. That is, for
i=1,2
and
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, we also have the inequalities

c^{-1}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\|\Xi_{i}^{*}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}}\|\Delta^{*}_{i,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(51)

2.4
Good and bad cubes and functions

In the preceding section we have seen that the transit cubes, which enjoy some desirable properties with respect to the measure
\mu
, can be used to decompose any function
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. The next step to study
L^{2}
boundedness of our operator
T
will be to use this decomposition when working with the suppressed operators
K_{\Theta}
. However, the distinction between transit and terminal cubes will not be enough, and we will need to further classify the transit cubes.

More precisely, we are now going to define what it means for a dyadic cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}(w_{1})
to be bad (or good) with respect to another dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
, with
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega
. The purpose of doing so is that, using the decomposition in (
44
), we will be able to write
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
as

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=f_{\text{good}}(w_{1},w_{2})+f_{\text{bad}}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}(w_{1})\cap\text{good}(w_{2})}\Delta_{i,Q}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}(w_{1})\cap\text{bad}(w_{2})}\Delta_{i,Q}f.

(52)

Then, denoting by
P^{\Omega^{2}}=P^{\Omega}\times P^{\Omega}
the product probability measure in
\Omega\times\Omega
, and
\text{w}=(w_{1},w_{2})
, for an appropriately chosen
1
-Lipschitz function
\Psi_{\text{w}}
, we will define the operator

\widetilde{K}f(x)=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w}).

We will later see that we can prove the
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
-boundedness of our singular integral operator
T
by proving that
\widetilde{K}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
. To do so, if we denote by
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}
the expectation with respect to
P^{\Omega^{2}}
, and for
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
,

\langle f,g\rangle=\int fgd\mu,

we will see that we need the bound

\left|\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f,g\rangle\right)\right|=|\langle\widetilde{K}f,g\rangle|\leq c\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(53)

Using the decomposition (
52
), we can write the expectation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f,g\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}},g_{\text{good}}\rangle)+\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}},g_{\text{bad}}\rangle)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{bad}},g\rangle).

(54)

To obtain the bound in (
53
), first we are going to show that for the good part we almost have a bound of the type

|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}},g_{\text{good}}\rangle|\leq c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
almost
here means that on the right hand side we will get an extra term, which will be dealt with in the final step of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, using a probabilistic argument.

For the other two terms in (
54
), which contain the bad part, we are going to choose an appropriate definition of bad cubes such that for each fixed
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}(w_{1})
, the probability that it is bad with respect to the other dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
can be made arbitrarily small. Using this, we will be able to make the two last terms in (
54
) also small, thus proving the
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
boundedness of
\widetilde{K}
.

To define what it means for a cube to be bad, we need the following notion. If
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we say that
\partial Q
is
(\mu,M)
-small if

\mu\left(\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\text{dist}(x,\partial Q)\leq\lambda\ell(Q)\right\}\right)\leq\lambda M\,\|\mu\|,

for all
\lambda>0
. If
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is another cube, we say that
\partial Q
is
(\mu,M,P)
-small if
\partial Q
is
(\mu\lfloor P,M)
-small.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
, with
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega
, be two dyadic lattices and put
\gamma=\frac{\eta}{2(n+\eta)}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we say that a transit cube (of the
i
-th kind)
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{1}
is
i
-
bad
(with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
) if either

(a)

there exists a cube
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}
and
2^{m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{N}
(where
m\geq 1
is some integer that we will fix below), or

(b)

there exists a transit cube
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}}}_{2}
such that
2^{-m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
,
\text{dist}(Q,R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
such that for some
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)
, there is
S\in\mathcal{CH}(R)
such that
\partial P
is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small.

If
Q
is not
i
-bad, then we say that it is
i
-
good
. It is important to remark that we say that a cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
is good or bad depending on another lattice
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. This dependence on another dyadic lattice is new; recall that the definition of transit and terminal cubes did not contain any interaction between two different lattices. Moreover, the definition of bad cubes also depends on the constants
m
and
M
, but for commodity we will not talk about
(m,M)
-bad cubes, only bad cubes.

With this notion, we can define precisely what it means for a function to be good. Recall that given any fixed dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
, by Lemma
2.21
, we can write any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
as

f=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{1}}\Delta_{i,Q}f.

We say that
f
is
i
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-
good with respect to

\mathcal{D}_{2}
(or simply,
i
-good) if
\Delta_{i,Q}f=0
for all
i
-bad cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{1}
(with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
).

3
Estimates for good functions

3.1
The main lemma for good functions

Recall that in Section
2.2
, we considered
\Theta\,\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,+\infty)
and we defined a new Calderón-Zygmund kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
, which had some properties that the original kernel
k
did not necessarily satisfy (see Lemma
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
). Our goal now will be to estimate the action of the SIO
K_{\Theta}
, which arises from the kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
, on good functions, which we defined in the last section of the preceding section. In this direction, we will have to restrict the function
\Theta
a little more, in the sense that we will need it to be sensible to the sets that are singled out in the statement of Theorem
2.1
and in Section
2.1
. We give the details of this connection between the function and said sets below.

For each dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
, we define the
total exceptional set
as

W_{\mathcal{D}}=H_{\mathcal{D}}\cup T_{\mathcal{D}}^{1}\cup T_{\mathcal{D}}^{2}\cup S.

Moreover, we define the function

\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}(x)=\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}}).

This is a
1
-Lipschitz function that vanishes on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}}
. Moreover,
\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}(x)\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(H_{\mathcal{D}}\cup S))
, so that from Lemma
2.15
, we obtain that

K_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},*}\nu_{1}(x)\leq c_{4},\quad K_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},*}^{*}\nu_{2}(x)\leq c_{4},\quad\text{for all }\,x\in F.

In the next lemma, we show that an inequality of the type in (
53
) almost holds for good functions
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
and an appropriate
1
-Lipschitz function
\Theta
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
, with
w_{1},w_{2}\in\Omega
, be two dyadic lattices. Given
\varepsilon>0
, let
\Theta\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[\varepsilon,\infty)
be a
1
-Lipschitz function such that
\Theta(x)\geq\max(\Phi_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}(x),\Phi_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
f
is
1
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
g
is
2
-
\mathcal{D}_{2}
-good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then

|\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle|\leq c_{5}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
\text{dist}(Q,R)<\text{max}(\ell(Q),\ell(R))\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|,

where
c_{5}
is a constant that depends on
c_{0},c_{b},c_{*}
and
\delta_{0}
, but not on
\varepsilon
.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that the assumption that the function
\Theta
is bounded below by some positive constant
\varepsilon>0
is only for technical reasons, as it ensures that the operator
K_{\Theta}
is bounded in
L^{p}(\mu)
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
(with norm depending on
\varepsilon
). Indeed, if
\Theta(x)\geq\varepsilon
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then we have that

\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\Theta(x)\Theta(y)}\leq\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}},

from which we see that if
|x-y|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}
,
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)=0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left|K_{\Theta}f(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{|x-y|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}}\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)f(y)\,d\mu(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{|x-y|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}}\left|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\left(\int_{|x-y|>\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}}|f(y)|^{p}\,d\mu(y)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{CZ}2^{\frac{n}{2}}}{\varepsilon^{n}}\mu(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}.

In particular,
K_{\Theta}f
is well defined for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. The main point in the lemma is that the estimate does not depend on
\varepsilon
, unlike above.

3.1.1
Beginning of the proof of Lemma
3.1

In the next few pages, we are going to introduce the strategy for the proof of Lemma
3.1
. We are going to use the decomposition in
L^{2}(\mu)
, given by Lemma
2.21
, in terms of the operators
\Delta_{1,Q}
and
\Delta_{2,R}
for
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
and
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, to show that the bound for
\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle
reduces to studying a sum of terms of the form
\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle
. Once we have shown this, we are going to separate the aforementioned sum according to the relation between the cubes
Q
and
R
. The bounds for each of the terms arising from this separation will be obtained through different arguments for each one and for the most part will be highly technical.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
be as in Lemma
3.1
;
f
is
1
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and
g
is
2
-
\mathcal{D}_{2}
good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. Then, we can write them as

f=\Xi_{1}f+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1}\\
1\text{-}\mathcal{D}_{1}\text{-good w.r.t. }\mathcal{D}_{2}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{1,Q}f,\qquad g=\Xi_{2}g+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\text{tr},2}\\
2\text{-}\mathcal{D}_{2}\text{-good w.r.t. }\mathcal{D}_{1}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{2,R}g

(55)

Since we also assume that
\Theta(x)\geq\varepsilon>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by our previous remark we have that
\|K_{\Theta}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}<\infty
(depending on
\varepsilon
), and thus we can also apply Lemma
2.21
to
K_{\Theta}f
,

K_{\Theta}f=K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1}\\
1\text{-}\mathcal{D}_{1}\text{-good w.r.t. }\mathcal{D}_{2}\end{subarray}}K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),

(56)

with the sum being unconditionally convergent in
L^{2}(\mu)
, and the analogous identity also holds for
g
.

To prove the lemma, we will assume that
\Delta_{1,Q}f=0
except for a finite family of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
and
\Delta_{2,R}g=0
except for another finite family of cubes
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
. To our advantage, the estimates that we will obtain will not depend on the number of non-zero terms. This assumption will help us because there will be no problems with convergence and will allow us to change the order of summation in the sums involving the cubes
Q,R
. The lemma in the general case will then follow by taking
L^{2}(\mu)
limits, using the fact that
\|K_{\Theta}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}<\infty
and the identity (
56
).

We will say that
f
is
very

1
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to

\mathcal{D}_{2}
(or just
1
-very good or even very good) if it is
1
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
and moreover
\Delta_{1,Q}f=0
except for a finite family of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
. We also consider the analogous notation interchanging
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
.

To simplify notation, from now on we are going to write
Q^{0}=Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
and
R^{0}=Q^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
(as defined in (
42
)). Recall that our goal now is to bound
\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle
. To do so, using the decompositions (
55
), we can write

\displaystyle\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Xi_{2}g+\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Theta}\left(\Xi_{1}f\right),g\rangle+\langle K_{\Theta}(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Xi_{2}g\rangle+\langle K_{\Theta}(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f),g\rangle+\langle K_{\Theta}f,\Xi_{2}g\rangle-\langle K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f),\Xi_{2}g\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\langle K_{\Theta}(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle.

(57)

We now show that the first two terms in (
57
) can be estimated easily. Indeed, by Lemma
2.15
,
K_{\Theta,*}\nu_{1}(x)\leq c_{4}
and
K_{\Theta,*}^{*}\nu_{2}(x)\leq c_{4}
, for all
x\in F
, with
c_{4}
depending on
c_{0},c_{b},c_{*}
and
\delta_{0}
, so we have

\left|K_{\Theta}b_{1}(x)\right|=\left|K_{\Theta}\nu_{1}(x)\right|\leq c_{4},\quad\left|K_{\Theta}^{*}b_{2}(x)\right|=\left|K_{\Theta}^{*}\nu_{2}(x)\right|\leq c_{4},\quad x\in F.

Thus, since both
Q^{0}
and
R^{0}
are transit cubes, we can use Remark
2.18
to bound
|\langle b_{1}\rangle_{Q^{0}}|
and
|\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R^{0}}|
from below, and by the previous bounds,

\displaystyle\|K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}}\right|}{\left|\langle b_{1}\rangle_{Q^{0}}\right|}\|K_{\Theta}b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\left|\langle f\rangle_{Q^{0}}\right|\mu(Q^{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

\displaystyle\|K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{2}g)\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\langle f\rangle_{R^{0}}\right|}{\left|\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R^{0}}\right|}\|K_{\Theta}b_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\left|\langle g\rangle_{R^{0}}\right|\mu(R^{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq c\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Therefore, on the one hand we have that

\left|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f),g\rangle\right|\leq c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

On the other hand, by duality,

\left|\langle K_{\Theta}f,\Xi_{2}g\rangle\right|=\left|\langle f,K_{\Theta}^{*}(\Xi_{2}g)\rangle\right|\leq c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Now, for the third term in (
57
), we can use the same estimate for
f
and the fact that
\Xi_{2}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, and we obtain that

\left|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Xi_{1}f),\Xi_{2}g\rangle\right|\leq c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Hence, to prove the lemma it is enough to estimate the quantity

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle.

(58)

We are going to use different approaches to bound this last sum depending on the relative positions and sizes of the cubes that are involved. This is why we introduce the following notion. We say that two cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
are
distant
if

\text{dist}(Q,R)\geq\min(\ell(Q),\ell(R))^{\gamma}\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R))^{1-\gamma}.

Recall that
\gamma=\frac{\eta}{2(n+\eta)}
, from the definition of bad cubes.

Remark 3.3
.

If
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1}
and
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\text{tr},2}
are disjoint cubes which are not distant and both are good (each with respect to the lattice to which the other cube belongs), it turns out that

2^{-m}\ell(R)<\ell(Q)<2^{m}\ell(R),

where
m
is the integer from the definition of bad cubes. Indeed, assume first that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
. Then, since
Q
and
R
are disjoint and not distant,

\text{dist}(Q,R)=\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}.

Moreover, since
Q
is good, there does not exist any
\widetilde{R}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that

\text{dist}(Q,\partial\widetilde{R})\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(\widetilde{R})^{1-\gamma}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(\widetilde{R})\geq 2^{m}\ell(Q).

Hence, since
R
satisfies the first condition, it must be that
\ell(R)<2^{m}\ell(Q)
, that is,
2^{-m}\ell(R)<\ell(Q)
, which is the first inequality in our claim. Regarding the second inequality, since
m\geq 1
, we have that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)<2^{m}\ell(R)
. Assume now that
\ell(Q)>\ell(R)
. Then,

\text{dist}(Q,R)=\text{dist}(R,\partial Q)\leq\ell(R)^{\gamma}\ell(Q)^{1-\gamma}.

Since
R
is good, arguing analogously as before we find that
\ell(Q)<2^{m}\ell(R)
and since
m\geq 1
,
2^{-m}\ell(R)<\ell(R)<\ell(Q)
.

This new notion is what motivates us to separate the sum in (
58
) as follows,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\cdots+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}\cdots

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant}\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R=\varnothing\\
Q,R\text{ not distant}\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R=\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\text{ or }\ell(R)<2^{-m}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=:S_{1}+S_{2}+S_{3}+S_{4}.

(59)

Our strategy now to complete the proof of Lemma
3.1
will be to show that sum of the above is bounded by
c\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. We are going to do this in sections
3.2
through
3.4
.

3.2
Estimate of the sum
S_{1}
: distant cubes

We can split the sum
S_{1}
in two by considering

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant }\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant }\\
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant }\\
\ell(Q)>\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle=:S_{1,1}+S_{1,2}.

First we will deal with the sum
S_{1,1}
and once we have obtained the right bound for it we will argue that by analogous arguments, we obtain the same bound for
S_{1,2}
.

A quantity that will be useful to us is the following,

D(Q,R):=\text{dist}(Q,R)+\ell(Q)+\ell(R),

which we call the
long distance
between
Q
and
R
. Using this notion, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be disjoint cubes and let
\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\in L^{2}(\mu)
be functions supported on
Q
and
R
, respectively. If
\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq\ell(Q)
and
\int\varphi_{Q}\,d\mu=0
, then

|\langle K_{\Theta}\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(60)

If, in addition,
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
and for some constant
c_{6}>0
,

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq c_{6}\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma},

(61)

then we also have

|\langle K_{\Theta}\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(62)

Proof.

Let us denote by
z_{Q}
the center of the cube
Q
. From the assumption, we infer that if
s\in Q
and
t\in\text{supp}(\psi_{R})
are arbitrary points, then

|z_{Q}-t|\approx|s-t|\geq\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq\ell(Q)\geq c_{d}|s-z_{Q}|,

where
c_{d}
is a constant that only depends on the dimension
d
, because
s\in Q
. Thus, we can use the Calderón-Zygmund estimate we know for the kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
to obtain

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Theta}\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\left|\iint\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(t,s)\varphi_{Q}(s)\psi_{R}(t)\,d\mu(s)\,d\mu(t)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\iint\left(\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(t,s)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(t,z_{Q})\right)\varphi_{Q}(s)\psi_{R}(t)\,d\mu(s)\,d\mu(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\iint\frac{|s-z_{Q}|^{\eta}}{|t-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}\varphi_{Q}(s)\psi_{R}(t)\,d\mu(s)\,d\mu(t)

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)},

which is exactly (
60
). Suppose now that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
and that the condition (
61
) holds. If
\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq\ell(R)
, the previous inequality becomes

|\langle K_{\Theta}\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(63)

We claim that in this situation, the following three quantities are comparable,

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\approx\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\approx D(Q,R).

(64)

Indeed, for the first one, one inequality is obvious, and for the other, just notice that by our hypothesis, we have

3\,\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))+\ell(Q)+\ell(R).

To see the second comparability in (
64
), since
\text{supp}(\psi_{R})\subseteq R
, it is obvious that

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\geq\text{dist}(Q,R)+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)=D(Q,R).

For the other inequality, note that

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\leq\text{dist}(Q,R)+\text{diam}(R)\leq c(\text{dist(Q,R)}+\ell(R)),

where
c
depends only on the dimension,
d
. Hence,

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\leq c^{\prime}D(Q,R).

If we plug this in (
63
), we get (
84
). Now, if
\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\leq\ell(R)
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))^{n+\eta}}\leq\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{(c_{6}\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma})^{n+\eta}}=\frac{c_{6}^{-n-\eta}\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{n+\eta}}\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}},

where we have used that in this case
D(Q,R)\leq 3\ell(R)
.
∎

This lemma, along with the following, will be the essential ingredient in dealing with the term
S_{1,1}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

(Schur’s lemma) Let
I
be some set of indices, and for each
i\in I
a number
w_{i}>0
. Suppose that, for some constant
a\geq 0
, the matrix
\{T_{i,j}\}_{i,j\in I}
satisfies

\sum_{j\in I}|T_{i,j}|\,w_{j}\leq a\,w_{i},\quad\text{for each }\,i,

and

\sum_{i\in I}|T_{i,j}|\,w_{i}\leq a\,w_{j},\quad\text{for each }\,j.

Then, the matrix
\{T_{i,j}\}_{i,j\in I}
defines a bounded operator in
\ell^{2}(I)
with norm bounded above by
a
.

Proof.

Let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset\ell^{2}(I)
and set
y_{i}=\sum_{j\in I}T_{i,j}x_{j}
. We have to show that

\sum_{i\in I}|y_{i}|^{2}\leq a^{2}\sum_{i\in I}|x_{i}|^{2}.

It will be useful to write

|T_{i,j}||x_{j}|=\left(|T_{i,j}|^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(|T_{i,j}|^{\frac{1}{2}}w_{j}^{-\frac{1}{2}}|x_{j}|\right),

to which we apply Cauchy-Schwarz and we get

|y_{i}|^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{j\in I}|T_{i,j}|w_{j}\right)\left(\sum_{j\in I}|T_{i,j}|w_{j}^{-1}|x_{j}|^{2}\right)\leq a\,w_{i}\sum_{j\in I}|T_{i,j}|w_{j}^{-1}|x_{j}|^{2},

where we have used the first hypothesis. Now, summing over
i\in I
and interchanging the order of the sums, the second hypothesis yields

\sum_{i\in I}|y_{i}|^{2}\leq a\sum_{j\in I}\left(\sum_{i\in I}|T_{i,j}|w_{i}\right)w_{j}^{-1}|x_{j}|^{2}\leq a^{2}\sum_{j\in I}|x_{j}|^{2},

as desired.
∎

Combining the two previous lemmas we will be able to bound the term
S_{1,1}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
are as in Lemma
3.1
and they are very good, then we have

\displaystyle S_{1,1}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

(65)

Proof.

Recall that the cubes
Q,R
that appear in the sum
S_{1,1}
are distant and moreover
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
. This means that

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\Delta_{2,R}g))\geq\text{dist}(Q,R)\geq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}\geq\ell(Q).

Hence, we can apply the second estimate from Lemma
3.4
and we obtain that

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where in the second inequality we have simply used Cauchy-Schwarz. Summing over
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
,
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
and
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
, we obtain the first inequality in (
65
). To prove the second inequality in (
65
), we want to use Schur’s lemma with the matrix
\{T_{Q,R},{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\}
defined by

T_{Q,R}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad&\text{if }\,(Q,R)\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\times\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\,\text{ and }\,\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Recall that by Lemma
2.21
,

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq c\,\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},\quad\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

so we only have to show that the matrix defined above generates a bounded operator in
\ell^{2}
, in the sense that

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}T_{Q,R}\,a_{Q}\,b_{R}\leq\left(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}a_{Q}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}b_{Q}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

for all
\ell^{2}
sequences
\{a_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}
,
\{b_{R}\}_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}
. Choosing

a_{Q}=\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{R}=\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

we will obtain (
65
). Using Schur’s lemma, it is enough to prove that

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}T_{Q,R}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq c\,\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\text{for all }\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2},

(66)

and

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}T_{Q,R}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq c\,\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad\text{for all }\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},

(67)

that is, we have chosen the numbers
w_{Q}=\mu(Q)>0
that appear in the statement of Schur’s lemma. First we prove (
66
). Fix
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
and write

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}T_{Q,R}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)

\displaystyle=\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\left(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)\right).

We can rewrite the last sum as follows. If we denote
2^{-1}R=\varnothing
,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\cap 2^{j}R\neq\varnothing\\
Q\cap 2^{j-1}R=\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q),

(68)

that is, we are adding the contribution of each
Q
depending on how big we have to make the fixed cube
R
in order to meet
Q
. We claim that in this situation, for each
j\geq 0
,
D(Q,R)\geq c\ell(2^{j}R)
. Indeed, from the condition
Q\cap 2^{j-1}R=\varnothing
, we have that, if
z_{R}
denotes the center of
R
,
\|x-z_{R}\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}\ell(2^{j-1}R)
for all
x\in Q
. Hence, for
x\in Q
and
y\in R
,

|x-y|\geq\|x-y\|_{\infty}\geq\|x-z_{R}\|_{\infty}-\|y-z_{R}\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}\ell(2^{j-1}R)-\frac{1}{2}\ell(R).

Therefore, taking infimum we obtain

\displaystyle D(Q,R)

\displaystyle=\text{dist}(Q,R)+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\ell(2^{j-1}R)-\frac{1}{2}\ell(R)+\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\geq\frac{1}{2}\ell(2^{j-1}R)=\frac{1}{4}\ell(2^{j}R).

Hence, the right-hand side in (
68
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle c\sum_{j\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\cap 2^{j}R\neq\varnothing\\
Q\cap 2^{j-1}R=\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{\ell(2^{j}R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)

\displaystyle=c\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{\ell(2^{j}R)^{n+\eta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\cap 2^{j}R\neq\varnothing\\
Q\cap 2^{j-1}R=\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\mu(Q)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{\ell(2^{j}R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(2^{j+2}R)

\displaystyle=c\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\mu(2^{j+2}R)}{\ell(2^{j}R)^{n}2^{j\eta}}.

(69)

Recall that by Lemma
2.17
, since
R
is transit,

\mu(2^{j+2}R)\leq c_{0}\,\ell(2^{j+2}R)^{n}=c\,\ell(2^{j}R)^{n},

so the sum in (
69
) is bounded above by some constant that depends on
c_{0}
and
\eta
. If we plug this inequality in (
68
), we obtain (
66
). The last thing left is to prove (
67
), which we will do by similar arguments as what we have just done. Fix
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
and write, as before,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}T_{Q,R}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(R)

\displaystyle=\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\left(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(R)\right).

Now, for each
k\geq 0
, call
Q^{k}
a cube concentric with
Q
whose side length is
2^{k}\ell(Q)=\ell(R)
. Then, arguing exactly as before,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(R)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\cap 2^{j}Q^{k}\neq\varnothing\\
R\cap 2^{j-1}Q^{k}=\varnothing\\
\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(R),

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\ell(Q^{k})^{\eta}}{\ell(2^{j}Q^{k})^{n+\eta}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\cap 2^{j}Q^{k}\neq\varnothing\\
R\cap 2^{j-1}Q^{k}=\varnothing\\
\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\mu(R)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\mu(2^{j+2}Q^{k})}{\ell(2^{j}Q^{k})^{n}2^{j\eta}}.

Again by Lemma
2.17
,
\mu(2^{j+2}Q^{k})\leq c\,\ell(2^{j}Q^{k})^{n}
, so as before we obtain (
67
).
∎

Now we recover the sum
S_{1,2}
. Recall that

S_{1,2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant }\\
\ell(Q)>\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ distant }\\
\ell(Q)>\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K_{\Theta}^{*}(\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle|.

From this we see that we need the analogous of Lemmas
3.4
, but interchanging the roles of
Q
and
R
and
K_{\Theta}
and
K^{*}_{\Theta}
. One can go over the proof and see that in that case the same estimates hold by exactly the same arguments, only by changing the names of the cubes and using that the SIO
K^{*}_{\Theta}
has the same relevant properties as
K_{\Theta}
. That is, we have the following result.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be disjoint cubes and let
\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\in L^{2}(\mu)
be functions supported on
Q
and
R
, respectively. If
\text{dist}(R,\text{supp}(\varphi_{Q}))\geq\ell(R)
and
\int\psi_{R}\,d\mu=0
, then

|\langle\varphi_{Q},K^{*}_{\Theta}(\psi_{R})\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(R)^{\eta}}{\text{dist}(R,\text{supp}(\varphi_{Q}))}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

If, in addition,
\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)
and for some constant
c_{6}>0
,

\text{dist}(R,\text{supp}(\varphi_{Q}))\geq c_{6}\ell(R)^{\gamma}\ell(Q)^{1-\gamma},

then we also have

|\langle\varphi_{Q},K^{*}_{\Theta}(\psi_{R})|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Now, recall that the cubes
Q,R
that appear in the sum
S_{1,2}
are distant and moreover
\ell(Q)>\ell(R)
. This means that

\text{dist}(R,\text{supp}(\Delta_{1,Q}f))\geq\text{dist}(Q,R)\geq\ell(R)^{\gamma}\ell(Q)^{1-\gamma}\geq\ell(R).

Hence, we can apply the second estimate from the previous Lemma and we obtain that

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Since the last bound is symmetric in
Q
and
R
(note that here it does not matter whether the cubes are terminal of the first or second king), by the second estimate in Lemma
3.6
, it is controlled above by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, which finishes the estimate of the sum
S_{1}
.

Before moving on to the next estimates, let us make a final remark about how we bounded the sum
S_{1}
. The fact that we were dealing only with transit cubes (of either kind) was crucial. Indeed, it allowed us to use Lemma
2.17
and bound for every transit cube
S
and
\lambda\geq 1
,
\mu(\lambda S)\leq c_{0}\ell(\lambda S)^{n}
, which would not have been possible if there had been terminal cubes, for which we do not have such a type of control, in the martingale decomposition given by Lemma
2.21
.

3.3
Estimate of the sum
S_{4}

As we did with the sum
S_{1}
, we can split the sum
S_{4}
in two by considering

\displaystyle S_{4}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\text{ or }\ell(R)<2^{-m}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{R}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\cdots+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
\ell(R)<2^{-m}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\cdots

\displaystyle=:S_{4,1}+S_{4,2}.

By duality, we can write the second sum as

S_{4,2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
\ell(R)<2^{-m}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle.

Since the assumptions that
K_{\Theta}^{*}
satisfies are analogous to those for
K_{\Theta}
(for example, recall how Lemma
3.4
has its corresponding version with
K^{*}_{\Theta}
, Lemma
3.7
), it is enough to prove the desired estimate only for
S_{4,1}
, which we are going to do in this section.

It is important to recall that all these estimates are part of the proof of Lemma
3.1
, and so the functions
f
and
g
are supposed to be good, each with respect to the indicated lattices. This means that the cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
appearing in the sum
S_{4,1}
are
1
-
\mathcal{D}_{1}
-good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, and analogously for the cubes
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. In particular, recalling condition (a) in the definition of bad cubes, we see that every
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
in the sum
S_{4,1}
must satisfy that

\text{dist}(Q,\partial S)>\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma},\quad\text{for every }\,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\,\text{ such that }\,\ell(S)\geq 2^{m}\ell(Q).

(70)

Since every cube
R
in the sum
S_{4,1}
satisfies that
\ell(R)\geq 2^{m+1}\ell(Q)
, we can choose the cube
S
to be any of the
2^{d}
children of
R
. Moreover, since
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing
and the distance to the boundary is strictly positive, we see that
Q
must be completely contained in one of these
2^{d}
children, which we will denote from now on by
R_{Q}
.

Now, we can use the distinction between transit and terminal cubes to split further the sum
S_{4,1}
, by considering

\displaystyle S_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle

(71)

\displaystyle:=S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}+S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}.

3.3.1
Estimate of
S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}

We can use duality to write
S_{4,1}^{\text{tr}}
as

\displaystyle S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R\setminus R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle.

(72)

The last sum on the right-hand side above will be easy to deal with, using our work from the previous section, as shown in the next lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
be very good. Then,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K_{\Theta}^{*}(\chi_{R\setminus R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(73)

Proof.

Let
Q
and
R
be as above and good. Moreover, since
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)
, we have

\displaystyle\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\chi_{R\setminus R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g))

\displaystyle\geq\text{dist}(Q,\partial R_{Q})\geq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R_{Q})^{1-\gamma}=2^{\gamma-1}\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}.

We can order the children of
R
that do not contain
Q
as
\{R_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{d}-1}
. Then,

\left|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R\setminus R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2^{d}-1}|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,Q}g)\rangle|.

For each
1\leq j\leq 2^{d}-1
, since
R_{j}\subset R\setminus R_{Q}
,

\displaystyle\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,R}g))\geq\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\chi_{R\setminus R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g))

\displaystyle\geq 2^{\gamma-1}\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}

\displaystyle\geq c\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R_{j})^{1-\gamma}.

So, for each
R_{j}
we can apply Lemma
3.4
and we obtain that

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,R}g)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R_{j})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R_{j})^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(74)

Taking into account that
\ell(R_{j})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}=2^{-\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}
,
\|\chi_{R_{j}}\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}
and that

D(Q,R_{j})=\text{dist}(Q,R_{j})+\ell(Q)+\ell(R_{j})\geq\underbrace{\text{dist}(Q,R)}_{=0}+\ell(Q)+\frac{1}{2}\ell(R_{j})\geq\frac{1}{2}D(Q,R),

we have that the right-hand side of (
74
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle c^{\prime}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Summing over the children of
R
, we obtain that the sum in (
73
) is bounded by a constant times the sum in (
65
), from the previous section. Hence, by Lemma
3.6
, it is bounded by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Now it will be useful to consider, for
g\in L^{2}(\mu)
as above, the number

c_{R,Q}(g)=\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R_{Q}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}-\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R_{Q}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R}},

which is chosen so that
\chi_{R_{Q}}\Delta_{2,R}g=c_{R,Q}(g)\chi_{R_{Q}}b_{2}
(recall that the cubes
R
that appear in the sum
S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}
are in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
). With this, we can write the first sum on the right-hand side of (
72
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle.

(75)

Our next strategy will be to bound each of the two sums on the right-hand side above by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Once we have done that, we will have proved that

|S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Let us start by taking care of the second term in (
75
), which we do in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
be very good. Then,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}|c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proof.

First, we will give bounds for the coefficients
c_{R,Q}(g)
and
|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|
separately. We have

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{2,R}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\geq\int_{R_{Q}}|\Delta_{2,R}g|^{2}\,d\mu=|c_{R,Q}(g)|^{2}\int_{R_{Q}}|b_{2}|^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\geq|c_{R,Q}(g)|^{2}|\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}|^{2}\mu(R_{Q})

\displaystyle\geq c_{\text{acc}}^{-2}|c_{R,Q}(g)|^{2}\mu(R_{Q}),

because the cube
R_{Q}
is transit. So, we get that

|c_{R,Q}(g)|\leq c\,\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mu(R_{Q})^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(76)

To estimate the terms
|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})|
, we will argue in a similar way to what we did in the proof of Lemma
3.4
. Denote by
z_{Q}
the center of
Q
. We have that for
y\in Q
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}
,

|z_{Q}-x|\approx|y-x|\geq c\,\ell(Q)\geq c\,|y-z_{Q}|.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f)(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)\,d\mu(y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int\left(\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)-\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,z_{Q})\right)\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)\,d\mu(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq c\int\frac{|y-z_{Q}|^{\eta}}{|x-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{|x-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Integrating this inequality, we find that

\displaystyle|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{|x-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}|b_{2}(x)|\,d\mu(x).

Now we use the fact that
b_{2}
is a bounded function and integrate over annuli to get that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\mathbb{R}_{Q}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{|x-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}|b_{2}(x)|\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq c_{b}\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{2^{k+1}R_{Q}\setminus 2^{k}R_{Q}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{|x-z_{Q}|^{n+\eta}}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}\mu(2R_{Q})}{\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R_{Q})^{n+\eta}}+c\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}\mu(2^{k+1}R_{Q})}{\ell(2^{k}R_{Q})^{n+\eta}},

(77)

where the first term on the right-hand side of the second line corresponds to
k=0
in the sum in the first line. Recall that by Lemma
2.17
, since
R_{Q}
is transit,
\mu(\lambda R_{Q})\leq c_{0}\,\ell(\lambda R_{Q})^{n}
for all
\lambda\geq 1
. Moreover, recall that by (
70
),

\text{dist}(Q,\partial R_{Q})\geq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R_{Q})^{1-\gamma}=2^{\gamma-1}\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}\geq 2^{\gamma-1}\ell(Q).

Using this, we can bound the right-hand side of (
77
) by

\displaystyle c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}\ell(R_{Q})^{n}}{(\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R_{Q})^{1-\gamma})^{n+\eta}}+c\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}\ell(2^{k+1}R_{Q})^{n}}{\ell(2^{k}R_{Q})^{n+\eta}}

\displaystyle=c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}+c\,2^{\eta}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\eta}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\eta}}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{1}{2^{k\eta}}

\displaystyle\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\left(1+\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}},

where in the last line we have used that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R_{q})
, as
Q
is contained in
R_{Q}
. This leads to the bound

|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|\leq c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Combining this with (
76
), we obtain

|c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Therefore, summing over the admissible cubes, if we write
Q\in I(R)
to indicate that
Q
is contained in one of the children of
R
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}|c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}(\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus R_{Q}}b_{2})\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R_{Q})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(78)

For each
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, we can bound, using Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}||\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\eta}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\eta}}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

If we split the sum in the last factor above according to the size of the cubes
Q
, we can bound it by an absolute constant. Indeed,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\eta}}{\mu(R_{Q})^{\eta}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\in I(R)\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\frac{\mu(Q)}{\mu(R_{Q})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{2^{d}-1}\frac{1}{\mu(R_{j})}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R_{j},\,\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\mu(Q)\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\leq c,

where we have used that the cubes
Q
appearing in the innermost sum are pairwise disjoint. This means that the sum on the right-hand side of (
78
) does not exceed, by Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle c\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\subset R\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\left(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\subset R}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By Lemma
2.21
, the first factor is bounded by
c\,\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, which means that the proof will be finished if we are able to show that the second factor, the one with the double sum, is bounded above by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. This is indeed the case, because we can write it as

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\supset Q}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg(\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2},R\supset Q\\
\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)\end{subarray}}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{-\frac{k\eta}{2}}\right)\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2},

where we have used, first, that for each
Q
and
k
, there is at most one
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
that contains
Q
and
\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)
, and lastly, Lemma
2.21
for
f
.

∎

Lastly, we need to bound the first term in (
75
). Namely, we should be able to prove that

\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle\Bigg|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

When estimating the previous terms, we always used the triangle inequality and we actually bounded the sum of the modulus of the terms. Now, we will not use this technique. Observe that the terms
\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle
do not depend on
R
. This lets us separate the previous sum in two, by considering

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\supset Q\\
\ell(R)>2^{m}\ell(Q)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\Bigg).

(79)

Let us now consider the function
\widetilde{g}=g-\Xi_{2}g
. Fix any good cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
with
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R^{0})
. Since

\frac{\langle\Xi_{2}g\rangle_{R_{Q}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}=\frac{1}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}\frac{1}{\mu(R_{Q})}\int_{R_{Q}}\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R^{0}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R^{0}}}b_{2}\,d\mu=\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R^{0}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R^{0}}}=\frac{\langle\Xi_{2}g\rangle_{R}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R}},

we have that

c_{R,Q}(\widetilde{g})=\frac{\langle\widetilde{g}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}-\frac{\langle\widetilde{g}\rangle_{R}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R}}=\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R_{Q}}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R_{Q}}}-\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R}}=c_{R,Q}(g).\\

Since
\langle\widetilde{g}\rangle_{R^{0}}=0
, by a telescoping argument we have that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\supset Q\\
\ell(R)>2^{m}\ell(Q)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:R\supset Q\\
\ell(R)>2^{m}\ell(Q)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(\widetilde{g})=\frac{\langle\widetilde{g}\rangle_{R(Q)}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R(Q)}},

(80)

where
R(Q)
denotes the smallest transit cube
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
such that
\ell(R)\geq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
. Such a cube always exists because
Q
is completely contained in
R^{0}
. Indeed, since
\ell(R^{0})\geq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
and
R^{0}
is transit, we have that

\text{dist}(Q,\partial R^{0})>\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}>0,

so
Q\cap\partial R^{0}=\varnothing
. Since
F\cap Q\neq\varnothing
,
Q
must be entirely contained within
R^{0}
. Also, in the special case where the smallest such cube
R(Q)
is precisely
R^{0}
, the identity (
80
) also holds, since both sides of the equality are zero. Hence, using the expression (
79
),

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\widetilde{g}\rangle_{R(Q)}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R(Q)}}\langle f,\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2})\rangle.

Let us write the sum above in a more compact form. For a function
\psi\in L^{2}(\mu)
such that
\Delta_{2,R}\psi=0
except for a finite number of cubes
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, we consider the following paraproduct,

\Pi_{K_{\Theta}^{*}b_{2}}(\psi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},Q\text{ good}\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R^{0})\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\psi\rangle_{R(Q)}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R(Q)}}\Delta_{1,Q}^{*}(K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}),

so that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle=\langle f,\Pi_{K_{\Theta}^{*}b_{2}}(\widetilde{g})\rangle.

(81)

Remark 3.10
.

The classes of functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\varphi\in L^{2}(\mu):\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\neq 0\,\text{ for finitely many }\,Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\psi\in L^{2}(\mu):\Delta_{2,R}\psi\neq 0\,\text{ for finitely many }\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\},

are both dense in
L^{2}(\mu)
. This is justified, in both cases, by Lemma
2.21
, which tells us that we can write, for
i=1,2
