, and any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
,

f=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{i}}\Delta_{i,Q}f,

with convergence in
L^{2}(\mu)
. This means that
f
is the limit in
L^{2}(\mu)
of functions of the form

f_{k}=\Xi_{i}f+\sum_{j\in I_{k}}\Delta_{i,Q_{j}}f,

where
I_{k}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{i}
is some finite subset. By the properties of
\Delta_{i,Q}
(d) and (e), from Lemma
2.20
, we have that

\Delta_{i,Q}f_{k}=\begin{cases}\Delta_{i,Q_{j}}f\quad&\text{ if }\,Q=Q_{j},\,\text{ for some }\,Q_{j}\in I_{k},\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

that is,
\Delta_{i,Q}f_{k}\neq 0
for finitely many
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},i}_{i}
, equivalently,
f_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
.

This last remark will be key in proving that the paraproduct that we have just defined extends to a bounded operator in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The paraproduct
\Pi_{K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}}
extends to a bounded operator in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{T}_{2}
. These subsets, by the previous remark, are dense in
L^{2}(\mu)
. Then, we have

\langle\varphi,\Pi_{K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}}(\psi)\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,Q\text{ good}\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\langle\psi\rangle_{R(Q)}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R(Q)}}\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K_{\Theta}^{*}b_{2}\rangle.

Of course, we can restrict the cubes
Q
that appear in the sum above so that
\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\not\equiv 0
. Then, we can write, using that
R(Q)
is transit,

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi,\Pi_{K_{\Theta}^{*}b_{2}}(\psi)\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{c_{\text{acc}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},Q\text{ good}\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R^{0})\\
\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\not\equiv 0\end{subarray}}|\langle\psi\rangle_{R(Q)}|\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle|}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{c_{\text{acc}}}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},Q\text{ good}\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R^{0})\\
\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\not\equiv 0\end{subarray}}|\langle\psi\rangle_{R(Q)}|^{2}\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle|^{2}}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(82)

Since
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mu)
, by Lemma
2.21
, we know that the last factor on the right-hand side above does not exceed
c\,\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, so to prove the lemma it suffices to show that the first factor is bounded by
c\,\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. To do so, we will denote, for
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
,

F(R)=\{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:Q\,\text{ is }1\text{-}\mathcal{D}_{1}\text{-good w.r.t. }\,\mathcal{D}_{2},\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\not\equiv 0,\,R(Q)=R\}.

With this notation, we can rewrite the first factor in (
82
) as

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}|\langle\psi\rangle_{R}|^{2}\sum_{Q\in F(R)}\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle|^{2}}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}},

which we want to bound by
c\,\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}
. By the dyadic Carleson embedding theorem
1.12
, it is enough to check that the coefficients

a_{R}:=\sum_{Q\in F(R)}\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle|^{2}}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}}

satisfy the condition (
14
). Since
\Delta_{1,Q}=\Delta^{2}_{1,Q}
, we have

a_{R}=\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle|^{2}}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}=\frac{|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi,\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2})\rangle|^{2}}{\|\Delta_{1,Q}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}\leq\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Observe that the families
F(R)
are not overlapping. This is due to the minimality of the side length of each
R(Q)
and the fact that the cubes
R
that we consider are from the same dyadic lattice. Moreover, since all the cubes from
F(R)
are contained in
R
, we have that for every
S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\,R\subset S}a_{R}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\,R\subset S}\sum_{Q\in F(R)}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset S}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(\chi_{S}K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

By (
51
), the last sum is bounded above by
c\,\|\chi_{S}K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Moreover, using that
\|K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq c_{4}
(recall Lemma
2.15
), we deduce that

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}:\,R\subset S}a_{R}\leq c\,\|\chi_{S}K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq c\,\mu(S),

which verifies the Carleson condition (
14
).
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

If
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
are as in Lemma
3.1
and very good, then we have

|S^{\text{tr}}_{4,1}|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proof.

As remarked before, from our previous work, the statement in the lemma is equivalent to proving the estimate

\Bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr,2}}_{2}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)<2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
R_{Q}\text{ transit}\end{subarray}}c_{R,Q}(g)\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}\rangle\Bigg|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Remember that by our definition of the numbers
c_{R,Q}(g)
, the expression on the left-hand side of the previous inequality is, as noted in (
81
),
|\langle f,\Pi_{K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}}(\widetilde{g})\rangle|
. Using that, by the previous lemma, the paraproduct is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, we obtain that

|\langle f,\Pi_{K^{*}_{\Theta}b_{2}}(\widetilde{g})\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\widetilde{g}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

because
\Xi_{2}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
.
∎

3.3.2
Estimate of
S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}

The sum
S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}
, which we defined in (
71
), can be rewritten, by changing
R_{Q}
to
R
and
R
to
\widehat{R}
(the parent of
R
), respectively, as

S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
Q\subset R\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\rangle,

(83)

where, to simplify the notation, we have omitted the condition that
\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, which we are still considering, since
\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}
has not been defined for terminal cubes.

For any cube
R
as in the sum above, we consider a Whitney decomposition of such a cube. That is, a family
W(R)
of cubes from
\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that

1.

the family covers
R
, that is
\displaystyle R\subseteq\bigcup_{Q\in W(R)}Q
,

2.

if
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in W(R)
, then either
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
or
Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}=\varnothing
,

3.

for every
S\in W(R)
,
\text{dist}(S,\partial R)=\ell(S)
,

4.

for every
S\in W(R)
,
3S\subset R
,

5.

the family of dilated cubes,
\{2S\}_{S\in W(R)}
has bounded overlap, that is, there exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that

\sum_{S\in W(R)}\chi_{2S}\leq c.

Moreover, for each
S\in W(R)
, we will consider the following two functions,

g_{R,S}=\chi_{2S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g,\qquad\widetilde{g}_{R,S}=\chi_{\widehat{R}\setminus 2S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g,

so that
\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g=g_{R,S}+\widetilde{g}_{R,S}
. With all this in mind, we can write, for every
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\rangle=\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g_{R,S}\rangle+\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\rangle.

The goal of this section is to provide a bound for each of the two sums above, after summing
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}
. We will do so in two lemmas, each one dealing with only one of the sums.

Lemma 3.13
.

If
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
are as in Lemma
3.1
, very good and
m
, from the definition of bad cubes, is chosen big enough, we have that

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proof.

For the sake of clarity, we will follow three distinct steps.

Step 1:
recall Lemma
3.4
, in which we considered
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
disjoint cubes and
\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}
two functions in
L^{2}(\mu)
supported on
Q
and
R
, respectively. If
\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq\ell(Q)
,
\int\varphi_{Q}=0
,
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
and for some constant
c_{6}>0
,

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\psi_{R}))\geq c_{6}\,\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma},

then

|\langle K_{\Theta}\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,R)^{n+\eta}}\|\varphi_{Q}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\psi_{R}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(84)

Of course, we would like to use the Lemma with
\varphi_{Q}=\Delta_{1,Q}f
and
\psi_{R}=\widetilde{g}_{R,S}
. We have that
\Delta_{1,Q}f
is supported in
Q
, it has zero integral, and
\widetilde{g}_{R,S}
is supported in
\widehat{R}
. The obstacle now is that
Q
and
\widehat{R}
are not disjoint. However, we claim, and will prove later, that in this situation we still have

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\widetilde{g}_{R,S}))\geq\text{dist}(Q,\partial 2S)\geq c\,\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(\widehat{R})^{1-\gamma},

(85)

which is what we illustrate in Figure
1
(in dimension
d=2
, for simplicity).

\widehat{R}
R
S
2S
z_{Q}
Q
\text{supp}(\widetilde{g}_{R,S})

Figure 1:
The cube
Q
and
\text{supp}(\widetilde{g}_{R,S})
are disjoint, whereas
Q
and
\widehat{R}
are not. Of course, the side length of
S
in this picture is bigger than it should be, that is,
\text{dist}(S,\partial R)
, because of property
3
from the Whitney decomposition. This is due to legibility reasons.

Going over the proof of Lemma
3.4
, one can see that the same arguments apply in this case. Hence, we obtain that

|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\rangle|\leq c\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(\widehat{R})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,\widehat{R})^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Step 2:
take the sum over the corresponding cubes
, also, using
\|\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\rangle|

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(\widehat{R})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,\widehat{R})^{n+\eta}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\|\widetilde{g}_{R,S}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq c\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\\
Q\subset R\end{subarray}}\frac{\ell(Q)^{\frac{\eta}{2}}\ell(\widehat{R})^{\frac{\eta}{2}}}{D(Q,\widehat{R})^{n+\eta}}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(\widehat{R})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Recall that
\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, so the right-hand side of the last inequality is bounded by the left-hand side of the first line in (
65
) from Lemma
3.6
. By that same lemma, it is bounded by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
.

Step 3:
proof of (
85
)
. First, we have that

\ell(Q)=\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(Q)^{1-\gamma}\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}2^{(1-\gamma)m}\,\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}.

Hence, choosing
m
big enough we have that

8\,\ell(Q)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}\leq\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R)\leq\ell(S),

where we have used that
Q
is good. Moreover, for any
x\in Q
and
y\in\partial 2S
, since
z_{Q}\in S
,

|x-y|\geq\|x-y\|_{\infty}\geq\|y-z_{Q}\|_{\infty}-\|z_{Q}-x\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{\ell(s)}{2}-\frac{\ell(Q)}{2},

from which we deduce that

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\widetilde{g}_{R,S}))\geq\text{dist}(Q,\partial 2S)\geq\frac{\ell(S)-\ell(Q)}{2}\geq\frac{\ell(S)}{4}\geq c\,\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(\widehat{R})^{1-\gamma},

which is (
85
).
∎

Remark that in this proof we have used properties 1 and 2 of the Whitney decomposition of
R
, namely that the cubes from this decomposition cover
R
and are pairwise disjoint. In the next Lemma, which will complete the proof of the bound for
S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}
and hence for
S_{4}
, we will use, in addition, properties 3, 4 and 5.

Lemma 3.14
.

If
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
are as in Lemma
3.1
, very good and
m
is chosen big enough, then

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),g_{R,S}\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and thus
|S^{\text{term}}_{4,1}|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
.

Proof.

As we have remarked before stating the Lemma, the second statement follows from the previous lemma and the first inequality, which we prove now.

By the second inequality in (
85
) from the previous lemma, if
Q
and
S\subset R
are cubes from the sum in the lemma and
z_{Q}\in S
, it turns out that
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(S)/8
and
Q\subset 2S
. Moreover, since
R
is terminal and
2S\subset R
, for any
y\in 2S
we have

\displaystyle\Theta(y)\geq\text{dist}(y,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}_{2}})\geq\text{dist}(y,\partial R)

\displaystyle\geq\text{dist}(2S,\partial R)

\displaystyle=\text{dist}(S,\partial R)-\frac{\ell(S)}{2}=\frac{\ell(S)}{2}.

Hence, for all
y\in 2S
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, recalling the pointwise estimate from Lemma
2.5
,

\left|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(y,x)\right|\leq\frac{c}{\Theta(y)^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\ell(S)^{n}}.

Hence,

\left|K_{\Theta}^{*}g_{R,S}(x)\right|\leq c\,\frac{\|g_{R,S}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}}{\ell(S)^{n}}\leq c\,\frac{\|f_{R,S}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mu(2S)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell(S)^{n}}.

(86)

Using that
\Delta^{2}_{1,Q}=\Delta_{1,Q}
, this leads us to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\left|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),g_{R,S}\rangle\right|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\left|\langle\Delta_{1,Q}f,\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K^{*}_{\Theta}(g_{R,S}))\right|

(87)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K_{\Theta}^{*}(g_{R,S}))\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By Remark
51
and using the inequality in (
86
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K_{\Theta}^{*}(g_{R,S}))\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(\chi_{2S}K^{*}_{\Theta}(g_{R,S}))\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\chi_{2S}K_{\Theta}^{*}(g_{R,S})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\frac{\|g_{R,S}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mu(2S)^{2}}{\ell(S)^{2n}}.

Note that if for a cube
2S
appearing in the inequality above there are no transit cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, the summand is zero. Hence, we can assume that any cube
2S
as above contains a transit cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
. By Lemma
2.17
, for such
Q
we have
\mu(\lambda Q)\leq c_{0}\,\ell(\lambda Q)^{n}
, for all
\lambda\geq 1
, which implies that
\mu(2S)\leq c\,\ell(2S)^{n}
(possibly with a different constant). Therefore, using this in the last inequality above,

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta^{*}_{1,Q}(K_{\Theta}^{*}(g_{R,S}))\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|g_{R,S}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Applying this in (
87
) and summing over
S\in W(R)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),g_{R,S}\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\,\sum_{S\in W(R)}\Bigg[\Bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|g_{R,S}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\Bigg(\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{S\in W(R)}\|g_{R,S}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Remember that our goal is to bound this quantity, after summing over
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}
, by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. We look at each of the two factors above separately. First, using that the cubes
2S
have bounded overlap,

\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset 2S}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset R}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and also,

\sum_{S\in W(R)}\|g_{R,S}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\sum_{S\in W(R)}\int_{2S}|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g|^{2}\,d\mu\leq c\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

which means that

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{S\in W(R)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
Q\subset R,\,z_{Q}\in S\\
\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),g_{R,S}\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\Bigg[\Bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset R}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\Bigg(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}:\,Q\subset R}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since the cubes
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}
with
\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
are clearly pairwise disjoint, we have, for the first factor on the right-hand side above,

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,Q\subset R}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Lastly, since each cube has
2^{d}
children and
\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
,

\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{R}}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 2^{d}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,2^{d}\,\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

thus concluding the proof.
∎

As a final remark for this section, let us highlight that, while the Whitney decomposition was key in the proof of the preceding Lemma
3.14
, the role of the suppressed kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}
and our hypothesis on the function
\Theta
were also essential. Indeed, combining all these, we were able to control

\left|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(y,x)\right|\leq\frac{c}{\ell(S)^{n}},\quad y\in 2S,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

which is the cornerstone of the argument used above. In contrast, the main inequality in Lemma
3.13
was

\text{dist}(Q,\text{supp}(\widetilde{g}_{R,S}))\geq c\,\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(\widehat{R})^{1-\gamma}.

This enabled us to apply a version of Lemma
3.4
, which depends not on the suppression of the kernel, but rather on the Calderón-Zygmund estimates that it satisfies.

3.4
Bound for
S_{2}+S_{3}

To finish the proof of Lemma
3.1
, recall that the two remaining terms from (
59
) are

S_{2}+S_{3}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R=\varnothing\\
Q,R\text{ not distant}\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle.

Remember that the functions
f
and
g
that we consider are supposed to be good, which means that the cubes
Q
and
R
that appear in the sums above are good. Then, by Remark
3.3
, the fact that the cubes
Q,R
in
S_{2}
are disjoint and not distant implies that
2^{-m}\ell(R)<\ell(Q)<2^{m}\ell(R)
. Hence, we can put together the two sums above and we get that

\displaystyle|S_{2}+S_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
Q,R\text{ not distant}\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
\text{dist}(Q,R)<\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R))\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|,

(88)

where in the last inequality we have used that if
Q,R
are not distant,

\text{dist}(Q,R)<\min(\ell(Q),\ell(R))^{\gamma}\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R))^{1-\gamma}\leq\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R)).

The term in (
88
) is exactly the bound that appears in the statement of Lemma
3.1
, thus finishing the proof.

3.5
Partial bound for the extra term for good functions

The aim of this section is to control the additional term that appeared in the bound for the lemma about good functions
3.1
, which we recall below,

A:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
\text{dist}(Q,R)<\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R))\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|.

(89)

Of course, we would like to find a bound of the type
A\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. However, this goal is overly optimistic. We are going to propose a splitting of
A
, similarly to what we did in (
59
), and we will encounter a term that would vanish if our operator were antisymmetric. Since this is not our case, we will have to introduce a probabilistic argument to deal with it, which will be done in Section
5.4
.

For now, notice that in the sum in (
89
), each cube
Q
can interact with a number of cubes
R
bounded above by some constant depending only on the dimension
d
and the number
m
. This is because we have control of the distance between
Q
and
R
and of the side length of
R
in terms of
2^{m}\ell(Q)
, which is what we illustrate in Figure
2
(for simplicity, in dimension
d=2
).

\text{dist}(Q,R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
\leq 2^{m+1}\ell(Q)
z_{Q}
Q
R

Figure 2:
Once we fix
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, the admissible cubes
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
cannot be too far away from it.

The same reasoning applies if instead of fixing
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, we fix
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
. Hence, by Cauchy-Schwarz and Lemma
2.21
, one way of proving the desired bound
A\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
would be to show that for all
Q
and
R
in the sum in (
88
),

|\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|\leq c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

We can separate the right hand side above according to the children of the cubes that appear,

\langle K_{\Theta}(\Delta_{1,Q}f),\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle=\sum_{P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q),\,S\in\mathcal{CH}(R)}\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P}\Delta_{Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{R}g\rangle.

For each pair
P,S
, we can separate further,

\displaystyle\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle+\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S\setminus P}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle.

(90)

Our strategy now will be to distinguish whether the cubes
P,S
appearing above are transit or terminal. In the first case, we will see that the first two terms can be bounded by
c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{2}\,\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{2}
, which, as we mentioned above, is enough for our purpose. In the second case, we will have to introduce a probabilistic argument. Let us first deal with the transit case.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
Q,R,P
and
S
be as in (
90
). If
P\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
and
S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}
, then

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{2}\,\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{2},

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S\setminus P}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{2}\,\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{2}.

Proof.

We will only prove the first inequality because of the symmetry of the conditions on
P
and
S
: they are both transit cubes, their side lengths are comparable and they are not too far away from each other.

First, note that if we assume that both
P
and
S
are transit, we can write

\displaystyle\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,Q}f

\displaystyle=c_{P}(f)b_{1},\qquad c_{P}(f)=\frac{\langle f\rangle_{P}}{\langle b_{1}\rangle_{P}}-\frac{\langle f\rangle_{Q}}{\langle b_{1}\rangle_{Q}},

(91)

\displaystyle\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g

\displaystyle=c_{S}(g)b_{2},\qquad c_{S}(g)=\frac{\langle g\rangle_{S}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{S}}-\frac{\langle g\rangle_{R}}{\langle b_{2}\rangle_{R}}.

Hence, the first term from (
90
) becomes

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\,c_{P}(f)\,b_{1}),\chi_{S}\,c_{S}\,b_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq c_{b}^{2}\,|c_{P}(f)|\,|c_{S}(g)|\int_{y\in P\setminus S}\bigg(\int_{x\in S}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\,d\mu(x)\bigg)\,d\mu(y).

(92)

Using the properties of the suppressed kernel, we see that we need to obtain a bound of the sort

I:=\int_{y\in P\setminus S}\bigg(\int_{x\in S}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}\,d\mu(x)\bigg)\,d\mu(y)\leq c\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(93)

Indeed, since the cube
P
is transit,

\mu(P)\leq c_{\text{acc}}\,|\nu_{1}(P)|=c_{\text{acc}}\bigg|\int_{P}b_{1}\,d\mu\,\bigg|\leq c_{\text{acc}}\,\|\chi_{P}\,b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}},

so we find that

|c_{P}(f)|\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq c_{\text{acc}}\,|c_{P}(f)|\,\|\chi_{P}\,b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=c_{\text{acc}}\,\|\chi_{P}\,\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{\text{acc}}\,\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

and the same holds for the coefficient
c_{S}(g)
and the function
\Delta_{2,R}g
. So, let us prove (
93
). Recall that since both
Q
and
R
are good cubes, we have that for any
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\mu\left(\left\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,\partial P)\leq\lambda\ell(P)\right\}\right)\leq\,\lambda\,M\,\mu(S),

(94)

\displaystyle\mu\left(\{x\in P:\text{dist}(x,\partial S)\leq\lambda\ell(S)\}\right)\leq\lambda\,M\,\mu(P).

(95)

We start with the inner integral in (
92
). Let
y\in P\setminus S
, then

\int_{x\in S}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x)\leq\int_{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq|x-y|\leq c^{\prime}\ell(S)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x).

(96)

Assume first that
\Theta(y)\leq\text{dist}(y,\partial S)
. We claim that the integral on the right-hand side above is bounded by
c\,\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)}\right)
, where
c
is not necessarily the same constant as in the domain of integration above. Indeed, we can integrate over annuli, calling
r=\text{dist}(y,\partial S)
and
R=c^{\prime}\ell(S)
, and we get that

\displaystyle\int_{r\leq|x-y|\leq R}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}d\mu(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\int_{2^{k}r\leq|x-y|\leq 2^{k+1}r}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}d\mu(x)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\mu(B(x,2^{k+1}r))}{(2^{k}r)^{n}},

(97)

where
N
is the biggest integer such that
2^{N}r\leq R
, from which we see that
N\leq\log_{2}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)
. Using our assumption,

\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\geq\Theta(y)\geq\text{dist}(y,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}),

so we see that
\mu(B(y,2^{k+1}r))\leq c_{0}(2^{k+1}r)^{n}
for each
k
, from which we get that the right hand side in the last inequality from (
97
) is bounded above by

c\left(\log_{2}\left(\frac{R}{r}\right)+1\right)\leq c\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)}\right),

as wished. Assume now that
\Theta(y)\geq\text{dist}(y,\partial S)
. In this case, we can separate the integral in (
96
) as

\displaystyle\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,x\in S,\\
&\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq|x-y|\crcr}}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,x\in S,\\
&\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq|x-y|\leq\Theta(y)\crcr}}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,x\in S,\\
&\Theta(y)<|x-y|\crcr}}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x):=J_{1}+J_{2}.

For
J_{1}
we have an easy bound,

J_{1}\leq\int_{|x-y|\leq\Theta(y)}\frac{1}{\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x)\leq\frac{\mu(B(y,\Theta(y)))}{\Theta(y)^{n}}\leq c_{0},

where we have used that, since
\Theta(y)\geq\text{dist}(y,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}})
, the ball appearing above is not contained in
H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
. For
J_{2}
, we can obtain, through analogous techniques, a similar bound to that obtained for the case that
\Theta(y)\leq\text{dist}(y,\partial S)
. Indeed,

\displaystyle J_{2}=\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr\,&x\in S,\\
&\Theta(y)<|x-y|\crcr}}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Theta(y)<|x-y|\leq c^{\prime}\ell(S)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\int_{2^{k}\Theta(y)<|x-y|\leq 2^{k+1}\Theta(y)}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\mu(B(y,2^{k+1}\Theta(y)))}{(2^{k}\Theta(y))^{n}}\leq(N+1)2^{n}c_{0}.

Now, the bound we obtain for
N
is slightly different than before, but we can retrieve the same bound easily,

N\leq\log_{2}\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\Theta(y)}\right)\leq c\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)}\right).

Hence, the integral on the left-hand side of (
93
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle c\int_{P\setminus S}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)}\right)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle=c\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,y\in P,\\
&2^{k-1}\ell(P)\leq\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq 2^{-k}\ell(P)\crcr}}}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(S)}{\text{dist}(y,\partial S)}\right)\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k\mu\left(\left\{y\in P:\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq 2^{-k}\ell(P)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k\mu\left(\left\{y\in P:\text{dist}(y,\partial S)\leq c\,2^{-k}\ell(S)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq cM\mu(P)\sum_{k\geq 0}k2^{-k}\leq cM\mu(P),

where we have used that
\ell(P)
and
\ell(S)
are comparable and the inequality (
95
), which we obtained from the fact that
Q=\widehat{P}
is good with respect to
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. However, this is not quite the bound that we were aiming for on the right-hand side of (
93
): we need both
\mu(P)
and
\mu(S)
to appear instead of only one of them. To obtain such a bound, we are going to use a similar strategy to what we have done now to estimate the same integral, but in terms of
\mu(S)
. Afterwards, we will take an average of these estimates, which will yield (
93
).

Let us write

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in S\cap P}\bigg(\int_{y\in P\setminus S}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\bigg)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{x\in S\setminus P}\bigg(\int_{y\in P\setminus S}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}\,d\mu(y)\bigg)\,d\mu(x):=J_{3}+J_{4}.

For
J_{3}
, via arguments analogous to those we used to obtain the first bound for
I
, we have that if
s\in S\cap P
, then

\int_{y\in P\setminus S}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(y)\leq c\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right).

Thus,

J_{3}\leq c\,\int_{S\cap P}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right)\,d\mu(x)\leq c\mu(S\cap P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{S\cap P}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right)^{2}\,d\mu(x)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using the small boundary condition, this time from (
94
), the last integral can be estimated by

\displaystyle\int_{S\cap P}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{S}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,y\in S,\\
&2^{-k-1}\ell(S)\leq\text{dist}(x,\partial P)\leq 2^{-k}\ell(S)\crcr}}}\log\left(\frac{c^{\prime}\ell(P)}{\text{dist}(x,\partial P)}\right)^{2}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{2}\mu\left(\left\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,\partial P)\leq 2^{-k}\ell(S)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{2}\mu\left(\left\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,\partial P)\leq c2^{-k}\ell(P)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq cM\mu(S)\sum_{k\geq 0}k^{2}2^{-k}\leq cM\mu(S).

So we see that
J_{3}\leq cM\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Let us turn our attention to
J_{4}
. Arguing analogously as before, one bounds the inner integral, for
x\in S\setminus P
, by the logarithmic term involving
\ell(P)
and
\text{dist}(x,\partial P)
. Afterwards, the small boundary condition (
94
) yields that
J_{4}\leq cM\mu(S)
.

Putting together all these estimates, we have found that both
I\leq cM\mu(P)
and
I\leq c\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}+c\mu(S)
. Distinguishing whether
\mu(P)\leq\mu(S)
or the converse inequality is true, we obtain the desired bound (
93
), thus finishing the proof.
∎

The last term form (
90
) turns out to be more complicated, because the small boundary condition is not helpful when dealing with the intersections. Assuming that both
P
and
S
are transit, we can write (see (
91
))

\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle=c_{P}(f)c_{S}(g)\,\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\,b_{1}),\chi_{P\cap S}\,b_{2}\rangle.

Note that if our operator
K_{\Theta}
were antisymmetric and instead of two functions
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
we were working with only one function,
b
, this problematic term would vanish.

Let us denote
\Delta=P\cap S
. Let
x_{\Delta}
be its center, so we can write it as

\Delta=x_{\Delta}+\prod_{j=1}^{d}\bigg[-\frac{a_{j}}{2},\frac{a_{j}}{2}\bigg],

where
a_{j}>0
for each
j=1,\dots,d
. Now, for a (small) fixed number
0<\varepsilon_{a}
, we denote
\ell_{\varepsilon_{a}}=\varepsilon_{a}\,\min(\ell(P),\ell(S))
and consider a smaller concentric hyperrectangle
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, defined by (see Figure
3
, which, for simplicity, illustrates the situation in dimension
d=2
)

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}=x_{\Delta}+\prod_{j=1}^{d}[-b_{j},b_{j}],\quad\text{where }\,b_{j}=\frac{a_{j}}{2}-\ell_{\varepsilon_{a}},

(see Figure
3
, which, for simplicity, illustrates the situation in dimension
d=2
).

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
P
S
\ell_{\varepsilon_{a}}
\ell_{\varepsilon_{a}}

Figure 3:
The set
\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
inside
\Delta
.

Now, we claim that for each
\Delta
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, there are numbers
\gamma_{j}\in[b_{j},b_{j}-\ell_{\varepsilon_{a}}/2]
such that the hyperrectangle

{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}:=x_{\Delta}+\prod_{j=1}^{d}[-\gamma_{j},\gamma_{j}],

satisfies that there is some
t_{\Delta,\varepsilon_{a}}>0
, depending on
\varepsilon_{a}
and
\Delta
, such that for any
\lambda>0
,

\mu\big(\big\{x\in\Delta:\text{dist}(x,\partial\,{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}})\leq\lambda\,\text{diam}({\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}\big)\big\}\big)\leq\,t_{\Delta,{\varepsilon_{a}}}\lambda\mu(\Delta).

(98)

The existence of such a hyperrectangle
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}\subset\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}\subset\Delta
is justified by the following lemma, which is proved by a simple modification of Lemma 9.43 in
[
15
]
. We omit the details.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a>0
. Let
t_{a}
be some constant big enough (depending only on
a
and
d
) and let
R\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any fixed hyperrectangle. Then, there exists a concentric hyperrectangle
R^{\prime}
with
R\subset R^{\prime}\subset aR
such that for any
\lambda>0
,

\mu\left(\left\{x\in aR:\text{dist}(x,\partial R^{\prime})\leq\lambda\text{diam}(R^{\prime})\right\}\right)\leq t_{a}\,\lambda\,\mu(aR).

With
{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
satisfying (
98
), we consider two more subsets of
\Delta
defined by the following relations. First, consider
\widetilde{P}
a cube, concentric with
P
and contained inside
P
, such that its boundary hyperplanes inside
\Delta
go along the boundary hyperplanes of
{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
. Then, we call
S_{\partial}:=\Delta\setminus\widetilde{P}
, see Figure
4
.

{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
\Delta
P
\widetilde{P}
S_{\partial}

Figure 4:
The definition of
S_{\partial}
.

Analogously, we consider another cube,
\widetilde{S}\subset S
, concentric with
S
, asking for the same condition concerning its boundary hyperplanes and those of
{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, and now we define
P_{\partial}:=\Delta\setminus\widetilde{S}
. Putting all this together, we can write

\Delta={\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}\cup P_{\partial}\cup S_{\partial},

where
S_{\partial}
and
P_{\partial}
are both disjoint from
{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, but
P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial}\neq\varnothing
(see Figure
5
).

\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
P
S
P_{\partial}
S_{\partial}

Figure 5:
The sets
P_{\partial},S_{\partial}
and
\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
inside
\Delta
.

This enables us to separate the additional term even further,

\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\,b_{1}),\chi_{P\cap S}\,b_{2}\rangle=\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle+\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle.

(99)

In the following lemma, we will obtain a bound for the first term, which will depend on
\varepsilon_{a}
. This dependence will cause no problems in the final argument, since we will fix
\varepsilon_{a}
and it will simply be a constant.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
P,S,Q,R
and
\Delta,\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
be as above. Then, we have,

|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|\leq c_{a}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where
c_{a}
is a constant that depends on
\varepsilon_{a}
and on the constant
\alpha
that we fixed in the last paragraph from Section
2.1
, but neither on
P
nor on
S
.

Remark 3.18
.

Before proving the lemma, we make two easy but useful observations. If
\Theta
is as in Lemma
3.1
, we have that

\sup_{r\geq 2\Theta(x)}\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}\leq c_{0},\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{\delta}(\nu_{1})(x)|\leq\alpha,

where
\alpha
is the number that we fix in the definition of the exceptional set
S
, in Section
2.1
.

To prove the first assertion, we have to use the property that
\Theta(x)\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}})
, and distinguish two cases.

•

If
\Theta(x)>0
, for
r>\Theta(x)
we will have that
r>\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}})
. Hence,
B(x,r)\not\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\subset\cap_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
. By hypothesis (a) from Theorem
2.1
, it follows that
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}
.

•

If
\Theta(x)=0
, we deduce that
\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}})
, so for any
r>0
,
B(x,r)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}})\neq\varnothing
and we have the same control for
\mu(B(x,r))
.

Now we turn our attention to the second assertion. We have to show that for any
\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)
, we have that
|T_{\delta}(\nu_{1})(x)|\leq\alpha
. Again, we will separate two cases. First, if
x\not\in S^{1}_{0}
, the inequality that we want to prove is clear. If
x\in S^{1}_{0}
, we can use the fact that
\Theta(x)\geq\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus S)\geq e_{1}(x)
. In this case, if
\Theta(x)=0
, then
e_{1}(x)=0
as well and so obviously
|T_{\delta}(\nu_{1})(x)|\leq\alpha
. If
\Theta(x)>0
, we have that
\delta>e_{1}(x)
, and so by definition of supremum it holds that
|T_{\delta}(\nu_{1})(x)|\leq\alpha
. Since this works for any
\delta\geq 2\Theta
, taking supremum we obtain the inequality above.

Proof of Lemma
3.17
.

First of all, note that it suffices to show that for all
x\in\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, we can bound

|K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}}.

(100)

Indeed, using this and the fact that both
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are bounded functions, we obtain

|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle|\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon_{a}^{n}}\mu(\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon_{a}^{n}}\mu(P\cap S).

Hence,

\displaystyle|c_{P}(f)c_{S}(g)\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta_{\varepsilon}}b_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}}|c_{P}(f)|\,|c_{S}(g)|\,\mu(P\cap S)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}}|c_{S}(g)|\,\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,|c_{P}(f)|\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where we have used, of course, that the cubes appearing above are all accretive. This is the inequality that we wanted to prove. It remains to show (
100
). Let
x\in\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, we have that

|K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)-T_{2\Theta(x)}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|\leq c\sup_{r\geq 2\Theta(x)}\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{r^{n}}<\infty.

The first inequality is proved with an analogous reasoning as what we argued in Lemma
2.12
, taking
\varepsilon=2\Theta(x)
. The fact that the supremum above is finite is the first part of the discussion in the previous remark. So, we obtain that

|K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|\leq|T_{2\Theta(x)}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|+c\leq\sup_{\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{\delta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|+c.

Now we focus on estimating the supremum on the right-hand side above. We can separate it by writing

\sup_{\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{\delta}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1})(x)|\leq\sup_{\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{\delta}(\nu_{1})(x)|+\sup_{\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{\delta}(\chi_{\Delta^{c}}\nu_{1})(x)|.

The first supremum on the right-hand side is finite (in fact, it is bounded above by
\alpha
) by the second part of the discussion in the previous remark. To finish the proof, we have to show that the second term is bounded by
c/\varepsilon^{n}_{a}
. Let
\delta>0
. By denoting
\delta^{\prime}=\text{diam}(\Delta)+\ell(P)
, we can separate

|T_{\delta}(\chi_{\Delta^{c}}\nu_{1})(x)|\leq|T_{\delta}(\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})\setminus\Delta}\nu_{1})(x)|+|T_{\delta}(\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})^{c}}\nu_{1})(x)|.

(101)

The second term above can be bounded by

\displaystyle|T_{\delta}(\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})^{c}}\nu_{1})(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{|x-y|\geq\delta}k(x,y)\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})^{c}}(y)\,d\nu_{1}(y)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{|x-y|\geq\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})}k(x,y)\,d\nu_{1}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|T_{\max(\delta,\delta^{\prime})}(\nu_{1})(x)\right|\leq\sup_{r\geq 2\Theta(x)}|T_{r}(\nu_{1})(x)|,

which we already know to be bounded above, by the previous remark. It only remains to give a bound for the first term on the right-hand side of (
101
). As usual, we will separate two cases and use an appropriate bound for each case.

Assume first that
\Theta(x)\leq\varepsilon_{a}\,\ell(P)
. Using that for
y\in\Delta^{c}
and
x\in\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
, we have that
|x-y|\geq c\,\varepsilon_{a}\ell(P)
, we obtain

\displaystyle|T_{\delta}(\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})\setminus\Delta}\nu_{1})(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x-y|\geq\delta}|k(x,y)||\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})\setminus\Delta}(y)|\,d|\nu_{1}|(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{(\varepsilon_{a}\ell(P))^{n}}|\nu_{1}|(B(x,\delta^{\prime}))\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon_{a}^{n}}\frac{\mu(B(x,\delta^{\prime}))}{(\delta^{\prime})^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}},

because on the one hand,
\ell(P)\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta^{\prime}
and on the other hand,
\delta^{\prime}\geq\ell\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{a}}\Theta(x)>2\Theta(x)
, by choosing
\varepsilon_{a}<\frac{1}{2}
.

Now, suppose that
\Theta(x)>\varepsilon_{a}\,\ell(P)
. In this case,
\delta^{\prime}\leq 2\,\ell(P)<\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{a}}\Theta(x)
. Hence, since
\delta\geq 2\Theta(x)
,

\displaystyle|T_{\delta}(\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})\setminus\Delta}\nu_{1})(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x-y|\geq\delta}|k(x,y)||\chi_{B(x,\delta^{\prime})\setminus\Delta}(y)|\,d|\nu_{1}|(y)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\frac{|\nu_{1}|(B(x,\delta^{\prime}))}{\delta^{n}}\leq\frac{c\mu(B(x,\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{a}}\Theta(x)))}{\varepsilon^{n}_{a}\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{a}}\Theta(x)\right)^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\varepsilon_{a}^{n}},

choosing
\varepsilon_{a}<\frac{1}{4}
, so that
\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\Theta(x)\geq 2\Theta(x)
and so our previous reasoning can apply.
∎

Let us put together all the estimates from this section. For the sake of readability, let us denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\{(Q,R)\in\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\text{tr},1}\times\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\text{tr},2}:\text{dist}(Q,R)<\max(\ell(Q),\ell(R)),\,2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\}.

Moreover, we can classify according to what type of cubes are the children of those
(Q,R)\in\mathcal{A}
, by letting

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\{(P,S)\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)\times\mathcal{CH}(R):(Q,R)\in\mathcal{A},\,P\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\,S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\},

and, similarly, we define
\mathcal{A}_{2,1}
if
P
is terminal and
S
is transit,
\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
for
P
transit and
S
terminal and
\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
for both
P
and
S
terminal. Combining Lemmas
3.15
and
3.17
, we have found that

\displaystyle|S_{2}+S_{3}|

\displaystyle\leq c_{a}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S\setminus P}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup A_{2,1}\cup A_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,1}}|\langle K_{\Theta}(\chi_{\Delta_{P,S}}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta_{P,S}\setminus(\Delta_{P,S})_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle=:c_{a}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle+A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}+A_{4},

(102)

where, of course, we denote
\Delta_{P,S}=P\cap S
for each of the cubes
P,S
appearing in the sum above. An upper bound for the sums
A_{i}
will be obtained via the probabilistic argument in Section
5.4
.

4
A Cotlar-type inequality

Recall that for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, its
n
-Ahlfors radius is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(x)

\displaystyle=\sup\{r>0:\mu(B(x,r))>c_{0}r^{n}\},

and we put
\mathcal{R}(x)=0
if there does not exist any
r>0
satisfying the condition that defines the set above. Moreover, we set

H=\bigcup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,\mathcal{R}(x)>0}B(x,\mathcal{R}(x)).

(103)

By assumption (a) from Theorem
2.1
, we have that
H\subset H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
for each
w\in\Omega
. In this section we are going to prove the following result, which will be key to finish the proof of Theorem
2.1
in the next section.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
T
be a Calderón-Zygmund operator of degree
n
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:x=y\}
with kernel
t(x,y)
satisfying

|t(x,y)|\leq\frac{1}{\max(\varepsilon,\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H),\text{dist}(y,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H))^{n}},\quad\text{for all }\,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(104)

Then, we have that

\|T_{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c(1+\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}),

where
c
depends only on
c_{0}
and on the Calderón-Zygmund constants of the kernel
t(x,y)
.

Remark 4.2
.

Since for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that
\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H)\geq\mathcal{R}(x)
. if the kernel
t(x,y)
satisfies the condition (
104
), then

|t(x,y)|\leq\frac{1}{\max(\varepsilon,\mathcal{R}(x),\mathcal{R}(y))^{n}}.

The proof of Lemma
4.1
will require a Cotlar-type inequality, which we state and prove below.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
T
be a singular integral operator satisfying the assumptions of Lemma
4.1
. Suppose that
T
is bounded from
L^{1}(\mu)
into
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
. Then, for every
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

T_{*}f(x)\leq c\,M_{\mu}(Tf)(x)+c\left(\|T\|_{L^{1}(\mu)\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}+1\right)M_{\mu}f(x),

where
c
depends only on
c_{0}
and on the Calderón-Zygmund constants of the kernel
t(x,y)
.

Using the boundedness of
M_{\mu}
in
L^{2}(\mu)
(recall Theorem
1.8
), to prove Lemma
4.1
, it suffices to apply the preceding result, after showing that

\|T\|_{L^{1}(\mu)\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}\leq c\left(\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+1\right).

(105)

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. By Theorem
1.9
(Cotlar’s inequality), we have that if
T
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
, and if

\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n},\quad\text{for }\ r\geq\varepsilon,

(106)

then

|T_{\varepsilon}\nu(x)|\leq cM_{\mu}(T\nu)(x)+c\left(\|T\|_{M(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}+1\right)M_{\mu}\nu(x),

for all
\nu\in M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If we assume that
T
, instead of being bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
into
L^{1,\infty}
, is of weak type (1,1) with respect to
\mu
, the same proof gives that for any
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
,

|T_{\varepsilon}f(x)|\leq c\,M_{\mu}(Tf)(x)+c\left(\|T\|_{L^{1}(\mu)\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}+1\right)M_{\mu}f(x).

(107)

So, in order to prove the lemma, we should be able to show that the preceding estimate also holds for our particular operator
T
without the growth condition (
106
). Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\mathcal{R}(x)=0
, then condition (
106
) holds and there is nothing to do. Hence, we can assume that
\mathcal{R}(x)>0
, and we have to show that (
107
) holds for any
0<\varepsilon<\mathcal{R}(x)
. To do this, we set

|T_{\varepsilon}f(x)|\leq|T_{\mathcal{R}(x)}f(x)|+\int_{0<|x-y|<\mathcal{R}(x)}|t(x,y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y).

The first term,
|T_{\mathcal{R}(x)}f(x)|
, is bounded by the right-hand side of (
107
). For the second term, we have to use the fact that
|t(x,y)|\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}(x)^{n}}
, and so

\displaystyle\int_{0<|x-y|<\mathcal{R}(x)}|t(x,y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{R}(x)^{n}}\int_{B(x,\mathcal{R}(x))}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{0}}{\mu(B(x,\mathcal{R}(x)))}\int_{B(x,\mathcal{R}(x))}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)\leq c_{0}\,M_{\mu}f(x),

where we have used that
\mu(B(x,\mathcal{R}(x)))\leq c_{0}\mathcal{R}(x)^{n}
. Thus, (
107
) holds for all
\varepsilon>0
in our case.
∎

The only task remaining to finish the proof of Lemma
4.1
is to prove (
105
), which is done in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
T
be a singular integral operator satisfying the assumptions of Lemma
4.1
. If
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, then it is also bounded from
L^{1}(\mu)
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mu)
. Moreover, we have

\|T\|_{L^{1}(\mu)\to L^{1,\infty}(\mu)}\leq c\left(\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+1\right).

Proof.

We have to show that for any
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
and
\lambda>0
,

\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|Tf(x)|>\lambda\})\leq c\left(\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+1\right)\frac{\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}}{\lambda}.

Clearly, we may assume that
\lambda>8\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}/\|\mu\|
, because otherwise the inequality above is obviously true.

Consider
\{Q_{i}\}_{i}
the Calderón-Zygmund decomposition at level
\lambda
, given by Lemma
1.6
, for the measure
\nu=f\mu
. Moreover, let
R_{i}
be the smallest cube of the of the form
R_{i}=6^{k}Q_{i},\,k\geq 1
, such that
\mu(6R_{i})\leq 6^{d+1}\mu(R_{i})
(recall Lemma
1.5
). Then, we write
f=g+b
, where

g=\chi_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}Q_{i}}f+\sum_{i}\varphi_{i},

and

b=\sum_{i}b_{i}:=\sum_{i}(w_{i}\,f-\varphi_{i}),

where the functions
\varphi_{i}
satisfy conditions (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
) of Lemma
1.6
, and
w_{i}=\frac{\chi_{Q_{i}}}{\sum_{k}\chi_{Q_{k}}}
.

Since each
Q_{i}
satisfies that
|f\mu|(Q_{i})>\frac{\lambda}{2^{d+1}}\mu(2Q_{i})
, we have that

\mu\bigg(\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}\bigg)\leq\sum_{i}\mu(2Q_{i})\leq\sum_{i}\frac{2^{d+1}}{\lambda}|f\mu|(Q_{i})\leq\frac{c}{\lambda}\sum_{i}\int_{Q_{i}}|f|\,d\mu\leq\frac{c}{\lambda}\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)},

because the cubes from the family
\{Q_{i}\}_{i}
are almost disjoint. So, it suffices to show that

\mu\bigg(\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}:|Tf(x)|>\lambda\bigg\}\bigg)\leq c\left(\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+1\right)\frac{\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}}{\lambda}.

(108)

Using the decomposition
f=g+b
, the right hand side of the inequality above does not exceed

\mu\bigg(\bigg\{\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|Tg(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg)+\mu\bigg(\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}:|Tb(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg).

(109)

To estimate the first term above, first we use that
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
and afterwards, that
|g|\leq c\lambda
, to get

\displaystyle\mu\bigg(\bigg\{\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|Tg(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mu\bigg(\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|Tg(x)|^{2}>\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}\bigg\}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|Tg|^{2}\,d\mu\leq\frac{4\|T\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g|^{2}\,d\mu.

Furthermore, again using the properties of the cubes from the Calderón-Zygmund decomposition,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g|\,d\mu\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}Q_{i}}|f|\,d\mu+\sum_{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi_{i}|\,d\mu\leq\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}+\sum_{i}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq c\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Hence,

\mu\bigg(\bigg\{\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|Tg(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg)\leq\frac{c\|T\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}}{\lambda}\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Let us examine now the second term in (
109
). By Chevishev, we have that

\displaystyle\mu\bigg(\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}:|Tb(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg)\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}}|Tb|\,d\mu\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}\sum_{i}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus 2Q_{i}}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu.

(110)

Now, we claim that for each
i
, the following inequality holds,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus 2Q_{i}}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(111)

Notice that from this claim and (
110
), we obtain that

\mu\bigg(\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{i}2Q_{i}:|Tb(x)|>\frac{\lambda}{2}\bigg\}\bigg)\leq\frac{c}{\lambda}\sum_{i}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\frac{c\,\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}}{\lambda}.

Let us prove (
111
). Denote by
x_{Q_{i}}
the center of the cube
Q_{i}
, and let
B_{i}=B(x_{Q_{i}},\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})/2)
. We can write

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus 2Q_{i}}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu\leq\int_{B_{i}}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu+\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(2R_{i}\cup B_{i})}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu.

(112)

We are now going to study each of the integrals on the right-hand side above separately. For the first one, notice that if
x\in B_{i}
, then
\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H)\geq\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})/2
, so

|t(x,y)|\leq\frac{2}{\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})^{n}}.

Thus, using that for each
i
, we have
\|b_{i}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}
(this is a direct consequence of the definition of
b_{i}
and the condition (
9
)), this yields

|Tb_{i}(x)|\leq\frac{2}{\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})^{n}}\|b_{i}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\frac{c}{\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})^{n}}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Lastly, using that
\mu(B_{i})\leq\mu(B(x_{Q_{i}},\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})))\leq c_{0}\,\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})^{n}
, we have that the first integral on the right-hand side of (
112
) is bounded by

\int_{B_{i}}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu\leq\frac{c}{\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})^{n}}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\mu(B_{i})\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Now we turn our attention to the second integral on the right-hand side of (
112
). Using (
9
), the
L^{2}(\mu)
boundedness of
T
and that
\mu(6R_{i})\leq 6^{d+1}\mu(R_{i})
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|T\varphi_{i}|\,d\mu\leq\left(\int_{2R_{i}}|T\varphi|^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(2R_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

(113)

Furthermore, since
\text{supp}(w_{i}f)\subset Q_{i}
, if
x\in 2R_{i}\setminus 2Q_{i}
and
y\in Q_{i}
, then
|x-x_{Q_{i}}|\leq c\,|x-y|
, so we have that

\displaystyle|T(w_{i}f)(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|t(x,y)|\,|w_{i}(y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)\leq\int_{Q_{i}}|t(x,y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n}}\int_{Q_{i}}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)=\frac{c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n}}.

Integrating this inequality,

\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|T(w_{i}f)|\,d\mu\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n}}\,d\mu(x).

(114)

By Lemma
1.7
, since by our choice of
R_{i}
, there are no
(6,6^{d+1})
-doubling cubes of the form
6^{k}Q_{i}
between
6Q_{i}
and
R_{i}
, we have

\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n}}\,d\mu(x)\leq c\frac{\mu(2R_{i})}{\ell(2R_{i})^{n}}.

Also, remark that if the integral above is non-zero, then we must have that
2R_{i}\not\subset B_{i}
, that is, if
\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})<\sqrt{d}\ell(2R_{i})
. Hence, by the definition of
\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})
, it must be that

\mu(2R_{i})\leq\mu\left(B(x_{Q_{i}},\sqrt{d}\,\ell(2R_{i}))\right)\leq c_{0}\left(\sqrt{d}\,\ell(2R_{i})\right)^{n},

so we can bound the integral above by

\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n}}\,d\mu(x)\leq c\,c_{0}\,d^{\frac{n}{2}}.

So, combining this with (
113
) and (
114
), we have that

\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu\leq\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|T(w_{i}\,f)|\,d\mu+\int_{2R_{i}\setminus(2Q_{i}\cup B_{i})}|T\varphi_{i}|\,d\mu\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Lastly, we estimate the rightmost integral in (
112
). We can use the fact that
\int b_{i}\,d\mu=0
,
\text{supp}(b_{i})\subset R_{i}
and
\|b_{i}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}
. This yields, for
x\not\in 2R_{i}
,

\displaystyle|Tb_{i}(x)|\leq\int|k(x,y)-k(x,x_{Q_{i}})|\,|b_{i}(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int c\,\frac{|x_{Q_{i}}-y|^{\eta}}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n+\eta}}\,|b_{i}(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c\,\ell(R_{i})^{\eta}}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n+\eta}}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}.

Integrating the last inequality,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(2R_{i}\cup B_{i})}|Tb_{i}|\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq c\,\ell(R_{i})^{\eta}\,\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus(2R_{i}\cup B_{i})}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n+\eta}}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\ell(R_{i})^{\eta}\|f\chi_{Q_{i}}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\int_{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|>\max(\ell(R_{i}),\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}}))}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n+\eta}}\,d\mu(x).

(115)

As usual, we can estimate the last integral splitting the domain of integration into annuli. Denoting
\alpha_{i}=\max(\ell(R_{i}),\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}}))
,

\int_{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|>\alpha_{i}}\frac{1}{|x-x_{Q_{i}}|^{n+\eta}}\,d\mu(x)\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\mu(B(x_{Q_{i}},2^{k+1}\alpha_{i}))}{(2^{k}\alpha_{i})^{n+\eta}}.

For each
k\geq 0
,
2^{k+1}\alpha_{i}\geq 2^{k}\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})\geq\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}})
, so we deduce that the sum above does not exceed

\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{c_{0}\,(2^{k+1}\alpha_{i})^{n}}{(2^{k}\alpha_{i})^{n+\eta}}=\frac{2^{n}\,c_{0}}{\max(\ell(R_{i}),\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{R}(x_{Q_{i}}))^{\eta}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{2^{k\eta}}\leq\frac{c}{\ell(R_{i})^{\eta}}.

Plugging this in (
115
), we have proved (
111
).
∎

5
A probabilistic argument

5.1
Low probability of bad cubes and functions

Let us remark that, up until now, all our efforts have been aimed towards estimating the behavior of the suppressed operators
K_{\Theta}
when they act on good functions. Recall that our motivation for obtaining such estimates, as mentioned in the beginning of Section
2.4
, was that the probability that a transit cube was bad with respect to a fixed dyadic lattice could be made arbitrarily small. The aim of this section is to, first, give a more precise statement of such claim and, afterwards, prove it.

Remember that for a transit cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, we say that it is bad with respect to the dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}_{2}
if either

(a)

there exists a cube
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
such that
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma}
and
2^{m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{N}
(where
m\geq 1
is some integer that we will fix below), or

(b)

there exists a transit cube
R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr}}_{2}
with
2^{-m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
and
\text{dist}(Q,R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)
, such that for some
P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q)
, there is
S\in\mathcal{CH}(R)
such that
\partial P
is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small.

Before estimating the probability that a given cube is bad with respect to another dyadic lattice, we need a couple of technical lemmas.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
Q\in\mathcal{D}(w_{1})=\mathcal{D}_{1}
with
w_{1}\in\Omega
and
k\geq m
be fixed. For
w_{2}\in\Omega
, let
R(w_{2})\in\mathcal{D}(w_{2})=\mathcal{D}_{2}
be the cube with side length
2^{k}\ell(Q)
containing the center
z_{Q}
. Suppose that
2^{k}\ell(Q)\leq 2^{N}
. Denote by
z_{R(w_{2})}
the center of
R(w_{2})
. Then, for any subset
A\subset 2^{k}Q
,

P^{\Omega}\left(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\}\right)\leq c\,\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(A)}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(2^{k}Q)}.

Proof.

Before proving the inequality in the statement, let us remark that
z_{R(w_{2})}
always belongs to
\overline{2^{k}Q}
. This is because
z_{Q}\in R(w_{2})
and so we have that

\|z_{Q}-z_{R(w_{2})}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\ell(R(w_{2}))}{2}=\frac{2^{k}}{2}\ell(Q)=\frac{\ell(2^{k}Q)}{2}.

Now, let us recall some useful information concerning the set
\Omega
, which was defined in Section
2.3.1
. Remember that we called
S^{0}=[0,2^{N}]^{d}
and we assumed that
F\subset\frac{1}{8}S^{0}
. Then,
\Omega
is defined as

\Omega=[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]^{d},

and for
w\in\Omega
, we write
Q^{0}(w)=w+S^{0}
. By Lemma
2.16
, since
\ell(\Omega)=2^{N-3}
, for each
w\in\Omega
we have that
F\subset\frac{1}{4}Q^{0}(w)
. Lastly, we denote by
P^{\Omega}
the normalized Lebesgue measure on the cube
\Omega
. We see that
\ell(\Omega)=2^{N-3}
could be smaller than
2^{k}\ell(Q)
. To fix this imbalance, we consider
\Omega^{\prime}:=[-2^{N-1},2^{N-1}]^{d}
, and we write

\displaystyle P^{\Omega}\left(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\})}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{3d}\,\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega^{\prime}:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\})}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega^{\prime})},

(116)

because
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega^{\prime})=2^{3d}\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)
. To estimate the measure above, we can think of the assignation
w_{2}\mapsto z_{R(w_{2})}
as a map defined not only on
\Omega
, but for
w_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\text{z}\,\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to 2^{k}Q
, i.e.
\text{z}(w_{2})=z_{R(w_{2})}
. Using this, we can write

\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega^{\prime}:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\})=\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega^{\prime}\cap\text{z}^{-1}(A)).

Let us now study the pre-image
\text{z}^{-1}(A)
. First, a simple computation shows that if we write, using coordinates,
z_{Q}=(z_{Q}^{1},\dots,z_{Q}^{d})
and
w_{2}=(w_{2}^{1},\dots,w_{2}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\text{z}(w_{2})

\displaystyle=w_{2}+2^{k}\ell(Q)\left(\left\lfloor\frac{z_{Q}^{1}-w_{2}^{1}}{2^{k}\ell(Q)}\right\rfloor,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{z_{Q}^{d}-w_{2}^{d}}{2^{k}\ell(Q)}\right\rfloor\right)+\frac{2^{k}\ell(Q)}{2}(1,\dots,1)

\displaystyle=w_{2}+2^{k}\ell(Q)(n_{1}(w_{2}),\dots,n_{d}(w_{2}))+2^{k-1}\ell(Q)(1,\dots,1).

Hence,

\displaystyle\text{z}^{-1}(A)

\displaystyle=\left\{w_{2}:2^{k}\ell(Q)(n_{1}(w_{2}),\dots,n_{d}(w_{2}))+2^{k-1}\ell(Q)(1,\dots,1)\in A\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(A-2^{k}\ell(Q)(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})-2^{k-1}\ell(Q)(1,\dots,1)\right),

and the sets appearing in the last union are pairwise disjoint. We are interested in taking their intersection with
\Omega^{\prime}
. Given any
y\in 2^{k}Q
, we have that
y=\text{z}(w_{2})
for some
w_{2}\in\Omega^{\prime}
if and only if there exist
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

y-2^{k}\ell(Q)(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})-2^{k-1}\ell(Q)(1,\dots,1)\in\Omega^{\prime}.

Since
\ell(2^{k}Q)=2^{k}\ell(Q)\leq 2^{N}=\ell(\Omega^{\prime})
, it is clear that we can always choose such integers
n_{1},\dots,n_{d}
(possibly there is more than one choice). Write
\ell(R(w_{2}))=2^{j}
, with
j\leq N
. Then, we can divide
\Omega^{\prime}
into
\alpha=(2^{N-j})^{d}
cubes of side length
2^{j}
. In this case, the image of each of these cubes via the function
z
is
2^{k}Q
, which means that

\text{z}^{-1}(A)\cap\Omega^{\prime}=\bigcup_{l=1}^{\alpha}\left(A-2^{k}\ell(Q)(n_{1}^{l},\dots,n_{d}^{l})-2^{k-1}\ell(Q)(1,\dots,1)\right):=\bigcup_{l=1}^{\alpha}A_{l}.

Since the sets
\{A_{l}\}_{l=1}^{\alpha}
are pairwise disjoint translates of
A
, we find that

\mathcal{L}^{d}(\text{z}^{-1}(A)\cap\Omega^{\prime})=\sum_{l=1}^{\alpha}\mathcal{L}^{d}(A_{l})=\alpha\,\mathcal{L}^{d}(A).

Taking
A=2^{k}Q
, we obtain
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\text{z}^{-1}(A)\cap\Omega^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega^{\prime})=\alpha\,\mathcal{L}^{d}(2^{k}Q)
. Using this in (
116
), we obtain

P^{\Omega}\left(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:z_{R(w_{2})}\in A\}\right)\leq 2^{3d}\frac{\alpha\,\mathcal{L}^{d}(A)}{\alpha\,\mathcal{L}^{d}(2^{k}Q)}=c\,\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(A)}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(2^{k}Q)},

which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
Q,k
be as in Lemma
5.1
. Using the same notation as in the lemma, the following are equivalent,

(1)

there exists some
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
with side length
2^{k}\ell(Q)
such that
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R^{\prime})\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R^{\prime})^{1-\gamma}
,

(2)

z_{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial R(w_{2}))\cap R(w_{2})}
, where
s=\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\ell(Q)
,

(3)

z_{R(w_{2})}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial(2^{k}Q))\cap 2^{k}Q}
.

Proof.

We start by showing that
(2)
implies
(1)
. Assume that
z_{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial R(w_{2}))\cap R(w_{2})}
. We can use that

\text{dist}(Q,\partial R(w_{2}))\leq\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R(w_{2}))\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}.

Hence, there is
R^{\prime}
some neighbor of
R(w_{2})
such that

\text{dist}(Q,\partial R^{\prime})=\text{dist}(Q,\partial R(w_{2}))\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}=\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R^{\prime})^{1-\gamma},

so we can take
R^{\prime}
to be the cube from
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
appearing in condition
(1)
.

In the converse direction, assume that there is
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
with
\ell(R^{\prime})=2^{k}\ell(Q)
such that
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R^{\prime})\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R^{\prime})^{1-\gamma}
. To check that
(2)
holds, since we already know that
z_{Q}\in\overline{R(w_{2})}
, we only need to check that
\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R(w_{2}))\leq s
.

There are two possibilities for the cube
R^{\prime}
. Either
R^{\prime}=R(w_{2})
or it is a neighbor of
R(w_{2})
. In either case,
\text{dist}(Q,\partial R^{\prime})=\text{dist}(Q,\partial R(w_{2}))\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}
. Since our cubes have sides parallel to the coordinate axes, we find that

\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R(w_{2}))=\text{dist}(Q,\partial R(w_{2}))+\frac{1}{2}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}+\frac{1}{2}\ell(Q)=s,

which means that
(2)
holds. Lastly, to show that
(2)
and
(3)
are equivalent, we will show that they are both equivalent to a fourth condition,

(4)\quad 2^{k-1}\ell(Q)-s\leq\|z_{Q}-z_{R(w_{2})}\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{k-1}\ell(Q).

Assume that
(2)
holds. In particular,
z_{Q}\in\overline{R(w_{2})}
, so
\|z_{Q}-z_{R(w_{2})}\|_{\infty}=2^{k-1}\ell(Q)
. Since
z_{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial R(w_{2}))}
, we can choose
x\in\partial R(w_{2})
such that
\|x-z_{Q}\|_{\infty}\leq s
. Using this auxiliary point,

\displaystyle\|z_{Q}-z_{R(w_{2})}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\|z_{R(w_{2})}-x\|_{\infty}-\|x-z_{Q}\|_{\infty}\geq 2^{k-1}\ell(Q)-s.

Conversely, assume that
(4)
holds. Since by construction
z_{Q}\in R(w_{2})
, we only have to check that
\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R(w_{2}))\leq s
. Again, since the sides of our cubes are parallel to the axes,

\text{dist}(z_{Q},\partial R(w_{2}))=2^{k-1}\ell(Q)-\|z_{R(w_{2})}-z_{Q}\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{k-1}\ell(Q)-2^{k-1}\ell(Q)+s=s.

Lastly, to see that
(3)
is equivalent to
(4)
, we argue as above, taking
x\in\partial(2^{k}Q)
instead of
x\in\partial R(w_{2})
, and the arguments are analogous.
∎

The following lemma is the main point of this section. It assures us that we can choose
m
and
M
in the definition of bad cubes so that the probability that a cube is bad is as small as we want.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
0<\varepsilon_{b}<1
be fixed. Suppose that the constants
m
and
M
are big enough, depending only on
\varepsilon_{b}
, in the definition of bad cubes. Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
, with
w_{1}\in\Omega
, be any fixed dyadic lattice. Then, for each fixed
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, we have that

P^{\Omega}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:Q\,\text{ is bad with respect to }\,\mathcal{D}(w_{2})=\mathcal{D}_{2}\})\leq\varepsilon_{b}.

(117)

Proof.

Fix
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
. Since it is bad if either (a) or (b) above in the definition happens, the probability in (
117
) is controlled by

P^{\Omega}(\{Q\,\text{is bad w.r.t.}\mathcal{D}_{2}\,\text{due to (a)}\})+P^{\Omega}(\{Q\,\text{is bad w.r.t.}\mathcal{D}_{2}\,\text{due to (b)}\}):=P_{{\text{(a)}}}+P_{{\text{(b)}}}.

Our aim now is to choose the constants
m
and
M
so that each of the two probabilities above is small. Note that condition (a) only takes into account the integer
m
, while condition (b) depends on both
m
and
M
. Due to this, our strategy will be to make
P_{\text{(a)}}
small by choosing
m
big enough, and for a fixed value of
m
, choose
M
big enough so that
P_{\text{(b)}}
is also small.

Choice of

m
:
we start by estimating

P_{\text{(a)}}=P^{\Omega}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}_{2},\,\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma},\,2^{m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{N}\}).

For each
k\geq m
, by Lemmas
5.2
and
5.1
, we have that

\displaystyle P^{\Omega}\left(\left\{\omega_{2}\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}_{2},\text{dist}(Q,\partial R)\leq\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R)^{1-\gamma},\ell(R)=2^{k}\ell(Q)\right\}\right)

(118)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=P^{\Omega}\left(\left\{w_{2}\in\Omega:z_{R(w_{2})}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial(2^{k}Q))\cap 2^{k}Q}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\,\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\overline{\mathcal{U}_{s}(\partial(2^{k}Q))\cap 2^{k}Q)})}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(2^{k}Q)}\leq c\,\frac{s^{d-1}\,2^{k}\ell(Q)}{(2^{k}\ell(Q))^{d}}.

Since
s\leq 2\ell(Q)^{\gamma}\ell(R(w_{2}))^{1-\gamma}=2^{k(1-\gamma)+1}\ell(Q)
, we have that the probability in (
118
) is bounded above by

c\,\frac{(\ell(Q)2^{k(1-\gamma)+1})^{d-1}2^{k}\ell(Q)}{(2^{k}\ell(Q))^{d}}=c\,2^{-k\gamma(d-1)}.

This means that

P_{\text{(a)}}\leq\sum_{k\geq m}c\,2^{-k\gamma(d-1)}=c\,\frac{2^{-m\gamma(d-1)}}{1-2^{-\gamma(d-1)}}\xrightarrow{m\to+\infty}0,

so we choose
m
so that
P_{\text{(a)}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2}
.

Choice of
M
:
we write

\displaystyle P_{\text{(b)}}=P^{\Omega}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:\,

\displaystyle\exists R\in\mathcal{D}(w_{2}),2^{-m}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q),\text{dist}(Q,R)\leq 2^{m}\ell(Q)

\displaystyle\text{and for some }P\in\mathcal{CH}(Q),\text{ there is }S\in\mathcal{CH}(R)\text{ such that }\partial P

\displaystyle{\text{is not }(\mu,M,S)}\text{-small}\}).

By writing
Q=\cup_{i=1}^{2^{d}}P_{i}
as the union of its children, we have that the probability above is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2^{d}}P^{\Omega}(\{w_{2}\in\Omega:\,

\displaystyle\exists S\in\mathcal{D}(w_{2}),2^{-m-1}\ell(Q)\leq\ell(S)\leq\min(2^{m}\ell(Q),2^{N}),

\displaystyle\text{dist}(P_{i},S)\leq 2^{m+2}\ell(Q)\text{ such that }\partial P_{i}\text{ is not }(\mu,M,S)\text{-small}\}).

(119)

Let us write the sum above as
\sum_{i=1}^{2^{d}}p_{i}
, where for each
i
,
p_{i}
denotes the probability associated to the
i
-th child of
Q
above. Since there is no canonical ordering of
\mathcal{CH}(Q)
, we will only estimate
p_{1}
and obtain a bound that does not depend on that specific child of
Q
. For convenience, we will denote from now on
P:=P_{1}
. Note that the boundary of
P
is the union of
2d

d-1
dimensional cubes, its “faces”, that is,

\partial P=\bigcup_{i=1}^{2d}H_{i}(P),

where each
H_{i}(P)
is contained in a hyperplane from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since

\mu\left(\left\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,\partial P)\leq\lambda\ell(P)\right\}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\mu\left(\left\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,H_{i}(P))\leq\lambda\ell(P)\right\}\right),

we see that if
\partial P
is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small, there is some
H_{i_{0}}(P)
that is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small. This is the essential observation in order to show that
P_{\text{(b)}}
can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
M
large enough. Our strategy will be to show that, for a fixed
S
satisfying (
119
), there are very few
H_{i}(P)
that are not
(\mu,M,S)
-small. This will be enough to complete our estimate, because using the fact that both
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
are translates of the same dyadic lattice, fixing
Q
and looking for
S
as in (
119
) is the same as fixing
S
and looking for a suitable
Q
.

Since our dyadic cubes have sides parallel to the coordinate axes, the directions that are normal to their faces are those given by the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which we denote by
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
. It is enough to show that there are very few
H_{i}(P)
with normal direction
e_{1}
.

Consider all the hyperplanes from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with normal direction
e_{1}
such that they intersect
S
, and call this family
H
. Consider
\sigma
the projection of
\mu\lfloor S
into the line
L_{1}
of direction
e_{1}
. That is, for
E\subset L_{1}
a measurable set, if
\pi\,\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the projection into the first coordinate,

\sigma(A)=\mu\lfloor S(\pi^{-1}(E))=\mu(S\cap\pi^{-1}(E))=\mu(\{x\in S:x_{1}\in E\}).

The key observation now is that for a hyperplane
h\in H
,

\displaystyle\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,h)\leq\lambda\ell(P)\}

\displaystyle=\{x\in S:|x_{1}-x_{h}|\leq\lambda\ell(P)\}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}([x_{h}-\lambda\ell(P),x_{h}+\lambda\ell(P)])\cap S,

where we have denoted
\pi(h)=x_{h}
. From this, we see that

\mu\left(\{x\in S:\text{dist}(x,h)\leq\lambda\ell(P)\}\right)=\sigma(\{y\in L_{1}:|y-x_{h}|\leq\lambda\ell(P)\}).

Hence, if the hyperplane
h\in H
is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small, then the point
x_{h}\in L_{1}
satisfies that there is
\lambda>0
such that

\sigma(\{y\in L_{1}:|y-x_{h}|\leq\lambda\ell(P)\})>\lambda M\mu(S).

This means that
h
is not
(\mu,M,S)
-small if and only if

M_{R}\sigma(x_{h}):=\sup_{r>0}\frac{\sigma(B(x_{h},r))}{r}>\frac{M\mu(S)}{\ell(P)}\geq\frac{c}{2^{N}}M\mu(S)=cM\mu(S),

because we always only consider cubes with side length bounded above by
2^{N}
, which we absorb inside the constant
c
. Recall that the maximal operator
M_{R}
is bounded from
M(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{1}\left(\left\{x:\exists\,h\subset\pi^{-1}(\{x\})\text{ not }(\mu,M,S)\text{-small}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}^{1}\left(\left\{x:M_{R}\sigma(x)>cM\mu(S)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A}{cM\mu(S)}\|\sigma\|\leq\frac{A}{cM}.

This reasoning works fixing any other of the
e_{i}
as the normal direction to our hyperplanes. We will use the same estimate for the sum of all of them, since there are
d
possibilities for
e_{i}
.

Let us call
SH
the set of hyperplanes, parallel to one of the faces of
S
, intersecting
S
and such that they are not
(\mu,M,S)
-thin. We can consider on each of them the projection of the Lebesgue measure onto the first coordinate, and from our reasoning above, we have that the measure of
SH
is bounded above by
\frac{A}{cM}
.

By periodicity of the dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}_{1}
at the fixed scale
\ell(S)
, the probability with respect to
P^{\Omega}
that there is
P\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
satisfying the condition from (
119
) has one of its faces in
SH
is, at most,
\frac{A}{cM}
. However, we also need to take into account the cubes
P
satisfying
2^{-m-1}\ell(R)\leq\ell(P)\leq 2^{m+1}\ell(R)
. Again by periodicity of the lattices, for
\ell(P)>\ell(R)
, we have the same bound for the probability. For the smaller cubes, the bound is
\frac{2^{m+1}A}{cM}
. If we add the probabilities over all the possible sizes, we obtain that

P_{\text{(b)}}\leq\frac{2^{2m+2}A}{cM}.

Since
m
is fixed from the estimate of
P_{\text{(a)}}
, we can always choose
M
large enough so that
P_{\text{(b)}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{b}}{2}
.
∎

Using the previous lemma, we are going to obtain a useful estimate of the expected value of the norm in
L^{2}(\mu)
of the bad part of a function. We give the precise statement in the lemma below.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. For
\text{w}=(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega^{2}
, set

f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{bad}(w_{2})}\Delta_{1,Q}f,

where “
Q\in\text{bad}(w_{2})
” means that
Q
is bad with respect to
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
. Then, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\|f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)\leq c_{3}^{2}\,\varepsilon_{b}\,\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where we have denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}
the expectation with respect to
P^{\Omega^{2}}=P^{\Omega}\times P^{\Omega}
.

Proof.

From the decomposition in
L^{2}(\mu)
, Lemma
2.21
, we have that
