\|f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{3}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{bad}(w_{2})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Then, using the preceding lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\big(\|f_{\text{bad}}(

\displaystyle\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\big)\leq c_{3}\int\bigg(\int\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{bad}(w_{2})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{2})\bigg)\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{1})

\displaystyle=c_{3}\int\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg(\int\chi_{\{Q\in\text{bad}(w_{2})\}}(w_{2})\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{2})\bigg)\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{2})

\displaystyle=c_{3}\int\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\,P^{\Omega}(\{w_{2}:Q\in\text{bad}(w_{2})\})\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{1})

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\,\varepsilon_{b}\int\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{1})\leq c_{3}^{2}\,\varepsilon_{b}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where in the last inequality we have used Lemma
2.21
again.
∎

The estimate above will be key in the final argument, where we will take an average over the set
\Omega
.

Recall now that we obtained a partial bound for
|S_{2}+S_{3}|
in terms of the sums
A_{i}
in (
89
). We mentioned that the full bound would have to wait until the last steps, since it will involve a probabilistic argument. Now, we introduce some new concepts, which will be the correct tools to deal with the additional term that we found when trying to bound
|S_{2}+S_{3}|
.

For a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varepsilon>0
, we denote
\delta_{Q}=Q\setminus(1-2\varepsilon)Q
. That is,
\delta_{Q}
consists of the points from
Q
that are at distance greater than
\varepsilon
from the center of
Q
, with respect to the distance induced by the
\infty
-norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Figure
6
).

z_{Q}
\ell(Q)
\ \varepsilon\ell(Q)

Figure 6:
In dimension
d=2
, the set
\delta_{Q}
is shaded in blue.

Now, fix any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. We denote by
p_{\varepsilon}
the probability, with respect to
w\in\Omega
, that
x\in\delta_{R}
for some
R\in\mathcal{D}(w)^{\text{tr},2}
and
2^{k-m}\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{k+m}
, that is,

p_{\varepsilon}=\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}(w)^{\text{tr},2},\,2^{k-m}\leq\ell(R)\leq 2^{k+m},\,x\in\delta_{R}\})}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}.

(120)

Let us now show how
p_{\varepsilon}
behaves as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\varepsilon>0
, define
p_{\varepsilon}
as above. Then,
p_{\varepsilon}
has limit
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, independently of
x
and
k
.

Proof.

Our strategy will be to reduce the problem to the one-dimensional case, where cubes will be intervals and for an interval
I
the set
I_{\delta}
will be two smaller intervals sharing one endpoint each with the bigger one
I
. In this simpler setting, we will be able to compute the set we are measuring in the numerator in (
120
).

If we denote by
\pi_{j}
the projection into the
j
-th coordinate, that is,
\pi_{j}(x)=x_{j}
, we obviously have that

\displaystyle p_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}(w),\,2^{k-m}\leq\ell(R)\leq\min(2^{k+m},2^{N}),\,x\in\delta_{R}\})}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}\sum_{j=k-m}^{\min(k+m,2^{N})}\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}(w),\ell(R)=2^{j},x\in\delta_{R}\})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}\sum_{j=k-m}^{\min(k+m,2^{N})}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{w\in\Omega:\exists R\in\mathcal{D}(w),\ell(R)=2^{j},x_{i}\in\delta_{\pi_{i}(R)}\})

\displaystyle=:\frac{1}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega)}\sum_{j=k-m}^{\min(k+m,2^{N})}\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{\varepsilon}^{i,j}.

It suffices to find a common bound, in terms of
\varepsilon
, for each
p_{\varepsilon}^{i}
, since
\Omega
is a fixed cube and there are always
2md
terms in the sum above, independently of
k
and of
x
. We will only estimate
p_{\varepsilon}^{1,j}
, because all the others are analogous. We consider
y=x_{1}\text{ mod }2^{j}
, so that
y\in[0,2^{j})
. If we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}
the usual dyadic intervals of side length
2^{j}
in the real line, we have that for
R\in\mathcal{D}(w)
,
\pi_{1}(R)\in\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+\pi_{1}(w)
. This means that

p_{\varepsilon}^{1,j}=\mathcal{L}^{d-1}([-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}])\cdot\mathcal{L}^{1}(\{w_{1}\in[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]:\exists\,I\in\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+w_{1},\,x_{1}\in\delta_{I}\}).

(121)

Clearly, the measure appearing in the first factor is
(2^{N-3})^{d-1}
. Our goal now is to describe the set appearing in the second factor. For this, we will distinguish three cases. We start with the simplest of the three: when
j=N-4
. We assume that
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
and consider an open interval of length
2^{N-3}\,\varepsilon
centered at
y
, which we call
J
.

Assume first that this interval is completely contained inside
[0,2^{N-4}]
. We can extend it periodically with period
2^{N-4}
, that is, we consider the intervals
J_{k}=J+k\,2^{N-4}
, with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Obviously, there are only two which have non-empty intersection with
[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]
, namely,
J_{0}=J
and
J_{-1}
(see Figure
7
).

0
2^{N-4}
-2^{N-4}
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}y}
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}J=J_{0}}
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}J}{{}_{-1}}

Figure 7:
The point
y=x_{1}
mod
2^{j}
and the intervals
J,J_{-1}
.

We claim that, up to the set of measure zero consisting of the
4
endpoints of the intervals, the set appearing in the second factor in (
121
) is
J_{-1}\cup J
. Indeed, for any
t\in J_{-1}\cup J
, there is a unique
J^{t}\in\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+t
containing
y
, which will be separated no less than
2^{N-3}\varepsilon
from one of its endpoints (see Figure
8
).

0
2^{N-4}
-2^{N-4}
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}y}

2^{N-4}

{\color[rgb]{0.91,0.57,0.95}t}

Figure 8:
J_{t}
is the dyadic interval from
\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+t
that contains
x
.

This means that in this case we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{1}(\{w_{1}\in[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]:\exists\,I\in\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+w_{1},\,x_{1}\in\delta_{I}\})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{1}(J_{-1}\cup J_{0})=2^{N-2}\varepsilon.

If
J
is not completely contained inside
[0,2^{N-4}]
, by considering its periodic extension of period
2^{N-4}
and its intersection with
[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]
, we still obtain a set of
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-measure
2^{N-4}\varepsilon
(see Figure
9
).

0
2^{N-4}
-2^{N-4}
{\color[rgb]{0.29,0.56,0.89}y}
J\cap\left[-2^{N-4},\ 2^{N-4}\right]

Figure 9:
The interval
J
is not necessarily completely contained in
[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]
.

Now, if
j<N-4
, the set we obtain is morally the same, but at a much smaller scale and instead of having the measure of
2
intervals (
J_{-1}
and
J_{0}
) of length
2^{N-3}\varepsilon
, we have
2^{N-3-j}
intervals of length
2^{j+1}\varepsilon
. Hence, in this case we find that

\mathcal{L}^{1}(\{w_{1}\in[-2^{N-4},2^{N-4}]:\exists\,I\in\mathcal{D}_{0,j}^{1}+w_{1},\,x_{1}\in\delta_{I}\})=2^{N-3-j}\cdot 2^{j+1}\varepsilon=2^{N-2}\varepsilon,

which is the same that we found in the case that
j=N-4
. Lastly, if
j>N-4
, for every fixed
\varepsilon>0
, the measure that we obtain is
\text{min}(2^{N-3},2^{j+1}\varepsilon)\leq 2^{N+1}\varepsilon
. This means that in any case,

p_{\varepsilon}^{1,j}\leq(2^{N-3})^{d-1}\,2^{N+1}\varepsilon,

which has limit
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, independently of
x
and of
k
, as we wanted to prove.
∎

Along with the notion of
p_{\varepsilon}
, we will also need to define, for
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
, its
bad part
as

Q_{\text{b}}=Q\cap\bigg(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\\
2^{-m}\ell(R)\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{m}\ell(R)\end{subarray}}\delta_{R}\bigg),

(122)

and the same swapping the roles of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. The reason for the size condition in the second line is the analogous one that appears in the third line of (
89
), which is what we are trying to control.

Now, we are going to deal with transit cubes whose parents are also transit. This motivates us to write, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

f^{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{1,Q}f,\quad f^{k}_{b_{1}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}\Delta_{1,Q}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}c_{Q}(f)\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}b_{1},

where the coefficients
c_{Q}(f)
are defined by the same rule as in (
91
). We define
g^{k}
and
g^{k}_{b_{2}}
analogously. By Lemma
2.21
, we can write

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\Xi_{1}f+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{k}=\Xi_{1}f+\sum_{k\leq N}f^{k},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\Xi_{2}g+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}g^{k}=\Xi_{2}g+\sum_{k\leq N}g^{k},

in
L^{2}(\mu)
, because the series above are unconditionally convergent and, by definition, transit cubes have side length bounded above by
2^{N}
. The importance of the functions
f^{k}_{b_{1}}
is reflected in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Denote by
P^{\Omega^{2}}=P^{\Omega}\times P^{\Omega}
and for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
f^{k}_{b_{1}}
be as above. Then,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{k\leq N}\|f^{k}_{b_{1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq p_{\varepsilon}\,c_{3}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proof.

The key step to prove the inequality above is to notice that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
,
P^{\Omega}(x\in Q_{\text{b}})\leq p_{\varepsilon}
. This is because if
x\in Q_{\text{b}}
, then there is
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}^{\text{tr},2}
such that
x\in\delta_{R}
. Hence, taking
k=\log_{2}\ell(Q)
in (
120
), we see that the desired inequality holds.

For any
x\in\text{supp}(\mu)
and
k\leq N
, we have

f^{k}_{b_{1}}(x)=\begin{cases}c_{Q}(f)\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}(x)b_{1}(x),\quad&\text{if }\,x\in Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\text{ with }\,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1},\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence, taking expectation over
w_{2}\in\Omega
, for
x
as in the first case above,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega}}\left(|f^{k}_{b_{1}}(x)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|c_{Q}(f)\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}(x)b_{1}(x)|^{2}\,dP^{\Omega}(w_{2})=|c_{Q}(f)b_{1}(x)|^{2}\int_{\Omega}\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}(x)\,dP^{\Omega}(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=|c_{Q}(f)b_{1}(x)|^{2}\,P^{\Omega}\left(x\in Q_{\text{b}}\right)\leq p_{\varepsilon}\,|\Delta_{1,Q}f(x)|^{2}=p_{\varepsilon}\ |f^{k}(x)|^{2},

because of our first remark. For
x
as in the second case, the expectation is trivially zero. This shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega}}\left(\sum_{k\leq N}\|f^{k}_{b_{1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\leq N}\int\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega}}\left(|f^{k}_{b_{1}}(x)|^{2}\right)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq p_{\varepsilon}\sum_{k\leq N}\int|f^{k}(x)|^{2}d\,\mu(x)

\displaystyle=p_{\varepsilon}\sum_{k\leq N}\|f^{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq p_{\varepsilon}\,c_{3}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where in the last inequality we have used again Lemma
2.21
. Taking expectation once again, we obtain the inequality in the statement of the current lemma.
∎

Moreover, for each
k\leq N
, we can write

\displaystyle\|f^{k}_{b_{1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\int\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}c_{Q}(f)\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}(x)b_{1}(x)\bigg|^{2}\,d\mu(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}\int_{Q_{\text{b}}}|c_{Q}(f)b_{1}(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{k}\end{subarray}}|c_{Q}(f)|^{2}\|\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}b_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Combining this identity with the inequality given in the preceding lemma, we have found that

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q,\,\widehat{Q}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}\end{subarray}}|c_{Q}(f)|^{2}\|\chi_{Q_{\text{b}}}b_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{k\leq N}\|f^{k}_{b_{1}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq p_{\varepsilon}\,c_{3}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Clearly, the analogous estimate for the function
g
also holds,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R,\,\widehat{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}|c_{R}(g)|^{2}\|\chi_{R_{\text{b}}}b_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq p_{\varepsilon}\,c_{3}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

(123)

These two identities are going to be essential to bound the last sum in (
102
).

5.2
The good set
G

Recall that the conclusion from Theorem
2.1
was that there exists a set

G\subset F\setminus\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right)

satisfying properties (i)-(iii). In this section we will give the precise definition of
G
. For this, let
W_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
and
W_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
be the total exceptional sets, as defined in Section
3.1
, corresponding to two dyadic lattices
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}=\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
. Using (
22
), we get that for some
0<\delta_{1}<1
and for all
w\in\Omega
,

\displaystyle\mu(W_{\mathcal{D}(w)})

\displaystyle=\mu(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup S)

\displaystyle\leq\mu(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)})+\mu(S\setminus H_{\mathcal{D}(w)})\leq\delta_{1}\mu(F),

from which we deduce that
\mu(F\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}(w)})\geq(1-\delta)\mu(F)
. Using this inequality, we are going to define
G
using, once again, a probabilistic argument. For each
x\in F
, we consider the probabilities

\displaystyle p_{0}(x)

\displaystyle:=P^{\Omega}\left(\left\{w\in\Omega:x\in F\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right\}\right),

\displaystyle p(x)

\displaystyle:=P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega\times\Omega:x\in F\setminus(W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{1})}\cup W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{2})})\right\}\right).

Recall that
P^{\Omega^{2}}=P^{\Omega}\times P^{\Omega}
, which is the product of the normalized
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure in
\Omega
with itself, so applying Fubini’s theorem we obtain the relation
p(x)=p_{0}(x)^{2}
. Moreover, integrating with respect to
\mu
, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{F}p_{0}(x)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{F}\left(\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\chi_{F\setminus(W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{1})}\cup W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{2})})}(x)\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(w_{1},w_{2})\right)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}\mu(F\setminus(W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{1})}\cup W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{2})}))\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(w_{1},w_{2})\geq(1-\delta_{1})\mu(F).

(124)

We define the sets

G:=\left\{x\in F:p_{0}(x)>\frac{(1-\delta_{1})}{2}\right\},

and
B:=F\setminus G
. The motivation for defining such a set
G
is that for every
x\in G
, we have a strictly positive lower bound of
p(x)
, namely,

p(x)=p_{0}(x)^{2}>\frac{(1-\delta_{1})^{2}}{4}:=\beta>0.

(125)

Informally, this number being strictly positive means that for a big proportion of dyadic lattices
\mathcal{D}(w_{1}),\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
, the points from
G
are in
F\setminus(W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{1})}\cup W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{2})})
.

We claim that
G
is the desired set from Theorem
2.1
. First, if we take
x\in\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{C}}(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)})
, in particular,
x\not\in F\setminus W_{\mathcal{D}(w)}
for every
w\in\Omega
. Hence,
p_{0}(x)=0
. By definition, this means that
x\not\in G
. This proves that

G\subset F\setminus\bigcap_{w\in\mathbb{C}}\left(H_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\cup T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}(w)}\right),

as wished. Moreover, we have, for
x\in B
,

p_{0}(x)\leq\frac{1-\delta_{1}}{2}\,\Leftrightarrow\,(1-p_{0}(x))\frac{2}{1+\delta_{1}}\geq 1,

so we find, using the lower bound in (
124
), that the
\mu
-measure of
B
is controlled by

\displaystyle\mu(B)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\left\{x\in F:p_{0}(x)\leq\frac{1-\delta_{1}}{2}\right\}\right)\leq\frac{2}{1+\delta}\int_{F}(1-p_{0}(x))\,d\mu(x)\leq\frac{2\,\delta_{1}}{1+\delta_{1}}\mu(F).

Hence,

\mu(G)=\mu(F)-\mu(B)\geq\frac{1-\delta_{1}}{1+\delta_{1}}\mu(F),

which is condition (i) from Theorem
2.1
. Now, we define a function

\displaystyle\Omega\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow[0,+\infty)

\displaystyle(w_{1},w_{2},x)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)=\text{dist}(x,F\setminus(W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{1})}\cup W_{\mathcal{D}(w_{2})})).

Observe that if
x\in G
, then

P^{\Omega^{2}}(\{(w_{1},w_{2}):\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)=0\})\geq p(x)>\beta.

From this, we deduce that

\mu(\{x\in F:p(x)>\beta\})\geq\mu(G)\geq\frac{1-\delta_{1}}{1+\delta_{1}}\mu(F),

and

\mu\left(\left\{x\in F:P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{(w_{1},w_{2}):\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)=0\right\}\right)>\beta\right\}\right)\geq\frac{1-\delta_{1}}{1+\delta_{1}}\mu(F).

The rest of the proof will be devoted to showing that
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
.

5.3
The functions
\Phi,\Psi_{\text{w}}
and the operators
\widetilde{K}
and
\widetilde{T}

Recall that in our main lemma for good functions (Lemma
3.1
), we considered a function
\Theta
satisfying certain conditions that allowed us to obtain the right bounds for
|\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle|
, where
K_{\Theta}
is the suppressed operator and
f
and
g
were good functions. In this section, we are going to choose an appropriate function
\Theta
, with which we will apply Lemma
3.1
. In addition, we will consider two operators, arising from the averages of our suppressed operator with respect to
P^{\Omega^{2}}
, which will be crucial in showing that
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
.

First, for a fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we define

\Phi(x)=\varepsilon_{0}+\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subset\Omega^{2}\\
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)=\beta\end{subarray}}\sup_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in B}\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)=:\varepsilon_{0}+I(x).

(126)

In the following lemma, we show three relevant characteristics of this function.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Phi
be the function defined in (
126
). Then, the following three properties hold.

1.

\Phi
is
1
-Lipschitz.

2.

\Phi(x)=\varepsilon_{0}
for all
x\in G
.

3.

For any
x\in F
,

\Phi(x)\geq\max(\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{C}\setminus H),e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x),\varepsilon_{0})\geq\max(\mathcal{R}(x),e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x),\varepsilon_{0}),

where
e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x)
are as we defined them in (
16
) and (
17
), respectively, and
H
and
\mathcal{R}(x)
in (
103
).

Proof.

For the first part, note that since for any fixed
(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega\times\Omega
,
x\mapsto\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)
is
1
-Lipschitz, for a fixed
B\subset\Omega^{2}
, taking supremum in
(w_{1},w_{2})\in B
still has the Lipschitz property. To see that it is also preserved after taking the infimum, for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
fixed and
\varepsilon>0
, let
B_{y}\subset\Omega^{2}
be such that
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)=\beta
and

S_{B_{y}}(y)=\sup_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in B}\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(y)\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subset\Omega^{2}\\
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)=\beta\end{subarray}}\sup_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in B}\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(y)+\varepsilon.

Then,

I(x)-I(y)\leq S_{B_{y}}(x)-I(y)=(S_{B_{y}}(x)-S_{B_{y}}(y))+(S_{B_{y}}(y)-I(y))\leq|x-y|+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, we obtain that
I(x)-I(y)\leq|x-y|
. Since
y,x
were also arbitrary, the function
x\mapsto I(x)
is
1
-Lipschitz, and so is
\Phi
.

Secondly, if
x\in G
, by definition we have that the set
A_{x}=\{(w_{1},w_{2}):\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)>0\}
satisfies that
P^{\Omega^{2}}(A_{x})>0
. Recalling that
P^{\Omega^{2}}=P^{\Omega}\times P^{\Omega}
, and each of these is a normalization of
d
-dimensional Lebesgue measure, this implies that there exists some
B_{x}\subset A_{x}
with
P(B_{x})=\beta
. Then, it is clear that

\sup_{(w_{1},w_{2})\in B_{x}}\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)=0,

which implies that
I(x)=0
, as defined above during the proof of the first part. Hence,
\Phi(x)=0
for such
x
.

For the proof of the third point, it suffices to notice that for all
x\in F
and
(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega\times\Omega
,

\Phi_{(w_{1},w_{2})}(x)\geq\max(\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{C}\setminus H),e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x)).

This is because if
B(x,r)>c_{0}r^{n}
, then this ball must be contained in
H_{\mathcal{D}}
for any dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
, in particular, for
\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
, and, of course, the set
S
is independent of
\mathcal{D}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}(w_{2})
.
∎

Given
\text{w}=(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega^{2}
, we set
\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)=\max(\Phi(x),\Phi_{\text{w}}(x))
, and we consider the operator obtained from the average

\widetilde{K}f(x)=\int K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w}).

That is
\widetilde{K}
is an integral operator with kernel given by

\widetilde{k}(x,y)=\int\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w}).

Analogously, we define an operator via averaging the truncated operators,

\widetilde{T}f(x)=\int T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}f(x)\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w}),

with kernel

\widetilde{t}(x,y)=\int k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w}),\quad\text{where }\,k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)=k(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}.

(127)

Note that
\widetilde{k}(x,y)
is a Calderón-Zygmund kernel, because it is the average of the kernels
k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)
, which we know that are also of this type. In contrast, this is not the case for the truncated kernel
k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)
, in general.

Below, we are going to show, using the operators
\widetilde{K}
and
\widetilde{T}
, that the suppressed operator
K_{\Phi}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, with norm independent of
\varepsilon_{0}
. The
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)

boundedness of our SIO
T
will be a consequence of the fact that
\Phi\equiv\varepsilon_{0}
on the set
G
, which does not depend on
\varepsilon_{0}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Consider the truncated kernels,

k_{\Phi}(x,y)=k(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)},\qquad k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)=k(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)},

and
\widetilde{t}
as in (
127
). Denote the associated operators by
T_{\Phi}
,
T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
, and
\widetilde{T}
, respectively. The maximal operator associated with the kernel
\widetilde{t}(x,y)
is defined as

\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)=\sup_{\delta>0}|\widetilde{T}_{\delta}f(x)|=\sup_{\delta>0}\left|\int_{|x-y|\geq\delta}\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,f(x)\,d\mu(y)\right|,\quad\text{for }f\in L^{2}(\mu),x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Then, for any
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have that

|T_{\Phi}f(x)|\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x),\qquad|T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)|\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x),\quad\text{for each }\,\text{w}\in\Omega^{2},

(128)

where
\beta
is the constant defined in (
125
).

Proof.

We start by proving the first inequality in (
128
). To do so, we write the kernel
\widetilde{t}
as

\widetilde{t}(x,y)=k(x,y)P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:|x-y|\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)\right\}\right)=k(x,y)v_{x}(|x-y|),

(129)

where we define

v_{x}(t)=P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:t\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)=\max(\Phi(x),\Phi_{\text{w}}(x))\right\}\right).

We claim that the new function satisfies the following properties:

1.

If
t<\Phi(x)
, then
v_{x}(t)=0
.

2.

v_{x}
is non-decreasing in
t
and right-continuous.

3.

If
t\geq\Phi(x)
, then
v_{x}(t)\geq\beta
.

The first property is clear: using that
\Phi(x)\leq\Psi_{\text{w}}
for any
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
, if
t\leq\Phi(x)
, the probability in the definition of
v_{x}
is obviously zero. For the second property, let
t\leq s
. Since

\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:s\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)\}\supset\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:t\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)\},

we have that
v_{x}(s)\geq v_{x}(t)
and so
v_{x}
is non-decreasing. The right-continuity follows from the fact that, for each
x
,
v_{x}
is the distribution function of the random variable
\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)
. To see that the last property holds, note that if
t\geq\Phi(x)
, then

v_{x}(t)=P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:t\geq\Phi_{\text{w}}(x)\right\}\right).

(130)

Moreover, recalling how we defined
\Phi(x)
in (
126
), if
t\geq\Phi(x)
, then it also holds that

t\geq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subset\Omega^{2}\\
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)\geq\beta\end{subarray}}\sup_{\text{w}^{\prime}\in B}\Phi_{\text{w}^{\prime}}(x).

Now, it can be that either the infimum is strictly smaller than
t
or that it is equal to
t
. We will separate these two cases and give a different argument for each one in order to see that
v_{x}(t)\geq\beta
.

•

If
t>I(x)
, let
0<\varepsilon<\frac{t-I(x)}{2}
. By definition of infimum, there is some
B_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega^{2}
with
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B_{\varepsilon})\geq\beta
such that

\sup_{\text{w}^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon}}\Phi_{\text{w}^{\prime}}(x)\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\Omega^{2}\\
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)\geq\beta\end{subarray}}\sup_{\text{w}^{\prime}\in B}\Phi_{\text{w}^{\prime}}(x)+\varepsilon=I(x)+\varepsilon<t.

This means that
B_{\varepsilon}
must be contained in the subset appearing on the right-hand side of (
130
). Then, taking probabilities, we obtain that
v_{x}(t)\geq P^{\Omega^{2}}(B_{\varepsilon})\geq\beta
.

•

If
t=I(x)
, let us call
s=\{s\in\mathbb{R}:v_{x}(s)\geq\beta\}
. We claim that
t=\inf S
. Indeed, if
s\in S
, we call
B_{s}=\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:\Phi_{\text{w}}(x)\leq s\}
. Then,
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B_{s})=v_{x}(s)\geq\beta
. Hence,
t\leq s
. For the converse inequality, let
B\subset\Omega^{2}
be such that
P^{\Omega^{2}}(B)\geq\beta
, and denote
s_{B}=\sup_{\text{w}\in B}\Phi_{\text{w}}(x)
, so obviously
B\subset\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:\Phi_{\text{w}}(x)\leq s_{B}\}
. Taking probabilities we have that

\beta\leq P^{\Omega^{2}}\left(\left\{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}:\Phi_{\text{w}}(x)\leq s_{B}\right\}\right)=v_{x}(s_{B}),

from which we have that
s_{B}\in S
. Since
t
is the infimum of the
s_{B}
, we obtain the desired inequality. Lastly, by definition of infimum, we can take
(s_{n})\subset S
a decreasing sequence with
t=\lim_{n}s_{n}
. Since for all
n
,
v_{x}(s_{n})\geq\beta
, by right-continuity of
v_{x}
we obtain that
v_{x}(t)\geq\beta
, which is what we wanted to show.

As well as these three properties of the function
v_{x}
, we will require an additional identity, which we deduce now. From (
129
) and the first property above, we infer that

\widetilde{t}(x,y)=\widetilde{t}(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}=k(x,y)\,v_{x}(|x-y|)\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}=v_{x}(|x-y|)\,k_{\Phi}(x,y).

Rearranging,

k_{\Phi}(x,y)=v_{x}(|x-y|)^{-1}\,\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}.

(131)

Using this identity and the previous three properties, we will be able to write the kernel
k_{\Phi}
as a convex combination of the kernels
\widetilde{t}(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq t}
, for
t\geq\Phi(x)
, which we will integrate with respect to
\mu
to obtain our result. To do so, since
v_{x}(t)^{-1}
, as a function of
t
, is continuous from the right and non-increasing, we can define a Lebesgue-Stieljes measure
\sigma
on
\mathbb{R}
by considering

\begin{cases}\sigma(a,b]=v_{x}(a)^{-1}-v_{x}(b)^{-1},\qquad\text{if }\,(a,b]\subset(\Phi(x),\infty),\\
\sigma(-\infty,\Phi(x)]=0.\end{cases}

From the non-increasing property, we see that this is indeed a positive measure. Moreover, it is finite, because

\sigma(\mathbb{R})\leq\sup_{t\geq\Phi(x)}\frac{1}{v_{x}(t)}\leq\beta,

by the third property from the start of the proof. Now, we write

\displaystyle\int_{t>\Phi(x)}\widetilde{t}(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq t}\,d\sigma(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,\sigma((\Phi(x),|x-y|])

\displaystyle=\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}(v_{x}(\Phi(x))^{-1}-v_{x}(|x-y|)^{-1}),

which, using (
131
), is the same as

k_{\Phi}(x,y)=v_{x}(\Phi(x))^{-1}\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}-\int_{t>\Phi(x)}\widetilde{t}(x,y)\chi_{|x-y|\geq t}\,d\sigma(t),

which is the convex combination that we mentioned above. If we multiply the left hand side of this identity by
f(y)
and integrate with respect to
\mu
on
y
, we get
T_{\Phi}f(x)
. If we do the same for the right hand side, we obtain

\displaystyle v_{x}(\Phi(x))^{-1}\int_{|x-y|\geq\Phi(x)}\widetilde{t}(x,y)f(y)\,d\mu(y)-\int_{t>\Phi(x)}\bigg(\int_{|x-y|\geq t}\widetilde{t}(x,y)f(y)\,d\mu(y)\bigg)\,d\sigma(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=v_{x}(\Phi(x))^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{\Phi(x)}f(x)-\int_{t>\Phi(x)}\widetilde{T}_{t}f(x)\,d\sigma(t),

where
\widetilde{T}_{t}
and
\widetilde{T}_{\Phi(x)}
are the
t
-truncated and
\Phi(x)
-truncated versions of the operator
\widetilde{T}
defined in the statement of the lemma, respectively. From this, since
\sigma(\mathbb{R})=v_{x}(\Phi(x))^{-1}\leq\beta^{-1}
, we obtain the first inequality in (
128
). Indeed,

|T_{\Phi}f(x)|\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)+\int_{t>\Phi(x)}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)\,d\sigma(x)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\,\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x).

It remains to prove the second inequality in (
128
). The strategy to do so will be analogous to what we have just argued. Indeed, notice that by the definition of
T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
, we have that

k_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)=k_{\Phi}(x,y)\,\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}=v_{x}(|x-y|)^{-1}\,\widetilde{t}(x,y)\,\chi_{|x-y|\geq\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}.

Observe that this is the same identity as (
131
), replacing
\Phi
by
\Psi_{\text{w}}
. Arguing analogously as before, integrating with respect to
\sigma
for
t>\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)
, we obtain that

T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)=v_{x}(\Psi_{\text{w}}(x))^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}f(x)-\int_{t>\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}\widetilde{T}_{t}f(x)\,d\sigma(x).

Therefore,

|T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)|\leq\frac{1}{\beta}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)+\int_{t>\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)\,d\sigma(t)\leq\frac{2}{\beta}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x),

which is exactly the second inequality in (
128
).
∎

Notice that the operator
\widetilde{T}
is the average of the operators
T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
over
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
. From this, we see that (
128
) asserts that each of the operators
T_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
is controlled by the maximal version of the average operator
\widetilde{T}
.

Lastly, we are going to see that we can control the operator norms of both
K_{\Phi}
and
K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
in
L^{2}(\mu)
by that of
\widetilde{K}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have that

\|K_{\Phi}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c\beta^{-1},

(132)

and

\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c\beta^{-1},\quad\text{for each w}\in\Omega^{2}.

(133)

Proof.

We start by applying Lemma
2.12
, with the measure
\sigma=f\mu
, for
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
. If
\Theta\,\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,+\infty)
is a
1
-Lipschitz function, for
\varepsilon\geq\Theta(x)
, we have that

|T_{\varepsilon}\sigma(x)-K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}\sigma(x)|\leq c\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}|f|\,d\mu,

(134)

where we denote by
K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}
the
\varepsilon
-truncated version of the suppressed operator
K_{\Theta}
. For an arbitrary
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we denote
T_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)=T_{\max(\Theta(x),\varepsilon)}f(x)
. We claim that then,

|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)-T_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)|\leq c\sup_{r\geq\Theta(x)}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}|f|\,d\mu.

Indeed, for
\varepsilon\geq\Theta(x)
the preceding inequality is simply (
134
). For
\varepsilon<\Theta(x)
, we can write

\displaystyle|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)-T_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)|

\displaystyle=|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)-T_{\Theta(x)}f(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|K_{\Theta,\Theta(x)}f(x)-T_{\Theta(x)}f(x)|+|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)-K_{\Theta,\Theta(x)}f(x)|

\displaystyle=|K_{\Theta,\Theta(x)}f(x)-T_{\Theta(x)}f(x)|+\int_{B(x,\Theta(x))}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\sup_{r\geq\Theta(x)}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)+\int_{B(x,\Theta(x))}\frac{c}{\Theta(x)^{n}}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq c\sup_{r\geq\Theta(x)}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}|f|\,d\mu

where we have used (
134
) with
\varepsilon=\Theta(x)
. This settles our claim. Suppose now that
\Theta
is one of the Lipschitz functions
\Phi
or
\Psi_{\text{w}}
, defined in the beginning of this section. Since, in any case,
\mu(B(x,r))\leq c_{0}r^{n}
for any
r\geq\Theta(x)
, the supremum above is bounded by
c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x)
, the maximal function. So, we have proved that

|K_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)-T_{\Theta,\varepsilon}f(x)|\leq c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x),\quad\text{for any }\varepsilon\geq 0.

(135)

Choosing
\Theta=\Phi
and
\varepsilon=0
, from Lemma
5.8
, we infer that

|K_{\Phi}f(x)|\leq|T_{\Phi}f(x)|+c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x)\leq 2\beta^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)+c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x).

(136)

Thus, knowing that the centered maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, we obtain that

\|K_{\Phi}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 2\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{T}_{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c.

Moreover, setting
\Theta=\Psi_{\text{w}}
in (
135
), and taking the mean on
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle|\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon}f(x)-\widetilde{T}_{\varepsilon}f(x)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{\Omega^{2}}\left(K_{\Psi_{\text{w}},\varepsilon}f(x)-T_{\Psi_{\text{w}},\varepsilon}f(x)\right)dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w})\bigg|\leq c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x).

Taking supremum in
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we deduce that

\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)\leq\widetilde{K}_{*}f(x)+c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x),

where we denote
\widetilde{K}_{*}f(x)=\sup_{\varepsilon>0}|\widetilde{K}_{\varepsilon}f(x)|
. If we plug this in (
136
),

|K_{\Phi}f(x)|\leq 2\beta^{-1}\widetilde{T}_{*}f(x)+c\,c_{0}M_{\mu}f(x)\leq 2\beta^{-1}\widetilde{K}_{*}f(x)+c\,c_{0}\,\beta^{-1}M_{\mu}f(x),

where we also used the fact that
0<\beta<1
. Then, noticing that each
\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
satisfies condition (
104
) with
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{0}
and so the same holds for the average kernel
\widetilde{k}
, applying Lemma
4.1
, we know that

\|\widetilde{K}_{*}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c.

Therefore,

\|K_{\Phi}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c\beta^{-1},

which is (
132
). The proof of (
133
) is analogous and we omit it.
∎

5.4
The key estimates for
A_{i}

Recall that for a function
\Theta
and good functions
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.1
, we were only able to bound

|\langle K_{\Theta}f,g\rangle|\leq c_{a}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}+A_{4},

where
c_{a}
is a constant that depends on some small number
\varepsilon_{a}
, to be fixed later, and the constant
\alpha
from Section
2.1
, and the sums
A_{i}
were defined in (
102
). This section is devoted to giving a probabilistic bound for the terms
A_{i}
. That is, we are going to bound their expectation with respect to
P^{\Omega^{2}}
. From now on, we are going to choose
\Theta=\Psi_{\text{w}}
, for
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
.

Remember that the terms
A_{1},A_{2}
and
A_{3}
concern the case where one of the cubes is terminal, while in
A_{4}
, all cubes that appear are transit. We will now see that the estimates for the first three terms are very similar. Recall that a cube
P\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
, we say that it is terminal (of the first kind) if either
2P\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
or if
P\subset T^{1}_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
. In the first case, note that then, for any
x\in P
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{\Psi_{\text{w}}(x)^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\Phi(x)^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\text{dist}(x,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus H)^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\ell(P)^{n}}.

Analogously, if
S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}
is terminal because
2S\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
,

|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{\ell(S)^{n}},\quad\text{for all }x\in S,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
A_{1}
be as in (
102
), with
\Theta=\Psi_{\text{w}}
, for
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
. Then, for any
\gamma>0
, and
f,g
as in Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(A_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\sqrt{\gamma}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Proof.

We will only show the details for
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
. All the other cases are analogous. Assume first that
S
is terminal because
2S\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
. Then,

\displaystyle|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{x\in S}|\Delta_{2,R}g(x)|\bigg(\int_{y\in P\setminus S}|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\,|\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)|\,d\mu(y)\bigg)d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\ell(S)^{n}}\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\frac{\mu(Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(R)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}}\ell(R)^{\frac{n}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq c\|\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

where we have used that
P\subset\mathcal{CH}(Q),S\in\mathcal{CH}(R)
,
\ell(P)\approx\ell(S)
and, lastly, that since both
Q
and
R
are transit,
\mu(Q)\leq c_{0}\ell(Q)^{n},\mu(R)\leq c_{0}\ell(R)^{n}
. Recalling that the cubes
Q
can only interact with a uniformly bounded number of cubes
R
and vice versa, we get that the sum over the terminal cubes
2S\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{2}}
is bounded above by
c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. The same holds if
2P\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
.

Now, assume that
S
is terminal because
S\subset T^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}
. In this case, we divide
P\setminus S=P_{0}\cup\delta_{PS}
, where (see Figure
10
).

\delta_{PS}=\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)\cap(P\setminus S).

P
S
P\cap S
P_{0}
\delta_{PS}
\gamma\ell(P)

Figure 10:
The sets
P_{0}
and
\delta_{PS}
inside
P\setminus S
.

Using this way of writing
P\setminus S
as a disjoint union of two subsets, we have that each term
|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P\setminus S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|
appearing in the sum
A_{1}
is controlled by

\displaystyle\int_{y\in P_{0}}\bigg(\int_{x\in S}|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\,|\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)|\,|\Delta_{2,R}g(x)|\,d\mu(x)\bigg)d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{y\in\delta_{PS}}\bigg(\int_{x\in S}|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\,|\Delta_{1,Q}f(y)|\,|\Delta_{2,R}g(x)|\,d\mu(x)\bigg)d\mu(y):=I_{1}+I_{2}.

The bound for
I_{1}
is simple, due to the distance to the boundary of
S
that we have created. Indeed, for
y\in P_{0}
and
x\in S
, we have
|x-y|\geq\gamma\ell(P)
, so

|\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(x,y)|\leq\frac{c}{|x-y|^{n}}\leq\frac{c}{\gamma^{n}}\frac{1}{\ell(P)^{n}},

and so we obtain the same bound as in the case that
2S\subset H_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
, with a constant that, of course, depends on the constant
\gamma
, which will be fixed later. For the second integral, we have that

I_{2}=I_{2}(P,S)\leq\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{\delta_{PS}}\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Let us take expectations over all these terms. Applying Cauchy-Scwharz twice, denoting
\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}=\mathbb{E}_{(w_{1},w_{2})}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{\delta_{PS}}\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{(w_{1},w_{2})}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{\delta_{PS}}\Delta_{1,Q}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\bigg)\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{(w_{1},w_{2})}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,R}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\bigg)\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad=:A\cdot B.

Our goal now is to bound
A\leq c\sqrt{\gamma}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
and
B\leq c\,\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. We start with the inequality involving the term
B
. Notice that the cubes
S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}
whose parent is transit are pairwise disjoint. Moreover, since each cube
R
can only interact with a bounded number of cubes
Q
(satisfying the conditions defining
\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
), we can bound, for each pair
(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega^{2}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{S}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{S}}g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\\
\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{S}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\bigg(\sum_{P:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}1\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\\
\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\|\chi_{S}\Delta_{2,\widehat{S}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{term},2}_{2}\\
\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}\end{subarray}}\|\Delta_{2,\widehat{S}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu\lfloor S)}^{2}\leq c\,\|g\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

from which we deduce that
B\leq c\,\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
. Let us turn our attention to the term
A
. For this one, it will be useful to write

\displaystyle A^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(w_{1},w_{2})}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{\delta_{PS}}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{(w_{1},w_{2})}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{w_{1}}\bigg(\sum_{P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\bigg[\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}\bigg(\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\bigg]\bigg).

(137)

We claim that for each
P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
,

\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}\bigg(\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq c\sqrt{\gamma}\,\|\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

For each
S
in the sum above, we can write its boundary as the sum of
2d

(d-1)
-dimmensional faces,
\partial S=\cup_{k=1}^{2d}c_{k}
. Hence,

\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)=\bigcup_{k=1}^{2d}\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k}).

Therefore,

\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k})}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

To estimate each of the terms in the sum above, let us consider, for each fixed
P,S
, the measure
d\sigma=|\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f|^{2}\,d\mu
. With this notation,

\displaystyle\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k})}|\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f(x)|^{2}\,d\mu(x)=\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k})).

Again, since each
S
in the sum that we are estimating can only interact with a finite number of cubes
P
, we can swap the order of the expectation and the sum, to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}\bigg(\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}(\sigma(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k}))).

Note that on the right-hand side above, the random term inside the expectation is
c_{k}
, which, strictly speaking, is
c_{k}(w_{2})
, but for commodity we have never written it in this form. Fix
1\leq k\leq 2d
, we are going to estimate the corresponding expectation above. We can assume that
c_{k}
is contained in the hyperplane
H_{1}(s)=\{x_{1}=s\}
, for
s\in\pi(P)=I
, where
\pi
denotes the projection into the first coordinate. This enables us to parametrize
c_{k}(w_{2})=c_{k}(s)
and because of the periodicity of the dyadic lattice,

dP^{\Omega}\leq\frac{c}{\mathcal{L}^{1}(I)}d(\mathcal{L}^{1}\lfloor I)=\frac{c}{\ell(P)}d(\mathcal{L}^{1}\lfloor I),

and so we can compute the expectation as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}\left[\sigma\left(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\sigma\left(\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(c_{k}(w_{2}))\right)dP^{\Omega}(w_{2})

\displaystyle\leq c\,\frac{1}{\ell(P)}\int_{x_{1}\in I}\bigg(\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,y\in P\,:\\
&|x_{1}-y_{1}|\leq\gamma\ell(P)\crcr}}}d\sigma(y)\bigg)\,dx_{1}

\displaystyle=c\,\frac{1}{\ell(P)}\int_{y\in P}\bigg(\int_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr&\,x_{1}\in I\,:\\
&|x_{1}-y_{1}|\leq\gamma\ell(P)\crcr}}}dx_{1}\bigg)\,d\sigma(y)

\displaystyle=2\gamma\int_{y\in P}d\sigma(y)=2\gamma\,\|\sigma\|=2\gamma\,\|\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

This means that for each
P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{w_{2}}\bigg(\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\|\chi_{P\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma\ell(P)}(\partial S)}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\leq\sum_{S:(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}}\sum_{k=1}^{2d}2\gamma\,\|\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\leq C\gamma\,\|\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2},

because each
P
can only interact with a finite number of cubes
S
. If we plug this in (
137
), we have been able to bound

\displaystyle A^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}_{w_{1}}\bigg(\sum_{P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}c\,\gamma\,\|\chi_{P}\Delta_{1,\widehat{P}}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq c\,\gamma\,\mathbb{E}_{w_{1}}\bigg(\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)\leq c\,\gamma\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2},

where we have used Lemma
2.21
. This finishes the proof that
A\leq c\,\sqrt{\gamma}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
and thus that of Lemma
5.10
, for the sum over the pairs
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,2}
. For
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{2,1}
, we would simply interchange the roles of
P
and
S
in the arguments above and we obtain the same bound. For
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{2,2}
, instead of using Lemma
2.21
in the last step, we would use the fact that the terminal cubes
P
whose parent is transit are pairwise disjoint, exactly as we have done for the estimate of the term
B
.
∎

For the second and third terms
A_{2}+A_{3}
, almost the same arguments, changing the roles of the cubes
P
and
S
whenever we need to, yield the same bound, which is the following lemma, the proof of which we omit in order to avoid unnecessary repetition.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
be as in (
102
), with
\Theta=\Psi_{\text{w}}
, for
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
. Then, for any
\gamma>0
and
f,g
as in Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(A_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{S\setminus P}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\sqrt{\gamma}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(A_{3})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,2}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2,2}}|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{1,Q}f),\chi_{P\cap S}\Delta_{2,R}g\rangle|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\sqrt{\gamma}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Lastly, we deal with the sum
A_{4}
, in which all the cubes that appear are transit. Unfortunately, the same technique as in the two preceding lemmas fails, because the family of transit cubes whose parents are also transit is not pairwise disjoint, unlike in the case where the children are terminal and the parents are transit. To get an appropriate bound in this case, we will need to separate each summand even further. We do this in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
A_{4}
be as in (
102
), with
\Theta=\Psi_{\text{w}}
for
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
. Then, for
f,g
as in Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(A_{4})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{\mathcal{A}_{1,1}}|c_{P}(f)||c_{S}(g)||\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta_{P,S}}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta_{P,S}\setminus(\Delta_{P,S})_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle|\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(p_{\varepsilon_{a}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right),

where
p_{\varepsilon_{a}}\to 0
as
\varepsilon_{a}\to 0
, as discussed in Lemma
5.5
, and
c_{\varepsilon_{a}}
is a constant that depends on
\varepsilon_{a}
.

Proof.

For commodity of the reader, we recall that for
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,1}
, we denote
\Delta_{P,S}=P\cap S
, but whenever there is no possible confusion, we simple write
\Delta
instead of
\Delta_{P,S}
. Moreover, we recall that we considered three subsets of
\Delta
:
\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
,
P_{\partial}
, and
S_{\partial}
(see Figure
5
and the discussion above).

This allows us to separate further, for each
(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,1}
,

\displaystyle\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{\Delta\setminus\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}b_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{S_{\partial}}b_{2}\rangle+\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{P_{\partial}\setminus(P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial})}b_{2}\rangle

(138)

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{S_{\partial}}b_{2}\rangle+\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P_{\partial}}b_{1}),\chi_{P_{\partial}\setminus(P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial})}b_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta\setminus P_{\partial}}b_{1}),\chi_{P_{\partial}\setminus(P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial})}b_{2}\rangle.

To bound the first two terms on the right-hand side of the last inequality, we are going to use Lemma
5.6
(recall the definition of the bad part of a cube in (
122
)). For the last term, we will introduce yet another probabilistic argument, which in this case will take advantage of the growth condition on our Calderón-Zygmund kernel
\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
.

Note that we can bound the first term in (
138
) by

|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{S_{\partial}}b_{2}\rangle|\leq\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{P}b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{S_{\text{b}}}b_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

because
S_{\partial}\subset S_{\text{b}}
(remember our choice of the width
\ell_{1}
). We take the sum over the cubes that we are considering,

\displaystyle\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,1}}|c_{P}(f)|\,|c_{S}(g)|\,\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{P}b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{S_{\text{b}}}g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg(\sum_{P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}|c_{P}(f)|^{2}\|\chi_{P}b_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\sum_{S,\widehat{S}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}_{2}}|c_{S}(g)|^{2}\|\chi_{S_{\text{b}}}b_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(139)

By Lemma
2.21
, the middle factor is controlled by

\sum_{P,\widehat{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}|c_{P}(f)|^{2}\|\chi_{P}b_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}_{1}}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{3}\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)},

Hence, taking expectation in (
139
), and using the inequality (
123
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\bigg(\sum_{(P,S)\in\mathcal{A}_{1,1}}|c_{P}(f)||c_{S}(g)||\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta}b_{1}),\chi_{S_{\partial}}b_{2}\rangle|\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq c\,p_{\varepsilon_{a}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

(140)

The second term in (
138
) can be bounded by

|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{P_{\partial}}b_{1}),\chi_{P_{\partial}\setminus(P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial})}b_{2}\rangle|\leq\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{P_{\text{b}}}b_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|\chi_{S}b_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Arguing analogously as above, taking sums and then expectation, we obtain the same bound for it as in (
140
). Let us turn our attention to term from the last line of (
138
), which is

\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}(\chi_{\Delta\setminus P_{\partial}}b_{1}),\chi_{P_{\partial}\setminus(P_{\partial}\cap S_{\partial})}b_{2}\rangle,

(141)

with
\Delta=P\cap S
and
P_{\partial}
and
S_{\partial}
as chosen in Section
3.5
. Clearly, this term is bounded above, in absolute value, by

c_{b}^{2}\int_{P_{\partial}\setminus S_{\partial}}\bigg(\int_{\Delta\setminus P_{\partial}}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\,d\mu(y)\bigg)\,d\mu(x).

Since, as we have argued in previous lemmas, for any fixed
S
as above, the number of cubes
P
with which it can interact is always bounded, and the same is true swapping the roles of
S
and
P
, it is enough to bound the integral above by
c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This bound is going to be obtained using the small boundary condition that
\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
satisfies. This condition was stated in (
98
), but for commodity of the reader we recall it now. We have that
\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}
satisfies that for any
\lambda>0
,

\mu\big(\big\{x\in\Delta:\text{dist}(x,\partial\,{\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}})\leq\lambda\,\text{diam}({\Delta}_{\varepsilon_{a}}\big)\big\}\big)\leq\,t_{\Delta,{\varepsilon_{a}}}\lambda\mu(\Delta).

(142)

The technique that we will use is almost the same as what we did in Lemma
3.15
. First, we fix
x\in P_{\partial}\setminus S_{\partial}
, and we need to estimate

\displaystyle\int_{y\in\Delta\setminus P_{\partial}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}+\Theta(y)^{n}}d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\int_{\text{dist}(x,\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})\leq|x-y|\leq c^{\prime}\text{diam}(\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{n}+\Theta(x)^{n}}d\mu(y).

We can estimate the last integral by arguments analogous to the bounds in (
96
), and so we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{P_{\partial}\setminus S_{\partial}}\bigg(\int_{\Delta\setminus P_{\partial}}|\widetilde{k}_{\Theta}(x,y)|\,

\displaystyle d\mu(y)\bigg)\,d\mu(x)\leq c\int_{P_{\partial}\setminus S_{\partial}}\log\bigg(\frac{c^{\prime}\text{diam}(\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})}{\text{dist}(x,\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})}\bigg)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k\,\mu\left(\left\{y\in P_{\partial}\setminus S_{\partial}:\text{dist}(x,\partial\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})\leq 2^{-k}\text{diam}(\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}})\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\sum_{k\geq 0}k\,2^{-k}\,t_{\Delta_{\varepsilon_{a}}}\,\mu(\Delta)=c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\mu(\Delta)\leq c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\,\mu(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu(S)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Combining this inequality with the ones that we obtained for the sums of the corresponding other terms in (
138
), we have obtained that

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(A_{4})\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(p_{\varepsilon_{a}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right),

which proves the assertion of the lemma.
∎

5.5
The final step

In this section, we are going to complete the proof of Theorem
2.1
. The main ingredient in this final step will be to show that the operator
\widetilde{K}
, whose kernel was the average of the kernels
\widetilde{k}_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}
, with respect to
P^{\Omega^{2}}
, is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
. The key tools to achieve this will be, of course, our result for good functions, Lemma
3.1
, combined with the probabilistic estimates from the preceding section.

Lemma 5.13
.

The operator
\widetilde{K}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Proof.

Let
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
. Our goal is to estimate
\langle\widetilde{K}f,g\rangle
, which, by definition, is

\langle\widetilde{K}f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\int_{\Omega^{2}}K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f(x)\,dP^{\Omega^{2}}(\text{w})\bigg)g(x)\,d\mu(x)=\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f,g\rangle).

Let us estimate, for each
\text{w}=(w_{1},w_{2})\in\Omega^{2}
, the term inside the expectation on the right-hand side above. As we mentioned before the definition of bad cubes, we are going to write

f_{\text{good}}(\text{w})=\Xi_{1}f+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{good}(w_{2})}\Delta_{1,Q}f,\qquad f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{bad}(w_{2})}\Delta_{1,Q}f,

and

g_{\text{good}}(\text{w})=\Xi_{2}g+\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}(w_{2})\cap\text{good}(w_{1})}\Delta_{2,R}g,\qquad g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})=\sum_{R\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},2}(w_{2})\cap\text{bad}(w_{1})}\Delta_{2,R}g,

where “good
(w_{i})
” means “good with respect to the dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}(w_{i})
”, and the analogous notion for “bad
(w_{i})
”. since, by Lemma
2.21
, we have that
f=f_{\text{good}}(w)+f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})
and
g=g_{\text{good}}(\text{w})+g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})
in
L^{2}(\mu)
, we can write

\displaystyle\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\rangle+\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\rangle

(143)

\displaystyle\qquad+\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w}),g\rangle.

Of course, the plan now will be to apply Lemma
3.1
for the first term and, for the other two, we can take advantage of the fact that the probability of bad functions can be made arbitrarily small. Indeed, by Lemma
3.1
and the results from Section
3.5
, we have that for each
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
, and for
\varepsilon_{a}>0
small,

|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\rangle|\leq c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}+R(\text{w}),

where
R(\text{w})
corresponds to the additional terms that we were not able to bound in Section
3.5
. For these terms, gathering the results from Lemmas
5.10
,
5.11
and
5.12
, we have that for any
\gamma>0
,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}(R)\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left[\left(\sqrt{\gamma}+p_{\varepsilon_{a}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right],

where
p_{\varepsilon_{a}}
has limit
0
as
\varepsilon_{a}
approaches
0
. Hence,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\rangle|\right)\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(\text{E}(\varepsilon_{a},\gamma)\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right),

where
\text{E}(\varepsilon_{a},\gamma)\to 0
as both
\varepsilon_{a}
and
\gamma
tend to
0
. For the second term in (
143
), we have

|\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\rangle|\leq c_{3}^{2}\,\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\|_{L^{2}(\mu)},

because, applying Lemma
3.1
two times,

\displaystyle c_{3}^{-1}\|f_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Xi_{1}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Xi_{1}f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\text{tr},1}(w_{1})\cap\text{good}(w_{2})}\|\Delta_{1,Q}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c_{3}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Moreover, by Lemma
5.4
,

\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\|g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}\right)\leq c_{3}^{2}\,\varepsilon_{b}\,\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w}),g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w}\rangle)\right)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{good}}(\text{w})\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\|g_{\text{bad}}(\text{w})\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}^{3}\,\varepsilon_{b}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Arguing analogously, for the last term in (
143
), we have that

|\mathbb{E}_{P^{\Omega^{2}}}\left(\langle K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}f_{\text{bad}}(\text{w}),g\rangle\right)|\leq c_{3}\,\varepsilon_{b}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}.

Since, by the inequality in (
133
),

\sup_{\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}}\|K_{\Psi_{\text{w}}}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}+c\beta^{-1},

from the previous estimates, taking averages in (
143
) over
\text{w}\in\Omega^{2}
, we obtain

|\langle\widetilde{K}f,g\rangle|\leq c\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}\left(\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)+\beta^{-1}\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right),

where, for commodity, we have written
\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)=\text{E}(\varepsilon_{a},\gamma)+c_{3}^{2}\varepsilon_{b}^{\frac{1}{2}}+c_{3}\varepsilon_{b}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, so that, of course,
\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)
has limit
0
as
\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b}
and
\gamma
tend to
0
. Taking suprema in
f,g\in L^{2}(\mu)
, we infer that

\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq c\,\left(\beta^{-1}\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)+\beta^{-1}\widetilde{\text{E}}(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b},\gamma)+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}\right).

From this, recalling that
\beta
is independent of
\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b}
and
\gamma
, we see that if we take
\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b}
and
\gamma
small enough,

\|\widetilde{K}\|_{L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)}\leq 2c(1+c_{\varepsilon_{a}}),

which is finite, as we wanted to show.
∎

Using the fact that
\widetilde{K}\,\colon L^{2}(\mu)\to L^{2}(\mu)
is bounded, in the next lemma we finish the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The operator
K_{\Phi}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
, with a bound independent of
\varepsilon_{0}
, and thus the SIO
T
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
.

Proof.

First, by Lemma
2.12
, we have that

|T_{\varepsilon_{0}}f(x)-K_{\Phi,\varepsilon_{0}}f(x)|\leq c\,\sup_{r\geq\varepsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{r^{n}}\int_{B(x,r)}|f|\,d(\mu\lfloor G).

Arguing as in Lemma
5.9
, the right hand side above is bounded by
c\,c_{0}\,M_{\mu\lfloor G}f(x)
. Since the maximal operator is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
, we deduce that
T_{\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
if and only if
K_{\Phi,\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
.

Moreover, for
x\in G
,

\displaystyle|K_{\Phi,\varepsilon_{0}}f(x)-K_{\Phi}f(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}}|\widetilde{k}_{\Phi}(x,y)|\,|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}^{n}}\int_{|x-y|\leq\varepsilon_{0}}|f(y)|\,d\mu(y)\leq c\,c_{0}\,M_{\mu}f(x),

again, arguing as in Lemma
5.9
and using that for
x\in G
,
\Phi(x)=\varepsilon_{0}
(recall Lemma
5.7
), combined with the fact that
|\widetilde{k}_{\Phi}(x,y)|\leq\frac{1}{\Phi(x)^{n}}
. By the boundedness of the maximal operator, we see that
K_{\Phi,\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
if and only if
K_{\Phi}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
.

Furthermore, combining inequality (
132
) with the previous lemma, we see that
K_{\Phi}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu)
independently of
\varepsilon_{0}
, and so, by our previous arguments,
T_{\varepsilon_{0}}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
, independently of
\varepsilon_{0}
. Equivalently,
T\,\colon L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)\to L^{2}(\mu\lfloor G)
is bounded, which is what we wanted to prove.
∎
